\title{Mortgage Lock-In and the Composition of the Federal Reserve's Agency-MBS Runoff}
\author{Eugene Ong}
\affil{Carnegie Mellon University}
\date{}
\begin{document}
\maketitle
\begin{abstract}
\noindent U.S. mortgages are fixed-rate, repaid at face value, and not portable; when rates rise, that combination locks households into old, cheap loans. I measure what it cost the Federal Reserve: repayments on its mortgage bonds ran \$764.7 billion below the monthly ceilings set for June 2022 through November 2025. Under the production floor form, most of that gap was never about behavior: switch the lock-in response off and the model still accounts for 85.7\% of it (35.6\% under the additive form), because scheduled amortization and baseline turnover (itself read from realized, partly behavioral turnover) fall short of a \$35 billion monthly ceiling however the rate-responsive margin behaves. A loan-by-loan model run on Freddie Mac data accounts for 91.3\%. \par What lock-in itself adds is a range rather than a number. The design bounds it between $+2.9$ and $+8.7$ points under its production floor form (the floor read's sampling error at my central elasticity, the widest uncertainty source with a coverage property though not the widest read of that source; a wild-cluster interval on 31 clusters; the percentile read under-covers; the unestimated baseline seasoning ramp spans $+0.9$ to $+15.6$ at the same calibration), with a form-conditional hull of $+3.5$ to $+13.1$ points, and it identifies a marginal, not a level and not monthly timing. Inside that range, $+5.6$ points, or \$42.6 billion, is the value at the calibration I headline, and the corrections I can measure to the baseline turnover floor or to the accounting basis no longer all run one way: calendar-standardizing the floor read moves it to $+4.7$, while matching that read's housing-activity level to the window's moves it to $+10.7$. That is the range's coordinate at one anchor---the middle of the three depth cuts fixed before the reads---and not its centre; I attach no posture to where it sits. The cost to households who could not move is real. The institutional cash-flow cost is small, on an accounting that counts repaid principal at face value, and under the production floor form; the additive form roughly doubles the margin.
\end{abstract}

\vspace{0.5\baselineskip}
{\footnotesize\noindent\textbf{Keywords:} mortgage prepayment; lock-in effect; quantitative tightening; mortgage-backed securities; agent-based models; hazard models.\\
\textbf{JEL codes:} E52, E58, G21, R31.\par}
\newpage
\section{Introduction}\label{sec:intro}

For most of its history, the Federal Reserve operated under a ``Treasuries-only'' framework, using sovereign debt with fixed, predictable maturities. During the 2008 Global Financial Crisis, and again during the 2020 COVID-19 pandemic, the central bank departed from this framework, launching aggressive Quantitative Easing (QE) programs that purchased trillions of dollars in Agency MBS to stimulate the consumer economy directly.

This intervention suppressed 30-year mortgage rates to historic lows of roughly 2.6\%, triggering a large refinance boom. As inflation surged in 2022, the Federal Reserve attempted to reverse this stimulus through Quantitative Tightening (QT), establishing a passive roll-off target intended to drain up to \$35 billion per month from its SOMA portfolio as homeowners relocated naturally, a cap phased in at \$17.5 billion per month and stepped up to \$35 billion three months later.

That the lock-in effect slowed the runoff of the Federal Reserve's mortgage portfolio is not a new claim. The Federal Reserve's own staff and officials have said as much (\citealp{na2024}; \citealp{perli2024}; \citealp{hammack2025}). This paper's contribution is not that mechanism but its decomposition. That decomposition is an accounting exercise within a fully disclosed model rather than a causal identification: switching the lock-in elasticity off partitions the realized shortfall between an elastic channel and a no-elasticity baseline (scheduled amortization plus baseline turnover held at its observed level; the floor that sets that level is itself read off realized, partly behavioral turnover, so the baseline is not a non-behavioral object), and the elasticity's causal content is inherited from the external estimates that calibrate it, not established here (Section~\ref{sec:identification}). I ask how much of the runoff shortfall the lock-in elasticity accounts for, as opposed to the scheduled amortization and baseline turnover that would have undershot a \$35 billion monthly cap regardless of any rate response above that baseline. Most of the shortfall sits in the no-elasticity baseline. A loan-level model with the lock-in elasticity switched off still recovers 85.7\% of the \$764.7 billion shortfall-against-cap benchmark constructed in Section~\ref{sec:method-benchmark} at the calibration this paper headlines, and 88.7\% at its in-sample calibration point, measured on the paper's benchmark-consistent accounting basis (Section~\ref{sec:hazard-interp}). The Federal Reserve's own ex-ante projection implies the same composition, though not independently: the projection may already have embedded part of the lock-in channel (Section~\ref{sec:method-benchmark}). The New York Fed's May 2022 staff baseline already anticipated the large majority of the realized cap-shortfall: 75.6--88.5\%, depending on a disclosed intra-2022 allocation choice (Section~\ref{sec:method-benchmark}), a range wide enough that its overlap with the mechanical null's recovery carries no precision.

The elasticity's identified footprint is therefore a bounded margin, not a pinned magnitude and not a sign. The floor that dominates the level is calibrated in-window on the same locked-in cohorts the margin decomposes, so I re-measure it outside that window, on pre-episode 2017--2019 discount cohorts, and re-run both legs at the result: the margin runs $+4.3$ to $+6.8$ points across the off-window floor's defensible range, and $+2.9$ to $+8.7$ points once that floor's own sampling error is propagated with a wild-cluster small-sample correction, which is the binding layer (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}). The value at the mid-grid anchor inside that interval is $+5.6$ points ($+\$42.6$ billion), and it is the range and not that value that the design delivers: the corrections I can measure to the floor or to the accounting basis no longer run one way either---calendar-standardizing the floor read moves the value to $+4.7$ points, matching that read's housing-activity level to the window's moves it to $+10.7$---and the specification variants that are not floor corrections run both ways as well, so the range and not any interior member carries the identified content (Section~\ref{sec:identification}). The in-window production calibration returns $+9.2$ points (within-stratum sampling interval $[+9.17, +9.23]$ points from the 200-replicate paired loan-level bootstrap, a scheme the cluster construction supersedes: resampling the 130 strata puts the paired marginal at $[+8.27, +10.19]$ points, a 30.8$\times$ wider read (run \texttt{bootstrap\_pathb\_cluster} at this floor); the operative uncertainty remains calibration, Table~\ref{tab:uncertainty}), reported here as the in-sample calibration point rather than the headline. Neither figure is the entire shortfall (Section~\ref{sec:identification}).

A pre-committed sweep of the two calibration inputs that dominate the level, the baseline turnover floor and the elasticity band, bounds the margin between $+2.1$ and $+13.2$ points, positive at every point of the calibration box and at every off-window anchor tested, though that positivity checks the specification rather than identifying the mechanism (Section~\ref{sec:identification}). A functional-form test that replaces the floor's hard maximum with a competing-risks combination leaves the margin inside that box and nearly floor-invariant (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}). (``Pre-committed,'' here and throughout, means the grid, parity gates, and interpretive thresholds were committed to the repository before any run executed; I reserve ``pre-registered'' for third-party registries, which none of this is; Appendix~\ref{app:verdicts} tabulates every such rule together with how its outcome was adjudicated after the run.)

I reach this decomposition through two structurally distinct estimators, a 10,000-household agent-based model and a loan-level hazard framework, that disagree sharply about how much household choice explains. The decomposition the hazard framework supports is the paper's central empirical result; the disagreement between the two estimators I read as a supporting diagnostic rather than a clean paradigm contrast, since a real-covariate variant of the ABM recovers far more (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign}).

This result sits inside a two-sided exchange: the pool homogeneity that generates To-Be-Announced (TBA) market liquidity is purchased at the price of household mobility, because the standardized contracts that TBA delivery presupposes carry the ``par-payoff'' obligation, under which a homeowner must extinguish an existing mortgage at full face value to relocate. The obligation itself is a property of the U.S. fixed-rate note enforced by the due-on-sale clause (made broadly enforceable by the Garn--St.~Germain Act of 1982) rather than of TBA eligibility, and it binds on portfolio and non-agency loans alike; the FHA/VA loans backing Ginnie Mae pools (20.4\% of the SOMA book) are assumable by statute, a carve-out from the book-wide mobility cost that Section~\ref{sec:pathb} scores by observed speeds. Realized take-up of that statutory right is not measured anywhere in this design, so the 20.4\% share bounds the carve-out from above rather than sizing it. The observed speeds bound it from above a second way, and in the same direction: an assumed loan does not prepay, so materially high take-up would show up as \emph{slower} Ginnie voluntary speeds, and over the window Ginnie's voluntary component instead runs $1.35$ points per year \emph{above} the conventional one. Both bounds are ceilings, not sizes; what neither prices is the frictions---second-lien gaps, equity buy-outs, qualification---that stand between the statutory right and its exercise. Extension-protected institutional cash flow is often named as a third design objective; Section~\ref{sec:conclusion} states what my evidence says about it and under what conditions. The exchange this paper measures is the two-sided one. The decomposition above disciplines this frame rather than dissolving it: household mobility is constrained, as the household-level lock-in literature documents, while the cash-flow shortfall is governed mostly by loan-level and institutional mechanics, so the trade-off's binding cost is denominated in mobility rather than in institutional cash flow (Section~\ref{sec:conclusion}). Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}'s Danish counterfactual initially implied a \$925.5 billion institutional gap. Recalibrating against separately estimated Danish elasticities (Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}) reduces this by an order of magnitude, to $+\$61.2$ billion (8\% of the benchmark), the zero-refinance edge of a band swept to $+\$256.8$ billion at 3\% refinance-in-place (Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}), under the shared-accounting hybrid pipeline when the transplant changes only the payoff rule (the Danish moving channel's estimated flatness anchored at the U.S. zero-gap intercept, baseline turnover floor respected). What that construction can and cannot demonstrate is set by the anchor: a Danish leg pinned at the zero-gap U.S. hazard must prepay faster than a U.S. book that carries a negative rate gap almost everywhere, so a positive gap---and its survival of the refinance-in-place sweep---is forced rather than found, and the informative content is the magnitude. The bracketing anchor that also imports Denmark's baseline mobility flips the sign, to $-\$99.9$ billion---but that leg runs its Danish households at 3.39\% annual prepayment, below the 4\% turnover floor both frameworks impose on the U.S. side, which is why I bracket with it rather than headline it. I treat the corrected magnitude as informative about where the trade-off actually binds, and the sign as anchor-determined---and, on the cash-flow side, incidence-determined as well: pricing the market-value buyback credit under a cash-haircut reading reverses the gap to $-\$89.4$ to $-\$117.7$ billion at the committed proxy discounts, so the institutional relief is established under face accounting only, which is the benchmark's own denomination (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}). The mobility-versus-cash-flow ranking above rests on external evidence, not on a within-paper comparison: I measure the institutional leg and import the mobility leg at an elasticity I never re-measure, so the two are nowhere commensurable. The one household-side magnitude I carry is \citepos{batzer2024} roughly \$2.4 trillion of foregone capital gains, computed on universal relocation and therefore an upper bound, and a stock of foregone gains is not a cash flow over forty-two months. The ranking's direction is what that supports; its ratio is not.

Unlike standard U.S. Treasuries, U.S. mortgages carry asymmetric, state-dependent payoff profiles. As the Federal Reserve's policy tightening pushed the 30-year fixed mortgage rate past 7.0\% (a rise decomposable into the 10-year Treasury yield's climb and a widening of the primary-mortgage-to-10-year spread from roughly 170 to over 300 bp at its 2022--2023 peak, FRED series MORTGAGE30US less DGS10; the decomposition of that spread into MBS supply, prepayment-risk repricing, and convexity-hedging components follows \citet{boyarchenko2019}, whose framework predates and does not document the 2022--2023 magnitudes), the par-payoff rule imposed a substantial financial penalty on relocation, producing a ``lock-in effect'' that suppressed prepayment activity across the U.S. housing market. Because mortgages did not prepay, principal cash flows from the Federal Reserve's MBS portfolio ran far below the redemption caps, extending the portfolio's effective maturity. Cumulative runoff fell \$764.7 billion short of the phased caps over the tightening cycle (methodology and interpretation in Section~\ref{sec:method-benchmark}). How much of that shortfall the lock-in channel itself accounts for is the question Sections~\ref{sec:abm} and~\ref{sec:hazard} decompose.

Standard macroeconomic models often underestimate this extension risk because they assume frictionless, rational household behavior. In practice, the lock-in effect was compounded by historic housing inventory shortages and depressed consumer sentiment.

This paper quantifies the mechanics of this shortfall using two structurally distinct estimators. The first is a 10,000-household Agent-Based Model parameterized with dynamic macroeconomic frictions, used to estimate stranded cash flows and to decompose that estimate against the empirical benchmark. The second, introduced after the ABM's residual proved persistent and unresponsive to additional behavioral realism, is a reduced-form hazard framework estimated on loan-level survival data that abandons the household utility function altogether. As Sections~\ref{sec:abm} and~\ref{sec:hazard} show, synthetic-population household choice explains only a small share of the shortfall (13.6\% on the fifty-seed mean, 10--17\% across seed noise, 11.9\% on the frozen production draw), while the loan-level hazard framework recovers the large majority of it at the aggregate level: 91.3\% in its literature-calibrated microsimulation form on the benchmark-consistent shared accounting basis at the off-window floor this paper headlines, 88.2--93.7\% across that floor's defensible range, and 97.9\% at the in-sample calibration point (92.2\%--99.2\% across covariate calibrations at that in-sample point), and 100.0\% (approximately 94.3\%--101.3\% across those calibrations; 98.1\% when sample and book are first put on one coupon convention) once the sample's coupon mix is reweighted to the SOMA book under the ex-ante priors. Its estimated cohort-hazard form (Path A) lands at 112.4\% but is excluded from headline ranges and does not corroborate the result: its forward simulation carries no useful propagated precision, and no inferential object has been recomputed under its production specification (Section~\ref{sec:patha}). Two qualifiers travel with the two-estimator contrast wherever I state it: the null that switches off the lock-in elasticity (the $\beta_1 = 0$ null) already recovers 85.7\% of the benchmark at the off-window floor and 88.7\% at the in-window production calibration, so the hazard framework's margin over a mechanical model is $+5.6$ points at the off-window floor ($+9.2$ at the in-window production calibration), not the level (Section~\ref{sec:identification}). A cross-design variant of the ABM recalibrated on real loan covariates recovers 59.3\% on the frozen seed, 60.2\% across fifty seeds with a 95\% band of 55.1--66.0\%, and 76.3\% at the book's composition (effective sample 1{,}332 of 10{,}000 loans; a re-draw returns 70.6\%; Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign}), so the contrast is not clean evidence about modeling paradigm alone. The hazard framework is the instrument; the decomposition it yields is the paper's central empirical result. (Section~\ref{sec:robustness} shows, on the production specification, that population size does not drive this gap.) A counterfactual simulation based on the Danish market-value repurchase regime further shows that the institutional cash-flow component of systemic duration risk is a consequence of U.S. institutional design: under the rule-only transplant it matches the lock-in marginal in origin, and in size at the in-sample calibration point on which both were run.

This paper's affirmative contributions are three. First, a decomposition of the QT mortgage-runoff shortfall into mechanical and elastic components, with the elastic part delivered as a bounded margin rather than a point (Sections~\ref{sec:abm}, \ref{sec:hazard} and~\ref{sec:identification}). Second, an expectations-based complement that measures the shortfall against the Federal Reserve's own ex-ante projection rather than the never-binding caps (Section~\ref{sec:method-benchmark}). Third, the cap-design arithmetic. That the caps would sit above achievable runoff was projected by New York Fed staff from the month the \$35 billion ceiling took effect \citep{nyfed2022}; the observation is theirs, and what I add is its quantification and its ex-ante construction: the ceiling was set roughly 1.7 to 1.9 times as high as the Federal Reserve's own contemporaneous projection of achievable runoff, and both components of the mechanical path a binding cap would have to sit below --- scheduled amortization from the book's coupon, age, and term composition, and baseline turnover at any assumed floor --- are computable in advance of the cycle, as a band rather than as a point (Sections~\ref{sec:identification} and~\ref{sec:discussion-capdesign}). Two further elements of the design are not contributions, and I label them here as what they are. The paired cross-design test --- the ABM's decision rule on real loan covariates, and the same survival structure on a fully synthetic population, under interpretive thresholds fixed ex ante --- is a validation device, and it ran against this paper's own ABM reading rather than for it: on all fifty seeds the real-covariate leg crossed the threshold, fixed before that run, at which that reading is undercut (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign}). The order-of-magnitude reduction of the Danish market-value counterfactual, with the incidence bracket that prices it, is a robustness result on a counterfactual rather than a measurement of the U.S. book: the \$925.5 billion figure it corrects is this paper's own earlier extrapolation, and the corrected gap's sign is anchor- and incidence-determined (Sections~\ref{sec:abm-danish} and~\ref{sec:pathb}).

\paragraph{Definitions used throughout.} Four accounting bases attach to every recovery percentage (Section~\ref{sec:method-benchmark}): the \emph{standalone-scorer} basis (no adjustment), the \emph{benchmark-consistent shared} basis (nets a common \$69.6 billion curtailment flow identically from every U.S. leg; the main-text convention), the \emph{full-book} basis (reweights to the SOMA book's coupon mix), and the \emph{composed} basis (both). Two floor calibrations attach to every marginal and level: the \emph{in-sample calibration point} (the 4\% baseline turnover floor measured in-window on the locked-in 2023--24 cohorts; returns $+9.2$ points) and the \emph{off-window calibration} (the 4.70--5.33\% floor measured on pre-episode 2018 discount cohorts; returns $+5.6$ points and is the headline). The floor itself is a measured object: realized-turnover reads put it at 3.8--3.9\% in-window (flat across seasoning cuts), 4.70--5.33\% on the off-window 2018 leg (all of it on cohort-months aged twelve to twenty-four, so that leg's mature-seasoning test is not computable rather than passed), 5.51\% under an imputation-heavy age standardization (84\% imputed weight, an indicative bound derived from the same 2018 leg), and 5.52\% on an out-of-agency Fannie Mae read of the pooled off-window period; the last two lines sit above the clean band, as does every mature-versus-young turnover contrast the data make measurable, which is why the band's lower edge is the soft one (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}). The ``baseline turnover floor'' (sometimes called an involuntary-turnover floor) names that measured object without decomposing it: what these reads measure is total deep-discount-cohort turnover, which mixes discretionary life-cycle moves and cash-out refinancings with strictly involuntary events, and the strictly-involuntary share is plausibly well under half (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}), a share the mixture curve of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor} prices directly. ``Trapped liquidity'' and ``shortfall'' denote the net shortfall of realized roll-off against the phased redemption caps (\$764.7 billion over June 2022--November 2025), and the \emph{lock-in marginal} is the difference between the central simulation and its $\beta_1 = 0$ null, the paper's identified object, whose operative uncertainty is the calibration-and-form envelope of Table~\ref{tab:uncertainty} rather than any sampling interval. That envelope is a grid, not a confidence region, and the distinction is load-bearing: the elasticity band it sweeps is \citeauthor{liebersohn2024}'s \emph{specification} range with my adopted midpoint at its centre, not a published point estimate with a published standard error, and no sampling error of theirs is propagated anywhere in this paper. So the envelope has no coverage property. The $\beta_1 = 0$ leg those readings are differenced against is the \emph{no-elasticity baseline}: it carries scheduled amortization from the book's own coupon, age, and term composition together with baseline turnover held at its observed level, and the marginal is the imported elasticity's increment above it. What the partition separates is that baseline from that increment, not mechanics from behavior---the floor that sets the turnover level is measured on realized turnover which is itself partly behavioral, so the baseline is not a non-behavioral object and I do not call it one. Where a genuine sampling interval exists---the floor reads' cluster bootstrap, and the loan- and stratum-level bootstraps of Section~\ref{sec:pathb}---I quote it as such and rank it against the envelope explicitly rather than merging the two. One global property of the design should be stated here as well as in the limitations, because every number below inherits it: \emph{no outcome-holdout months exist anywhere in this paper}, without exception. Its unavoidability is scoped to the cash-flow margin. The identified object has nothing to hold out: the marginal is a difference between two simulations, so no realized outcome corresponds to it. A holdout on the level's fit is conceivable but not attempted, since it would mean splitting the benchmark itself---a single cumulative path against one cap schedule. The household margin does admit outcome moments this design does not use, since it imports the mobility elasticity rather than estimating it (Section~\ref{sec:limitations}). The turnover floor that sets Path~B's level is calibrated on in-window turnover, Path~A trains on nineteen of the forty-two QT months, and Path~B's level is evaluated on the window that sets it. Each of these is disclosed again where it arises, and the nineteen-month floor variant reported later is an input-stability check rather than an outcome holdout: what it holds out is calibration months, not the benchmark. Every recovery percentage in this paper is accordingly an in-sample or calibrated quantity in the time dimension, and should be read as a description of fit rather than as a prediction (Section~\ref{sec:limitations}).

\paragraph{How to read this paper.} The short path for a referee: Sections~\ref{sec:lit}--\ref{sec:method-benchmark} for the claim and the design; Section~\ref{sec:pathb} for the operative estimator; Section~\ref{sec:identification} for what is and is not identified; Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor} for the calibration sensitivity the headline rests on; Section~\ref{sec:conclusion} for the bottom line. Everything after the online-appendix banner (Appendix~\ref{app:ledger} through Appendix~\ref{app:verdicts}) is apparatus: the run ledger, the specification blocks, the sweep mechanics, and the verdict-adjudication audit. The seven qualifications that scope the headline live in Section~\ref{sec:identification}; floor and form sensitivity in Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}; every non-mechanical adjudication in Appendix~\ref{app:verdicts}.

\section{Literature Review}\label{sec:lit}

The theoretical foundation of mortgage prepayment is the assumption of ``ruthless exercise,'' wherein borrowers refinance instantaneously as market conditions dictate \citep{dunn1981}. Subsequent literature recognized behavioral frictions: \citet{schwartz1989} estimate an empirical prepayment function from aggregate GNMA experience with maximum-likelihood hazard techniques and embed it in a valuation framework (the econometric ancestor of the loan-level survival approach I adopt in Section~\ref{sec:hazard}), while \citet{richard1989} formalize the industry practice of modeling prepayment as an empirical function of the refinancing incentive, seasoning, seasonality, and a pool's own prepayment history. \citet{stanton1995} introduced heterogeneous transaction costs, smoothing the instantaneous refinancing cliff into a predictable S-curve, and \citet{agarwal2013} give the rational benchmark its sharpest form, a closed-form optimal refinancing threshold against which observed behavior can be scored. \citet{chernov2018} recover the prepayment function implied by MBS market prices and find it driven by a macroeconomic turnover component alongside the rate response---the same turnover-versus-rate-response split this paper's mechanical null operationalizes on the cash-flow side. \citet{quigley1987,quigley2002} established the lock-in effect as a persistent friction limiting mobility during rising-rate environments. \citet{ferreira2010} provided early large-sample evidence for it, finding that rising mortgage rates and negative home equity each depress owner mobility---the latter an equity-lock-in channel distinct from, and complementary to, the rate channel this paper models (the equity dimension is left unmodeled here, a boundary disclosed in Section~\ref{sec:pathb}).

The 2022--2025 rate shock generated substantial empirical literature documenting the breakdown of standard prepayment assumptions. \citet{fonseca2024} documented the steep decline of U.S. mobility and refinancing activity, while \citet{batzer2024} estimated that U.S. households would have faced roughly \$2.4 trillion in foregone capital gains had they chosen to relocate. \citet{liebersohn2024} quantify the same lock-in mechanism in hazard form, estimating that each percentage point of prevailing rate above a borrower's locked-in coupon reduces quarterly mobility probability by 5.5\% to 7.7\% depending on specification: the elasticity band my Path B microsimulation adopts as its calibration; \citet{graybill2026}, in a Federal Reserve Bank of Philadelphia working paper, independently estimate the same margin from transaction records in a housing-market-equilibrium setting, with deep rate gaps cutting the sale hazard by roughly 7--11\% per percentage point, a corroboration of that band from data that never touch credit records, and one that sits at or above it rather than at its centre: only the 7\% low end of their range falls inside the 5.5\% to 7.7\% band, and its midpoint sits above the high edge (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}).

A related strand of literature decomposes how much of the observed mobility decline a rational, rate-gap-driven framework can explain, leaving the remainder as an unexplained residual. \citet{aladangady2024} construct a counterfactual rate-gap distribution holding the 2021 rate environment fixed and find that rate-gap-driven lock-in explains only 44\% of the actual decline in mortgage borrower mobility between 2021 and 2022, after correcting for selection bias from forgone refinancing opportunities. Section~\ref{sec:identification} returns to that 44\% share and maps it into this design's own units as a calibration target for the scaled-null variant (run \texttt{scaled\_null\_housing\_activity}). This decomposition approach is methodologically analogous to the one I take in Sections~\ref{sec:abm} and~\ref{sec:hazard}, though I apply it to dynamically modeled CPR and cash-flow shortfalls rather than to mobility rates directly, and use two structurally distinct estimators rather than one. \citet{gerardi2024} similarly compare a structural search-and-matching model of lock-in against empirical lifecycle migration patterns, finding that their model accounts for most, but not all, of the observed disruption to housing market liquidity.

A separate behavioral finance literature provides theoretical grounding for what such residuals likely represent. \citet{keys2016} document that more than 20\% of U.S. borrowers fail to refinance even when doing so would be unambiguously in their financial interest, attributing this to procrastination and variation in financial sophistication. \citet{defusco2020} document a structural counterpart to these behavioral frictions, showing that employment-documentation requirements and out-of-pocket closing costs bind most severely in recessions and inhibit refinancing pass-through. \citet{andersen2020} reach a similar conclusion using Danish administrative data, finding that inattention and inertia, rather than transaction costs, account for a substantial share of household failure to act on refinancing opportunities. \citet{andersen2022} further show, using Danish housing data, that households exhibit reference dependence and loss aversion relative to their original purchase price, weighting losses roughly 2.5 times more heavily than equivalent gains. \citet{johnson2019} examine sluggish refinancing using administrative, survey, and field-experimental data, finding that among the behavioral factors they test, suspicion of lenders' motives (rather than standard rational frictions such as time preference or risk aversion) is most consistently related to households' failure to accept profitable refinancing offers, further supporting a behavioral interpretation of the residual. \citet{bissiri2014} formalize this distinction directly, defining ``behavioral risk'' as the share of prepayment decisions not driven by a fully rational financial criterion, an operationalization more recently applied by \citet{perotti2024} to model behavioral uncertainty as a stochastic overlay on an otherwise rational prepayment framework and to derive robust hedging strategies that bound, rather than eliminate, the exposure such uncertainty creates.

A further strand treats prepayment as a loan-level survival process shaped by pool composition and servicer behavior rather than by borrower optimization. Prepayment ``burnout'' (the tendency for a pool's aggregate refinancing sensitivity to decline over time as its most rate-sensitive borrowers prepay earlier and self-select out of the remaining pool) is a standard feature of industry MBS valuation models (one of the four factors of \citepos{richard1989} canonical prepayment function, alongside the Public Securities Association seasoning convention), but is difficult to reconcile with a fixed, representative-agent population. Recent theory formalizes why: in a heterogeneous pool the observed pool-level hazard is the survival-weighted mean of the individual hazards, and its drift carries a negative selection term proportional to the cross-sectional dispersion of those hazards \citep[arXiv preprint]{lesniewski2026}, so pool speeds decline with seasoning even when no individual hazard changes. My hazard framework (Section~\ref{sec:hazard}) is designed to accommodate this loan-level survival structure.

Fixed-income literature separately highlights the systemic risks generated by the lock-in effect. \citet{jiang2023} documented that duration extension in bank asset portfolios, prominently including low-coupon MBS, generated roughly \$2 trillion in unrecognized mark-to-market losses across the U.S. banking sector, contributing to self-fulfilling solvency runs including the collapse of Silicon Valley Bank. \citet{smith2023} demonstrated that QT relies heavily on predictable MBS runoff schedules, which are structurally broken by these duration extensions.

Closest to this paper, the Federal Reserve's own staff and officials have already described the mechanism and measured its first-order footprint. \citet{na2024} report that ``a vast majority of the agency MBS held in the SOMA are far out-of-the-money to refinance and may be subject to the `lock-in' effect,'' and that because ``monthly principal payments have been below the \$35 billion-per-month redemption cap \ldots the size of the SOMA agency MBS portfolio has decreased at a much slower pace than in the case of a binding cap''; they put June 2022 to June 2024 runoff at roughly \$450 billion against the \$820 billion a binding cap would have retired, an implied shortfall near \$370 billion. The same note reports the composition of those below-cap payments: scheduled principal accounts for roughly half, with prepayments mostly due to turnover making up the remainder, an external, staff-measured anchor for the scheduled-plus-turnover decomposition my $\beta_1 = 0$ null quantifies. That anchor and my null have different denominators: Na et al.'s composition describes realized principal \emph{payments} (roughly half scheduled, half turnover-driven prepayment, and my null is built from the same two ingredients: scheduled amortization plus a turnover floor calibrated from realized, partly behavioral turnover), while my 88.7\% (in-sample; 85.7\% at the off-window floor) describes the null's share of the shortfall \emph{against the caps}; a payments mix of scheduled-plus-turnover running below \$35 billion in essentially every month is the state in which the shortfall accrues mechanically, so their composition and my null recovery are the same fact read against two denominators, on the composition the null is built to reproduce. \citet{perli2024}, then Manager of the System Open Market Account, stated that the cap ``has not been binding \ldots resulting in very slow prepayments,'' and \citet{hammack2025} observed that some of the low-coupon holdings ``could be very long lived.'' New York Fed staff had projected the below-cap regime from its first month: a September 2022 staff post put monthly agency MBS principal payments at \$20 to \$30 billion over the following months, below the \$35 billion cap that took effect that month \citep{nyfed2022}. I treat the qualitative mechanism as established by these sources rather than as my own finding. Four features separate my exercise from theirs. First, this characterization attributes the below-cap runoff to lock-in without isolating the elasticity's contribution from the scheduled amortization and baseline turnover that would have undershot the cap under any rate-driven prepayment response above that baseline; Sections~\ref{sec:abm} and~\ref{sec:hazard} supply that decomposition and find the identified lock-in margin is bounded between $+2.9$ and $+8.7$ points of the benchmark at a turnover floor measured off the locked-in population (a wild-cluster interval on the floor read's 31 clusters; the narrower percentile read under-covers), $+5.6$ percentage points at the mid-grid anchor inside that interval ($+9.2$ at the in-window production calibration), and qualified in Section~\ref{sec:identification}. Second, I extend the same shortfall-against-cap construction from the \$450 billion-through-June-2024 quantity to the full June 2022 to November 2025 window, where it reaches \$764.7 billion (Section~\ref{sec:method-benchmark}). Third, I measure the shortfall against the Federal Reserve's own ex-ante projection as well as against the caps, separating the component the Federal Reserve itself anticipated from the genuine surprise (Section~\ref{sec:method-benchmark}). Fourth, the decomposition turns the staff observation into a statement about cap design: scheduled amortization and a turnover floor read from realized, partly behavioral turnover undershoot a \$35 billion monthly ceiling in essentially every month, so a cap at that level could not have bound under any rate response above that floor (Section~\ref{sec:identification}). A more recent \citet{eyal2026} FEDS Note also decomposes the Federal Reserve's balance-sheet reduction, but into macroeconomic drivers (inflation, real GDP growth, and active SOMA reductions relative to nominal GDP) rather than into the loan-level sources of the MBS runoff shortfall itself, so it does not speak to the lock-in-versus-mechanical question I take up here.

The Danish mortgage securitization system offers a tested structural alternative to negative convexity. \citet{berg2018} outlined the Danish ``Balance Principle,'' which requires strict one-to-one match funding. \citet[\S3.2.3]{berger2026}, in a January 2026 SSRN working draft, estimate that under these rules household mobility is close to flat in the coupon gap, because borrowers hold the right to repurchase their outstanding mortgages at market value, neutralizing extension risk for the bondholder. That flatness is a slope result on Danish data, and Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish} is careful about what it licenses: this paper imports the slope and anchors the level at the U.S. hazard, since Berger et al.\ estimate no moving level for U.S. households under a Danish payoff rule. What this strand supplies is the rule and the slope; what it lacks for the question here is a priced U.S. counterfactual. Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish} scores the rule-only transplant on the SOMA book directly, at an order of magnitude below a mechanism-extrapolated read, and Section~\ref{sec:pathb} prices the buyback credit's incidence, which decides whether the institutional gap survives the accounting.

A final strand situates the lock-in effect within the broader theory of monetary policy transmission. \citet{berger2021} demonstrate that the Federal Reserve's ability to stimulate the economy through rate cuts is path-dependent, because prepayment behavior depends on the outstanding distribution of in-the-money refinancing incentives. This paper extends that logic to the tightening direction: the same path dependence that weakens easing after a low-rate period also weakens tightening after a low-rate period, since the resulting stock of deeply in-the-money, rate-locked mortgages cannot prepay regardless of how aggressively the central bank subsequently raises rates. \citet{eichenbaum2022} formalize this state dependence for the easing direction (the potency of monetary policy depends on the distribution of outstanding rate gaps). \citet{beraja2019} provide complementary regional evidence that the strength of the refinancing channel varies systematically with the local distribution of in-the-money mortgages, and \citet{dimaggio2020} supply direct evidence for the easing-direction channel this paper mirrors, showing that the Federal Reserve's QE1 agency-MBS purchases transmitted to the real economy specifically by inducing refinancing. This QT setting also connects the paper to the broader literature on how central-bank balance-sheet operations transmit through the supply of long-duration assets: \citet{krishnamurthy2011} document that quantitative easing operates through several distinct channels, including one specific to agency MBS that runs through prepayment, and \citet{vayanos2021} formalize, under preferred-habitat demand, why shifts in the outstanding maturity and supply of held bonds move the term structure at all. Lock-in is thus an input to the balance-sheet-supply channel these papers describe, not a phenomenon separate from it. \citet{du2024} assemble the cross-country QT record---seven central banks, active and passive runoff---and find announcement effects concentrated in yields; the MBS runoff shortfall this paper decomposes is the passive-runoff margin of that record, measured at the portfolio cash-flow level. \citet{vickery2013} document that the liquidity premium of the TBA market derives specifically from the homogeneity of deliverable pools, finding that TBA-eligible securities trade at significantly lower cost than otherwise similar specified pools; this homogeneity requirement is the institutional constraint that, in Section~\ref{sec:conclusion}, I argue makes the Danish market-value repurchase structure difficult to reconcile with the current U.S. secondary market absent a broader redesign of agency securitization rules.

My two estimators sit at the transparent end of two active methodological literatures. On the survival side, a growing body of work fits prepayment and default hazards with nonparametric and machine-learning methods, including random survival forests \citep{ishwaran2008}, competing-risks Bayesian additive regression trees \citep{sparapani2020}, and deep neural-network mortgage hazard models \citep{sadhwani2021}. The last of these is the benchmark for what flexibility buys at scale: fitted to origination and monthly performance records for over 120 million U.S. mortgages, the deep network of \citet{sadhwani2021} learns highly nonlinear, interaction-rich prepayment and default responses, including the burnout-type history dependence that Path~A carries as an explicit stratum-level regressor and the ABM probes by pre-committed falsification (Sections~\ref{sec:patha} and~\ref{sec:abm-results}), without hand-specified functional form. I instead estimate a fixed-effects Poisson hazard and a literature-calibrated microsimulation whose functional forms and calibration inputs are written out in full, because the object I need is not maximal predictive fit but an auditable decomposition: the null of Section~\ref{sec:identification} that switches the lock-in elasticity off is interpretable only if every term it holds fixed is visible, which a flexible black-box hazard would obscure. Those machine-learning survival methods are a natural robustness avenue for the loan-level fit, and I probe its tabular end directly: Section~\ref{sec:patha} reports a gradient-boosted Poisson comparator and an isotonic-recalibration comparator, each given Path A's information set on the identical pre-committed holdout, and each recalibrating levels without gaining cross-cell predictive power; the deep loan-level variants remain future work on data-availability grounds. A complementary strand attacks nonstationarity rather than nonlinearity: landmarking re-anchors a simple hazard among the survivors at successive prediction times \citep{vanhouwelingen2007,putter2022}, and recent credit-risk work pairs landmark targets with explicit probability recalibration to keep survival predictions stable under covariate and base-rate drift, demonstrated on the same Freddie Mac performance data my hazard paths use \citep{peng2026}. My design meets the same concern with fixed, visible machinery (the stratum fixed effects, calendar-month dummies, and dynamic friction index of Section~\ref{sec:patha}) and prices the residual time-variation it cannot remove through the temporal moving-block bootstrap reported there. On the agent-based side, my calibration is fixed by the pre-committed parameterization of Appendix~\ref{sec:method-abm} rather than by an automated estimator, but it sits alongside a maturing literature on data-driven agent-based-model calibration and estimation, including simulated-minimum-distance and Bayesian methods \citep{grazzini2017,platt2020} and the empirical-validation taxonomy of \citet{fagiolo2007}, within which the pre-committed stress-test design of Section~\ref{sec:abm} is one validation strategy among several. The paired design-crossing that Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign} builds on top of it --- the ABM's decision rule re-run on real loan covariates, and the same survival structure run on a fully synthetic population, with interpretive thresholds fixed before the cross-design runs --- is offered as a validation design in its own right. Its nearest relative in that literature is indirect inference, which estimates an auxiliary model on real and on simulated data and minimizes the distance between the two sets of parameters \citep{platt2020}; the pair here crosses designs not to estimate parameters but to attribute a result gap to modeling paradigm versus data source, and it is not, to my knowledge, a named member of the validation taxonomy of \citet{fagiolo2007} or of the estimation approaches surveyed by \citet{grazzini2017} and \citet{platt2020}.

\section{Empirical Methodology}\label{sec:method}

\subsection{Two Structurally Distinct Estimators}\label{sec:method-estimators}

The first estimator is a 10,000-household Agent-Based Model (ABM), specified in Appendix~\ref{sec:method-abm}, which draws a synthetic population calibrated to aggregate FRED medians and tests whether a utility-maximizing household decision rule, rational or behaviorally extended, can account for the shortfall; its results are reported in Section~\ref{sec:abm}. The second is a hazard framework, specified in Section~\ref{sec:hazard}, which abandons the household utility function entirely and models loan-level survival directly on real Freddie Mac loan-level data: a stratum-month discrete-time hazard model estimated by Poisson pseudo-maximum likelihood on the full 2017--2021 origination universe aggregated to stratum-months (Path A), and a loan-level microsimulation with competing prepayment and default risks on a stratified 75,000-loan sample drawn from that universe (Path B).

Both estimators are evaluated against the same \$764.7 billion empirical benchmark (Section~\ref{sec:method-benchmark}) and the same June 2022--November 2025 QT window, so their results are directly comparable even though the estimators draw on different data and different behavioral assumptions. The two estimators differ in modeling paradigm (household choice versus loan-level survival), and, on the hazard side, in the use of real rather than synthetic loan-level data; Section~\ref{sec:robustness} finds that the difference in population size between them (10,000 households versus Path B's 75,000 simulated loans, with Path A estimated on the full origination universe) does not drive the gap in their explanatory power, though the data-source difference remains an unresolved confound that qualifies the paradigm interpretation throughout. Figure~\ref{fig:architecture} maps the pipeline.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig7_architecture}
\caption{Pipeline architecture. Data sources (FRED macro series, NY Fed SOMA holdings, Freddie Mac loan-level performance) feed three estimators (the synthetic-population ABM and hazard Paths A and B), whose simulated roll-off paths are scored through one shared macro-accounting layer against the \$764.7 billion empirical benchmark of Section~\ref{sec:method-benchmark}. The two robustness cross-links are the cross-design test (real Freddie covariates into the ABM, Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign}) and the symmetric companion test (synthetic population into the hazard framework, Section~\ref{sec:robustness-synthesis}).}
\label{fig:architecture}
\end{figure}

The agent-based model's specification, and the structural extensions whose grounding Section~\ref{sec:abm}'s results reference, are given in full in Appendix~\ref{sec:method-abm} and Appendix~\ref{sec:method-frictions}.

\subsection{Empirical Validation Benchmark}\label{sec:method-benchmark}

I construct the empirical shortfall benchmark from the New York Fed's SOMA current face value series, netting realized roll-off against the actual phased redemption cap schedule (\$17.5 billion per month from June through August 2022, stepping up to \$35 billion per month from September 2022 onward) strictly within the active QT window of June 2022 through November 2025. Net accumulation, rather than one-sided accumulation of shortfall months only, is essential for comparability with the Danish counterfactual of Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}, where simulated roll-off routinely exceeds the cap. Figure~\ref{fig:benchmark} traces the construction: monthly roll-off against the phased cap, and the cumulative paths whose terminal gaps are the cap-relative benchmark and the expectations-based complement introduced below. The monthly series carries one artifact that no result in this paper reads as behavior: four months come in at exactly zero because the back-out's non-negativity clip pins any month whose reported roll-off falls below scheduled amortization and posts the displaced mass into the following month's reading, a month-boundary allocation artifact of the reporting series rather than a behavioral observation (run \texttt{h1\_zero\_months\_diagnosis}; Appendix~\ref{sec:robustness-seasonalfloor} traces the mechanism and shows that repairing the affected pairs flips this paper's frozen monthly-timing rule from pass to concede), which is one reason every claim made from this benchmark is a claim about levels.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.94\textwidth]{fig9_benchmark_construction}
\caption{The empirical benchmark. Left: realized monthly SOMA agency MBS roll-off against the phased redemption cap; the shaded area is the monthly shortfall, which accrues on a net basis (the two circled opening months are net settlement inflows). Right: the same construction cumulatively. The terminal gaps are the \$764.7 billion cap-relative benchmark and, against the New York Fed's May 2022 ex-ante projection, the \$87.8 billion expectations-based complement. Monthly series from the frozen production run's metrics; the projection's monthly path is derived from the published year-end levels under the pre-committed uniform-spread rule and asserted, in the generator, to reproduce the frozen expectations-run totals.}
\label{fig:benchmark}
\end{figure}

This methodology yields an empirical shortfall of approximately \$764.7 billion (unrounded: \$764.748 billion; every percentage-of-benchmark figure in this paper divides by this value), which I adopt as the benchmark for the ABM's and the hazard framework's simulated trapped liquidity throughout Sections~\ref{sec:abm} and~\ref{sec:hazard}. The SOMA face underlying this benchmark spans the Federal Reserve's full agency MBS book (including Ginnie Mae collateral, 20.4\% of face), and 66.2\% of that face lies in the 2017--2021 vintages covered by the Freddie Mac loan-level sample of Section~\ref{sec:hazard}; the hazard framework's loan-level foundation therefore directly samples neither Ginnie Mae collateral nor roughly one-third of the book by vintage (the Ginnie dimension is externally bounded and scored by a published-speed overlay, and the vintage dimension is bounded by observed out-of-sample-vintage speeds from the replication ingest, both in Section~\ref{sec:pathb}; what each bound leaves open is stated in Section~\ref{sec:limitations}). The two exclusions must be netted on the book's own joint cells rather than counted separately: 33.7\% of face lies in out-of-window vintages (the 2022 cohort 23.1\%, pre-2017 10.6\%), and the 20.4\% Ginnie face share cuts across vintage rather than nesting inside it, so the conventional-and-in-window intersection is 51.0\% of face on the book's own agency $\times$ vintage joint cells (bounded above by 51.8\% for cell truncation; 52.7\% under an independence approximation), and the Freddie 2017--2021 conventional universe on which Path B is estimated directly represents about \emph{half} of SOMA book face. Two-thirds is the vintage-only marginal and overstates coverage. Each exclusion is bounded by an external-speed overlay (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}): the Ginnie Mae share at \$20--\$47 billion by published observed speeds, the out-of-window vintages at \$11.7 billion by observed cohort speeds, each signed toward overstating trapped liquidity. The empirical CPR back-out nets cohort-weighted scheduled amortization; an income-scaled curtailment assumption averaging approximately 0.84\% annualized, consistent with GSE daily prepayment report estimates, enters on the simulated side (added to each simulated roll-off and netted as a common dollar item in the shared accounting layer of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-hybrid}), so the back-out's CPR series retains the curtailment component. Two implementations of this back-out exist in the pipeline (the ABM side nets cohort-weighted, term-aware scheduled amortization, while the hazard side uses a single-pool amortization approximation), and both amortize at the pass-through WAC, while scheduled principal physically follows the borrowers' note rate, which is that WAC plus a guarantee-fee and base-servicing wedge of $+0.80$ points: recomputing the hazard-side back-out on the note-rate basis raises the CPR level by $+0.27$ points as a deterministic schedule difference while every trapped-dollar figure is bit-identical across the correction --- the benchmark's dollars never pass through the CPR scale --- and every peak lag and $U_2$ is unchanged at reporting precision (run \texttt{coupon\_convention\_amortization}; Table~\ref{tab:theil} is scored on the corrected basis), and any correlation computed against them differs only at the third decimal; Table~\ref{tab:estimators}'s note states which rows use which. 

\paragraph{Accounting bases.} Every recovery percentage in this paper is quoted on one of four accounting bases, which differ only in two orthogonal choices, each a pure accounting layer with no modeling content: whether the common curtailment flow is netted out, and whether the estimation sample is reweighted to the SOMA book's coupon mix. The \emph{standalone-scorer basis} makes neither adjustment. The \emph{benchmark-consistent shared basis} nets the common curtailment flow just described (\$69.56 billion over the window) from every U.S. leg identically, a flat 9.1 percentage points of the benchmark for every U.S. estimator. The \emph{full-book basis} instead reweights the standalone figure from the estimation sample's coupon mix to the SOMA book's weighted-average coupon (WAC), with no curtailment netting. The \emph{composed basis} applies both adjustments: the same 9.1-point netting to the full-book (book-coupon-reweighted) figure. Because the netting is bit-identical across the central and null legs of every comparison, it cancels exactly from the lock-in marginal and from every cross-estimator gap, an invariance that holds at every cell of the calibration box and under a $\pm 25\%$ stress on the assumed curtailment rate (derivation and stress in Section~\ref{sec:robustness-hybrid}; the four bases are collected for every hazard leg in Table~\ref{tab:bases}). The cancellation is term-by-term monthly, not merely aggregate: the netted flow is the product of a shared monthly rate and the shared actual-holdings path, so any time profile for curtailment (calendar-seasonal, or income elasticities that differ across sub-periods) cancels identically from the marginal and from every cross-estimator gap, moving only the common wedge. The functional form of the income-to-rate mapping is immaterial to the marginal for the same reason: any alternative map (concave, threshold, or piecewise in income) produces some monthly rate series, which is again a shared profile, so it can move only the common wedge. Main-text headline figures are quoted on the shared basis throughout.

I construct a second, expectations-based benchmark alongside the cap-relative one, because the cap-relative construction carries a tension the complement addresses: the caps were widely expected to be non-binding for agency MBS, so a shortfall measured against them mixes an anticipated mechanical component with genuine surprise. Measured instead against the Federal Reserve's own ex-ante projection, the baseline path published in the New York Fed's May 2022 Open Market Operations annual report \citep{omo2021}, whose projections start from the February 2022 balance sheet, incorporate the FOMC's May 2022 Plans, and model runoff from June 2022, with the published year-end figures spread uniformly within each printed year and clipped to the window under a rule fixed before the numbers were seen (the report prints year-end levels only, and no staff projection at monthly granularity is public for any vintage covering this window; the spread rule was pre-committed precisely because the source is coarser than monthly), projected agency MBS runoff over the window is \$740.6 billion against realized runoff of \$652.8 billion: an expectations-based shortfall of \$87.8 billion, 11.5\% of the cap-relative \$764.7 billion. Equivalently, the Federal Reserve's own ex-ante projection already implied 75.6--88.5\% of the realized cap-shortfall, the range spanning defensible intra-2022 allocations of the projection's printed annual net: \$676.9 billion, or 88.5\%, under the pre-committed uniform-spread central construction. That a source predating every estimate in this paper already anticipated the large majority of the shortfall is the substantive point, and it stands on its own. What does not stand is a sharper reading it invites. Under the uniform-spread construction the 88.5\% sits 0.2 points from the 88.7\% the $\beta_1 = 0$ mechanical null recovers on the benchmark-consistent basis, and I presented that near-equality as convergent evidence. I withdraw the presentation: the 0.2 points require two conditions at once. They require the in-sample 4.0\% turnover floor, because at the off-window floor the paper headlines the null recovers 85.7\% and falls 2.8 points \emph{below} the projection rather than 0.2 above it; and they require the uniform-spread allocation, because the settlement-aware allocation puts the anticipated share at 75.6\%, which the null clears by 13.2 points in-sample and 10.1 points at the headline floor. A gap that ranges from $-2.8$ to $+13.2$ points across two disclosed choices is not a convergence, and I now report the projection range and the null's recovery as two separately stated quantities. Both recoveries are production-floor-form reads: under the additive form the null recovers 35.6\% on the benchmark-consistent basis at the headline floor (44.7\% standalone; Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}), so the projection is being compared against one form's null rather than against a form-invariant object. The agreement that survives is coarse and is the one I rely on: under the production floor form both objects put the anticipated mechanical component in the large majority of the shortfall. That majority is a fact about where the caps were set rather than about the size of the rate response --- the caps sat above any prepayment path this book could have delivered --- so leading with it understates lock-in, and the reading that speaks to mortgage markets is the marginal's share of the expectations-based shortfall, reported below. On the allocation choice itself, the central 88.5\% rides on the uniform-spread rule's treatment of the printed 2022 annual net, which nets first-half settlement inflows against June--December runoff, and the settlement-aware allocation that assigns those inflows to the first half (the realized pattern: holdings rose \$92.3 billion over January--May 2022 in the benchmark's own SOMA series) is what lowers the anticipated share toward roughly three-quarters (75.6\%), leaving the large-majority reading, and every gate and threshold of the run, unchanged. Parametric within-year ramps bound the remaining allocation freedom: linear 2022 ramps move the anticipated share only to 88.0--89.0\%, the December-2025 window clip spans 86.1--90.6\% between its all-front and linear-back extremes, and the extreme bracket that pushes all 2025 runoff to the clipped December raises the share to 115.3\%; the mechanical-majority threshold survives at every allocation. Every percentage-of-benchmark figure in this paper remains a cap-basis quantity; dollar objects and estimator rankings are basis-invariant, the same dollar decomposition rescales by the $8.7\times$ benchmark ratio on the expectations basis, and the identified lock-in marginal amounts to 49\% of the expectations-based shortfall under the central uniform-spread construction at the headline off-window floor ($+\$42.6$ billion), or 80\% at the in-sample calibration point ($+\$70.3$ billion); because that shortfall is itself allocation-dependent, the ratio falls as the anticipated share falls (to roughly 23\% and 38\% respectively at the 75.6\% allocation) and is undefined at the extreme 115.3\% bracket, where the expectations-based shortfall turns negative. Every one of these ratios rests on one condition I cannot test: it reads the expectations-based shortfall as a residual free of lock-in, when the projection's own prepayment model may already have embedded part of the channel. I cannot recover that model from the published path of holdings, so whatever lock-in the projection already anticipated sits inside the \$740.6 billion rather than in the \$87.8 billion residual, and the ratios are upper bounds on lock-in's share of the genuine surprise, exact only if the projection embedded none of the channel; a second condition points the same way and is likewise untestable from the published record: the projection conditions on the spring-2022 market-implied rate path, well below what was realized, so part of the residual is rate-path surprise rather than lock-in, and the ratios bound the lock-in share of a mixed surprise. Both conditions are properties of the projection rather than of any one quotation of it, so they govern every site at which these ratios appear --- the introduction, this section, and the conclusion --- and each ratio is to be read as an allocation-conditional upper bound on lock-in's share of a mixed lock-in-and-rate-path surprise, not as a partition of that surprise. One question of standing precedes this accounting, and I state it rather than leave it implicit: a shortfall against the MBS cap is not by itself evidence that a stated policy objective was missed. The Committee's operating object was the aggregate securities portfolio's decline, Treasury runoff ran against its own caps throughout the window, and nothing in this paper shows that reserves or the aggregate path came in off the intended course because MBS ran slow. What the \$764.7 billion measures is the \emph{composition} of the decline --- MBS principal that did not arrive on the book's own schedule --- and the duration consequences that composition carries (Section~\ref{sec:discussion-fallout}); it is a statement about how the decline was delivered and what that delivery cost in portfolio structure, not a claim that the decline itself fell short.

The benchmark measures shortfall relative to the redemption cap, and the cap is a ceiling on runoff rather than a forecast of it: New York Fed staff projected the below-cap regime from the month the \$35 billion cap took effect \citep{nyfed2022}, so the shortfall relative to the cap is an upper bound on the shortfall relative to any reasonable ex ante expectation, an upper bound the complement above now quantifies: the expectations-based shortfall is \$87.8 billion, 11.5\% of the cap-relative figure. I retain the cap as the headline counterfactual for two reasons. First, the cap is the only policy-committed quantity available: the staff projection underlying the complement is a baseline forecast, not an FOMC-stated target, and adopting any forecast, the Federal Reserve's or a model's, as the headline benchmark would render it circular with respect to models of expected prepayment, the object under study. Second, the cap-relative construction is a policy-relevant quantity: it measures the gap between the maximum balance-sheet reduction the FOMC authorized and the reduction the mortgage structure delivered. Throughout the paper, ``trapped liquidity'' and ``shortfall'' accordingly denote the shortfall relative to the cap.

\section{Agent-Based Model Results: A Synthetic-Population Stress Test}\label{sec:abm}

This section is a stress test of the household-choice hypothesis, not the paper's main estimate, and the first thing to report about it is what its own cross-design leg establishes about its scope, because that result governs how everything below should be read. Re-running the same decision rule on real Freddie Mac structural covariates, under the recalibration rule the model already applies elsewhere, recovers 59.3\% of the benchmark on the committed draw and 60.2\% averaged over fifty seeds---above the 50\% threshold fixed before that run at which this section's paradigm reading would be undercut, and undercutting on all fifty of them---rising to 76.3\% once the sample is reweighted to the SOMA book's own coupon composition. Three things bound that reading and travel with it wherever it appears: the same reweight moves the frozen-calibration leg the other way, down to 12.6\%; the covariates swapped in are structural only, since the loan records contain no analogue for mobility desire or loss aversion, so no leg here replaces a synthetic behavioral population with a real one; and the verdict the run supports is that the contrast cannot, on that test alone, be cleanly attributed to modeling paradigm rather than to calibration and data source (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign}). What this section falsifies is therefore a \emph{synthetic-population} household-choice specification: no specification tested here explains the Federal Reserve's trapped liquidity, and why it does not is not cleanly separable from the population it was tested on. A second qualifier travels with the verdict and is developed where it binds: the multi-vintage stage moved against its own predicted direction and the inversion is undiagnosed (Appendix~\ref{sec:robustness-extensions}).

\subsection{The Synthetic-Population Estimate: Baseline, Production, Extensions, and the Burnout Falsification}\label{sec:abm-results}

Section~\ref{sec:identification} \emph{declines} that same cross-design recovery as evidence that the imported lock-in elasticity is too small, on the ground that a recovery level is not what that design identifies; read as a scope limit on this section's verdict, which is how it is used above, it survives that objection, and neither use licenses the other. The production estimate is the synthetic-population special case, and it is the case in which the test bites: every specification tested here --- rational baseline, behavioral extensions, and a burnout variant --- moves further from, or fails to close, the \$764.7 billion benchmark, so I report them once and move to the hazard framework of Section~\ref{sec:hazard}. A purely rational baseline projects a structural cash flow deficit of \$544.0 billion (71.1\% of benchmark). The production specification adds scheduled amortization, a live CUSIP-level parse of SOMA holdings, a corrected empirical CPR back-out, a settlement-lag kernel, and the previously excluded 15-year MBS book in cohort weighting and scheduled amortization (structural terms only); its headline quantity is the seed distribution, not any single draw, at a fifty-seed mean of \$103.7 billion, or 13.6\% of the benchmark, with the frozen seed-42 draw (\$91.0 billion, 11.9\%, near the 34th percentile) retained as the reproducibility anchor the run manifests freeze. The kernel is retained in production and both frozen run manifests record it as applied, but it is a documented timing null rather than a fit improvement: a pre-fold-in ablation left the peak cross-correlation unchanged at $r = +0.192$ at lag $-3$ with the kernel on or off, and the kernel-applied production run peaks at $+0.195$ at the same lag. The production specification is not kernel-free, and every figure reported here is the kernel-applied run's. Seed dispersion dominates the third digit: the seed-level standard deviation is \$24.5 billion, a $\pm$1~SD share band of 10.4--16.8\%; the 95\% CI of the mean, \$96.9--\$110.5 billion, prices only simulation noise in the mean itself; and the 34th percentile is pinned only to within roughly $\pm$7 percentage points from 50 draws, so the ABM's share is better read as on the order of 10--17\% than as a three-digit point estimate (Figure~\ref{fig:mc}, Appendix~\ref{sec:method-abm}). The path fit is worse than the level fit and no extension repairs it (Figure~\ref{fig:abmpaths}, Appendix~\ref{sec:method-abm}): simulated mean CPR runs at 11.68\% against an empirical 5.14\%, the raw path correlation is negative at lag 0 ($r = -0.318$) with no value inside a $\pm$3-month window exceeding the +0.19 at the three-month lead, and the path $R^2$ is $-6.984$ (throughout, $R^2$ is computed as $1-\mathrm{SSE}/\mathrm{SST}$ against a mean-only null, so a negative value indicates fit worse than a constant at the empirical mean). Layering in three literature-grounded behavioral extensions --- a debt-to-income constraint with asymmetric loss aversion and a rate-shock freeze, income-scaled curtailment, and multi-vintage coupon composition --- moves the model to 54.9\%, then 45.1\%, then 33.7\% of the benchmark (Figure~\ref{fig:waterfall}); the first move is a recalibration artifact rather than evidence for loss aversion, since holding a real-world turnover floor fixed under a steeper loss-aversion penalty mechanically inflates rate sensitivity, and none of the three touches the CPR path's central failure, each operating on the level of simulated mobility rather than its timing. A fourth variant fails more informatively: path-dependent prepayment ``burnout,'' implemented as closed-population survivor selection, collapsed simulated CPR to approximately zero and raised trapped liquidity to 147\% of the benchmark, because a closed population of 10,000 agents depletes its mobile mass during the low-rate 2020--2021 window before QT even begins --- an absorbing state incompatible with a real MBS pool that continuously receives new originations, and \citepos{lesniewski2026} selection identity in its degenerate limit. It is a negative finding, not used in production, and it is why burnout is treated at the cohort rather than the per-agent level in Section~\ref{sec:hazard}. Figure~\ref{fig:recovery} places these levels beside the other estimators. The decision rule, every behavioral parameter and its source, the macroeconomic-friction and structural extensions, the mechanism-level detail behind all three behavioral stages, and the 15-year fold-in are specified in Appendices~\ref{sec:method-abm}, \ref{sec:method-frictions}, \ref{app:abmspec}, \ref{sec:robustness-extensions} and~\ref{sec:robustness-pipeline} and are reproduced end to end by the replication package (Appendix~\ref{app:ledger}); this section reports results only.

\subsection{Interpretation}\label{sec:abm-interp}

Every ABM specification tested here either widens the gap to the benchmark or leaves it unchanged, its CPR path stays uncorrelated or negatively correlated with the empirical path throughout, and Section~\ref{sec:robustness}'s scale-sensitivity replication finds no size effect on the production specification, so what remains is either that household-level decision-making does not govern the majority of the shortfall, or that this specification is missing a mechanism. The cross-design leg reported at the head of this section bears directly on that either-or and does not settle it: handing the same rule real structural covariates moves the recovery from 13.6\% to 60.2\% averaged over fifty seeds, which is what a missing-population problem looks like rather than a missing-mechanism one, but the behavioral layer stays synthetic in both legs, so the alternative is narrowed rather than eliminated. The clean falsification is the one that bypasses that free parameter entirely: the simulated-minimum-distance two-moment fit (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign}) returns joint infeasibility under a verdict vocabulary fixed before execution, its nearest admissible fit overshooting the benchmark at 107.1\%, and it does not run through the recalibration scale that qualifies the cross-design reading. Section~\ref{sec:hazard} tests the first branch directly on a different data source and modeling paradigm; whichever explanation holds, these results weigh against household mobility choices, rational or behavioral, as the primary price the Federal Reserve paid for secondary-market liquidity, and the institutional counterpart --- whether the par-payoff rule itself, rather than household behavior, drives the trap --- is examined as a securitization-policy alternative in Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}. One asymmetry in that comparison should be stated rather than left for a reader to notice. Section~\ref{sec:identification} insists that this design identifies a differential and not a level, and the ABM is nonetheless compared with Path B on levels, because the ABM admits no counterpart to the $\beta_1 = 0$ null. The reason is structural. In \eqref{eq:pathB} the elasticity is a separable term sitting over a floor that is measured independently and survives the elasticity's removal, so the null is still anchored to observed baseline turnover and lands at 85.7\% of the benchmark. The ABM has no such term: its 4--5\% turnover floor is not a component of \eqref{eq:abmmove} but a \emph{product} of it, obtained by searching the mobility-desire scale $\theta$ until the penalty-bearing rule returns 4--5\% CPR at an 8\% market rate, so zeroing the penalty removes the floor along with the channel. With the production $\theta = 43{,}882.8$ held fixed, the resulting leg returns 40.7\% CPR at the calibration point against the production rule's 4.8\%, over-retires the book, and scores $-259.4$\% of the benchmark; re-deriving $\theta$ under the null restores the anchor to 4.7\% but scores $+116.3$\%, because the re-search fits the null to the very turnover level the differential is meant to measure. The two readings imply marginals of $+270.4$ and $-105.3$ points. They do not bracket a magnitude; they disagree in sign, and neither is interpretable. I therefore report no ABM differential, and the estimator contrast of Section~\ref{sec:hazard} remains a contrast of levels, with the qualification that entails (run \texttt{abm\_null\_feasibility}; a feasibility probe, not a pre-committed estimate, and nothing in this paper's ranges depends on it).

\section{The Hazard Framework: A Structurally Distinct Estimator}\label{sec:hazard}

This is the estimator on which the paper's central decomposition rests. The ABM of Section~\ref{sec:abm} does not approach the hazard framework's recovery at the aggregate dollar level (91.3\% in the microsimulation form on the shared accounting basis of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-hybrid} at the headline off-window floor, 97.9\% at the in-sample calibration point; the estimated cohort-hazard form corroborates at 112.4\% but is excluded from headline ranges for the reasons Section~\ref{sec:patha} states): the ABM's production specification recovers 11.9\% on the frozen draw (fifty-seed mean 13.6\%), and Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign}'s cross-design leg leaves open whether that contrast belongs to modeling paradigm or to data source. Even its highest-recovery behavioral extension (the 54.9\% figure I attribute in Section~\ref{sec:abm-results} to a mechanical recalibration artifact rather than to loss aversion as a mechanism) falls well short. (The uncorrected rational baseline reaches 71.1\%, but that figure predates the scheduled-amortization, CUSIP-parse, and CPR-back-out corrections described in Section~\ref{sec:abm-results} and is not part of the production estimate family.) The hazard framework's recovery range, by contrast, rests on a different data source and modeling paradigm. Both hazard paths are evaluated against the same aggregate benchmark as the ABM, but only Path B's recovery is accurate on the benchmark-consistent accounting basis, and no estimator's monthly co-movement with the empirical path, Path B's included, survives removal of the window's shared trend (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}). Path A's monthly path fit is no better than the ABM's, a limitation Sections~\ref{sec:patha} and~\ref{sec:hazard-interp} quantify and assess, because it bears directly on how much evidential weight each recovery figure can carry.

\subsection{Motivation}\label{sec:hazard-motivation}

Section~\ref{sec:abm}'s pattern is difficult to reconcile with the hypothesis that the residual reflects a missing rational or behavioral household mechanism that a sufficiently rich utility function would eventually capture. It is more consistent with the hypothesis that the binding constraints on the empirical prepayment path lie at least partly outside the household decision function: in loan-level heterogeneity, servicer pipeline dynamics, and cohort-level path dependence that a fixed, representative-household population struggles to represent.

The hazard framework introduced in Section~\ref{sec:method-estimators} is built to test this hypothesis. It abandons the household utility function: no mobility-desire parameter, no loss-aversion coefficient, no utility-maximizing decision rule; it instead models loan-level survival directly, using proportional hazards (proportional above a baseline turnover floor; Section~\ref{sec:pathb}) estimated on real loan-level data, with the closed-population survivor-selection failure of Section~\ref{sec:abm-results} motivating a cohort-level rather than per-loan treatment of burnout. The framework retains the same \$764.7 billion empirical benchmark and the same QT window definition as the ABM for comparability, and comprises two paths: a stratum-month discrete-time hazard model estimated on aggregated loan-level data, and a loan-level microsimulation calibrated to literature elasticities with explicit competing risks.
