\subsection{Path B: Literature-Calibrated Microsimulation}\label{sec:pathb}

The first hazard path brings competing risks, literature-calibrated elasticities, and a cleaner isolation of the Danish counterfactual, without reintroducing the utility-maximizing machinery of the ABM. It simulates a stratified sample of 75,000 loans (40,234 of which are still active when the QT window opens; Appendix~\ref{app:params}), each carrying static covariates (FICO, region, original LTV, coupon, and a stratum identifier matching the Path A specification) and dynamic state (loan age, balance, and a regime-specific rate gap). Equity enters only through the static original-LTV covariate: no updated CLTV and no local house-price feed exists anywhere in the pipeline, so equity dynamics over the window are unmodeled---a disclosed design boundary for this turnover-dominated, deeply out-of-the-money window, with the FICO/LTV covariate ablation reported below bracketing what the static block contributes. Each loan's monthly hazard of prepayment is specified as
\begin{equation}\label{eq:pathB}
h^{\mathrm{prep}}_{i,t} = \max\!\left\{\, \underline{h},\; h_0\!\left(a_{i,t}\right) \exp\!\left( \beta_1\, g_{i,t} + \beta_F\, z^{\mathrm{FICO}}_{i} + \beta_L\, z^{\mathrm{LTV}}_{i} + \beta_B\, B_{s(i),t} \right) \right\},
\end{equation}
with a competing hazard of default
\begin{equation}\label{eq:default}
h^{\mathrm{def}}_{i,t} = h^{\mathrm{def}}_0 \exp\!\big( \gamma_r\, \sigma_{i,t} + \gamma_F\, z^{\mathrm{FICO}}_{i} + \gamma_L\, z^{\mathrm{LTV}}_{i} \big), \qquad \sigma_{i,t} = \max\{0,\ -\tilde g_{i,t}\},
\end{equation}
where $\sigma_{i,t}$ is rate stress ($\tilde g_{i,t} = g_{i,t}/100$ is the rate gap of \eqref{eq:pathB} expressed in decimal units, so $\sigma$ is the decimal magnitude of an out-of-the-money gap), and $h^{\mathrm{def}}_0 = 3\times10^{-4}$ monthly, a prior implying $0.36\%$ per year against a realized Freddie Mac default rate of $0.31\%$ over the window on this paper's own GMAR extraction---corroboration the calibration did not use, since the prior was fixed before that series was assembled. Every Path B parameter is fixed a priori in the configuration and none is estimated on the benchmark; Appendix~\ref{app:params} collects the full parameter vector with provenance, together with the delinquency transition matrix. Table~\ref{tab:hazard-notation} maps every symbol here to its Path A counterpart. Here $g_{i,t}$ is the rate gap (the loan's coupon minus the prevailing market rate, in 100 bp units, negative when the borrower is locked in, so that the positive $\beta_1$ of \eqref{eq:beta1} suppresses prepayment exactly when the par-payoff penalty binds); $B_{s(i),t} \in [0,1]$ is the cohort-level burnout state of loan $i$'s stratum $s(i)$ (the stratum's cumulative prepaid UPB as a share of its original UPB, capped at one, initialised at zero for every stratum at the window open so that it accumulates only within-window prepayment against an original-UPB denominator that still includes pre-window-terminated loans---window-cumulative, not life-cumulative---so $\beta_B = -0.5$ scales the hazard by $e^{-0.5 B} \in [0.61, 1]$); and $\underline{h}$ is the baseline turnover floor: the monthly single-month-mortality (SMM) equivalent of 4\% annual CPR, $\underline{h} = 1-(1-0.04)^{1/12} \approx 0.0034$. It floors total turnover, not a strictly involuntary component of it: deep-discount-cohort turnover at this level includes discretionary life-cycle moves and cash-out refinancings alongside strictly involuntary events, no component decomposition exists in this design, and the strictly-involuntary share---death, divorce, and forced relocation---is plausibly well under half. Where the floor's semantics do institutional work---selecting the U.S.-intercept Danish anchor (Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}) and retaining the max floor form (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor})---the operative premise is only that baseline turnover cannot fall below its observed deep-discount level when the payoff rule changes, which the total-turnover reading supports; those choices are conventions consistent with that premise, not consequences of a strictly-involuntary decomposition. The floor's level is anchored to the same empirical observation as the ABM's mobility calibration (Appendix~\ref{sec:method-frictions}): turnover on deeply out-of-the-money discount cohorts during 2023--2024, which lies \emph{inside} the June 2022--November 2025 evaluation window. While no Path B parameter touches the benchmark dollar figure, the specification's dominant level-setter is measured from in-window prepayment behavior. The verification content below is the marginal over the $\beta_1 = 0$ null rather than the aggregate level itself. The pre-committed floor sweep of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor} shows that even this marginal is conditional on the floor calibration: it spans $+10.8$ to $+5.5$ points at the central elasticity across the empirically plausible 3--5\% floor range ($+9.2$ at the production 4\% floor), with that interval, not any point inside it, the identified object; its positivity at every floor and band point swept is a consistency check the window's rate configuration cannot let fail (Section~\ref{sec:identification}). The floor's functional form matters too: replacing the hard maximum of \eqref{eq:pathB} with a competing-risks combination, under which the elasticity is never censored, moves the production marginal to $+11.25$ points and makes it nearly floor-invariant ($+11.29$ to $+11.21$ across the same 3--5\% range; Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}). The max-form marginal's floor-dependence is censoring-driven rather than evidence about the elasticity. The floor binds in 36\% of the 1,683,124 evaluated loan-months in the production U.S. run. A substantial share of the aggregate level rides on the floor rather than the elasticity, consistent with the no-lock-in null of Section~\ref{sec:hazard-interp}, which shows the mechanical model recovers 97.8\% of the benchmark under the standalone scorer (88.7\% on the shared accounting basis). Those are production-floor-form levels: under the additive form the same null recovers 44.7\% at the off-window floor against the production form's 94.8\%, both on the standalone scorer (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}). Proportionality in \eqref{eq:pathB} accordingly holds above the floor, not through it. The baseline hazard $h_0$ follows the 100~PSA seasoning ramp (CPR rising 0.2 percentage points per month of loan age to 6\% at month 30) applied at monthly frequency via $\mathrm{SMM} = 1-(1-\mathrm{CPR})^{1/12}$. The rate-gap coefficient is calibrated from \citet{liebersohn2024}, who estimate that a 100 bp increase in the prevailing rate above the locked-in coupon reduces quarterly mobility probability by 5.5\% to 7.7\% depending on specification; I adopt the 6.5\% midpoint of that band as the ex-ante central scenario, with the band edges as sensitivity bounds\footnote{The central 6.5\% is the midpoint of that band, adopted as the ex-ante central scenario rather than a published point estimate, with 5.5\% and 7.7\% as sensitivity bounds. Quarterly probability shocks are mapped to the proportional-hazard coefficient $\beta_1$ via the survival-function transform in \eqref{eq:beta1}, evaluated at a baseline turnover probability of 6\% in the transform's quarterly slot---a turnover-proxy calibration auxiliary rather than a measured quarterly mobility rate, and well above \citepos{liebersohn2024} own 1.5\% zero-gap quarterly moving level---that affects only the conversion level, not the elasticity band itself, and only weakly (derivation and range in Appendix~\ref{app:params}). \citet{fonseca2024} report a related but distinct estimand (a roughly 9\% reduction in moving rates per percentage point of mortgage-rate delta), which I cite as independent corroboration of the lock-in magnitude, not as a source of the band.}, converted to a monthly hazard via the survival-function transform
\begin{equation}\label{eq:beta1}
\beta_1 = -\ln\!\left[\frac{1-\big(1-(1-\delta)\,P_q\big)^{1/3}}{1-\big(1-P_q\big)^{1/3}}\right],
\end{equation}
where $\delta$ is the proportional quarterly mobility decline per 100 bp of rate gap, $P_q = 0.06$ a reference baseline turnover probability occupying the transform's quarterly slot---a conversion auxiliary rather than a measured quarterly mobility rate, well above \citepos{liebersohn2024} own 1.5\% zero-gap quarterly moving level (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign}), and retained because the conversion is nearly insensitive to it over $P_q \in (0, 0.12]$ (Appendix~\ref{app:params})---and the inner terms are the monthly single-month mortalities implied by constant-hazard compounding within the quarter, $1 - P_q = (1 - h_m)^3$. Equation~\eqref{eq:beta1} is the transform implemented in the codebase: at the central $\delta = 0.065$ it evaluates to $\beta_1 = 0.0686$ (unrounded $0.068571$), the value carried in the production configuration and rounded to $0.069$ in Table~\ref{tab:params}. The superseded $0.0693$ is a distinct object, the continuous-hazard variant $-\ln\!\big[\ln(1-(1-\delta)P_q)/\ln(1-P_q)\big] = 0.0693$ (unrounded $0.069258$), which the simulation does not use; the two forms differ by $0.0007$ and both round to $0.069$, but only \eqref{eq:beta1} reproduces the reported results. The transform yields the dimensionless log-hazard-ratio implied by a quarterly probability decline (it replaces a divide-by-three approximation, not a hazard itself), and \eqref{eq:pathB} then applies $\beta_1$ linearly per 100 bp of gap. At QT-era gaps of roughly $-380$ bp this compounds the local quarterly estimate to a hazard ratio of about $0.77$ ($\approx e^{3.8 \times -0.069}$), a log-linear extrapolation beyond the range in which the source elasticity was estimated. I flag it as a functional-form assumption and test it directly: a piecewise-concave transform (full marginal effect over the first 200 bp of lock-in, half beyond) moves the recovery from 107.0\% to 105.7\% (\$808.4 billion) and slightly improves the contemporaneous path correlation ($+0.281$ versus $+0.190$, peak lag unchanged at $-3$). The log-linear extrapolation is not load-bearing for the level, and is for the marginal---but the asymmetry is one of proportion, not of magnitude, and the same absolute $\pm 1$-point convention cannot support both readings. A pre-committed rerun of the paired legs under the concave transform (run \texttt{concave\_marginal}) puts the concave marginal at $+7.87$ points against the production $+9.20$ at the in-sample floor, and at $+5.06$ against $+5.57$ at the off-window point floor. At the in-sample floor the concave null is bit-identical to the production null (\$748.185 billion on both legs, because $\beta_1 = 0$ leaves nothing for the transform to act on), so the level move and the marginal move are numerically the \emph{same} 1.32 points. That 1.32 points is 1.2\% of a 107.0\% recovery and 14\% of a $+9.20$-point marginal, and it is that relative reading, not the absolute one, on which the transform is immaterial to the level and material to the marginal; on the absolute $\pm 1$-point materiality threshold the floor-form test of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor} applies, both moves clear it, and the transform dimension travels with the marginal's operative uncertainty in Table~\ref{tab:uncertainty}.

One more assumption travels with the import, and nothing here tests it. The decline \citet{liebersohn2024} estimate is a proportional fall in a quarterly ZIP-code moving probability, while \eqref{eq:pathB} applies it to a loan's total prepayment hazard, which contains refinancing as well as moving and which is floored at a turnover level that, as noted above, itself mixes discretionary life-cycle moves and cash-out refinancings. No split of realized prepayment into moving and refinancing components exists in this design, so treating the two objects as one is a specification assumption rather than a result. Two further transport assumptions travel with the import and are named here rather than left implicit: the source estimand is a ZIP-code-level moving hazard applied as a loan-level log-hazard coefficient, an aggregation transport that assumes response homogeneity; and it is estimated on a population not conditioned on mortgage survival, while \eqref{eq:pathB} applies it to a pool from which 34,734 of the 75,000 sampled loans had already prepaid before the window opened --- a survival-selected pool depleted of the rate-responsive, so this transport's direction is signed toward attenuation rather than amplification, and priced: under the gamma-frailty selection identity the attenuation is $a = S^{\theta}$ at the measured pre-window survival share, and the off-window marginal reads $+5.1$/$+4.6$/$+3.7$ points at $\theta = 0.25/0.5/1$ (run \texttt{attenuation\_sensitivity}; $\theta$ is not estimable in this design and is displayed as the free parameter --- Path A's burnout coefficient, the one object that speaks to it, is not distinguishable from zero under either bootstrap scheme).

Each month, both hazards are evaluated for every active loan. In the production configuration prepayment is fractional: each active loan's balance is drained deterministically by $h^{\mathrm{prep}}$ (a per-loan uniform draw for prepayment is a non-production discrete branch), while a uniform draw governs the default hazard, sending hits into the delinquency pipeline (current loans to D30, already-delinquent loans to default), where they transition through the Markov delinquency-resolution matrix of Appendix~\ref{app:params}. A proportional rescaling guards the case $h^{\mathrm{prep}} + h^{\mathrm{def}} > 1$. It is a safety clamp, not a competing-risks construction. It never binds in production: across all 1,683,124 evaluated loan-months per regime the maximum hazard sum is 0.016, two orders of magnitude below the threshold, at which level the exact exponential cause-allocation construction $1-\exp(-(\lambda_p+\lambda_d))$ with shares $\lambda_p/(\lambda_p+\lambda_d)$ differs from mine only at second order in the hazards. In the taxonomy of standard competing-risks formulations \citep{putter2007}, Path B is a discrete-time multi-state model parameterized entirely through cause-specific hazards: Equations~\eqref{eq:pathB} and \eqref{eq:default} are monthly event probabilities conditional on the loan remaining active (the estimand of discrete-time cause-specific regression, as implemented in open-source packages such as PyDTS \citep{meir2025}), with the delinquency pipeline of Appendix~\ref{app:params} extending the two-cause structure to a multi-state process with cures rather than an immediately absorbing default. No subdistribution (Fine--Gray) hazard \citep{fine1999} appears in the specification, and none is needed: that device places covariate effects directly on cumulative incidence functions, whereas the simulation obtains every cumulative-incidence functional (prepaid UPB, the delinquency stock, trapped liquidity) by forward composition of the cause-specific hazards. One caveat qualifies the mapping: in production, prepayment is a fractional balance-flow intensity rather than a per-loan absorbing transition, so the cause-specific reading is exact for default and holds at the balance-aggregate level for prepayment. Prepaid balances settle to simulated SOMA cash flow in the month they occur; no settlement delay is modeled on this path.

The transform above prices how the elasticity extrapolates in the gap; a second mapping assumption is what the elasticity applies to. \citepos{liebersohn2024} estimand is a moving probability, and \eqref{eq:pathB} applies the implied $\beta_1$ to the entire voluntary hazard, moving and refinancing alike. A pre-committed bracket prices that convention (run \texttt{moving\_share\_bracket}; the gap response enters through a mixture, with $1-s$ of the voluntary hazard held at its no-response level and $s$ of it carrying the full response, the turnover floor unscaled, and parity at $s = 1$ against the committed central leg plus an algebraic identity probe fixed in the script header before any run): at the production calibration the marginal is $+\$37.7$ billion ($+4.9$ points) at $s = 0.5$ and $+\$19.2$ billion ($+2.5$ points) at $s = 0.25$, against the production $+\$70.3$ billion at $s = 1$, close to proportional in $s$. Re-run at the 4.991\% off-window floor the paper headlines, the bracket is $+\$25.7$ billion ($+3.36$ points) at $s = 0.5$ and $+\$13.9$ billion ($+1.82$ points) at $s = 0.25$ against $+\$42.6$ billion at $s = 1$ (run \texttt{moving\_share\_bracket\_offwindow}), mildly super-proportional because the higher floor censors the elasticity in more loan-months and censored contributions do not scale with $s$. For this book's rate configuration the convention is therefore signed: applying the imported elasticity to less of the hazard can only shrink the marginal, and by roughly its share. No loan-purpose field exists in the ingested data, so $s$ is a bracketing parameter rather than an estimate, and the production convention $s = 1$ remains the reported form. \citet{graybill2026} corroborate the imported band on exactly this margin: their transaction-record estimate of the sale-hazard response --- roughly 7--11\% per percentage point of rate gap, from records that touch no credit file --- is an estimate of the moving component that $s$ varies.

The floor enters \eqref{eq:pathB} as a single constant $\underline{h}$ broadcast to every loan-month, carrying no rate-gap, calendar-time, or covariate argument, so the specification treats baseline turnover as acyclical and assigns every rate-responsive movement in prepayment to the elastic channel. The marginal-over-null design cancels the floor's level, since both the central and the $\beta_1 = 0$ legs carry the identical constant, but it does not cancel a floor that itself co-varies with the rate cycle: to the extent that involuntary moves are themselves suppressed when a household is locked in, that co-movement is absorbed into the estimated elastic marginal. The identified marginal at the in-window calibration is therefore best read as total rate-attributable prepayment suppression, an upper bound on the strictly-voluntary elasticity's contribution rather than a clean voluntary-versus-involuntary decomposition. At the off-window calibration the ordering reverses: the demotion's window component (roughly \$20.9 billion; Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}) is in-window baseline turnover that the off-window floor classifies as rate-suppressed and the marginal no longer counts, so the off-window $+\$42.6$ billion bounds total rate-attributable suppression from below (the matched-depth $+\$63.5$ billion restores that component), and the two calibrations bracket two estimands rather than conservative and liberal readings of one. The level sweep of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor} already brackets any plausible constant-floor excursion (the 3--5\% range keeps the marginal within $+10.8$ to $+5.5$ points at the central elasticity), and the within-run cyclical variant is now tested directly: replacing the constant with $\underline{h}_t = 4\% \times (1 + \kappa z_t)$, where $z_t$ is the window-standardized 30-year mortgage rate and $\kappa = 0$ nests (and in the run exactly reproduces) the production specification, moves the marginal between $+7.1$ and $+9.4$ points across $\kappa \in [-0.5, +0.5]$, every cell inside the level sweep's bracket.

That range is mostly not a measure of cyclicality, and I no longer read it as one. A separate pre-committed run (\texttt{floor\_cyclical\_permutation}) reproduces all seven committed $\kappa$ cells bit-exactly and then decomposes them. Scrambling the floor path's time order within each $\kappa$ cell, preserving the multiset of monthly floor values and their mean exactly, leaves 85.0\% of the range span standing: 72 draws, twelve per non-zero $\kappa$, span $+7.20$ to $+9.12$ points against the committed $+7.09$ to $+9.36$. Switching only the combination rule removes it: under the competing-risks (additive) form the identical $\kappa$ grid spans $+11.22$ to $+11.27$ points, a span of $0.045$ points, so changing the rule alone eliminates 98.0\% of the max-form span, and the additive form moves less across the whole $\kappa$ grid than it moves across the 3--5\% constant-floor level sweep. The mechanism is the one-sided Jensen effect that Appendix~\ref{sec:robustness-seasonalfloor} diagnoses at length for the seasonal floor: under a hard maximum, dispersion in $\underline{h}_t$ lifts the effective hazard whenever the high cells bind and is truncated away where they do not, regardless of \emph{when} it occurs, so $\kappa$ is a dispersion dial before it is a cyclicality dial. Decomposing the low endpoint confirms it. At $\kappa = +0.5$, which departs $-\$16.09$ billion from production, level equivalence accounts for $-\$2.14$ billion (13.3\%), order-free dispersion for $-\$11.42$ billion (71.0\%), and genuine time-order for $-\$2.53$ billion (15.7\%): 84.3\% of that endpoint is order-free. The high endpoint is not an endpoint at all, departing from production by $+\$1.23$ billion, $+0.16$ points, at $\kappa = -0.1$.

A cyclicality signal does survive, and it is real but small. The true marginal falls outside the entire twelve-draw permutation range at every one of the six non-zero $\kappa$ (0 of 72 draws bracket it), and the order components are monotone in $|\kappa|$ and sign-consistent with the co-movement story the concession above states: $+\$5.29$, $+\$4.77$, and $+\$2.69$ billion at $\kappa = -0.5$, $-0.25$, $-0.1$, and $-\$1.42$, $-\$1.75$, and $-\$2.53$ billion at $+0.1$, $+0.25$, $+0.5$. A floor rising with rates does lower the marginal and one falling with rates does raise it. But scrambling the time order removes most of the grid's asymmetry while leaving its level drop intact: the permuted means differ across the sign of $\kappa$ by only $\$0.22$, $\$1.80$, and $\$6.01$ billion at $|\kappa| = 0.1$, $0.25$, and $0.5$, against a true spread of $\$13.82$ billion at $|\kappa| = 0.5$ ($+\$68.08$ billion at $\kappa = -0.5$ against $+\$54.26$ billion at $+0.5$). A two-sided response that survives losing the time order is the signature of dispersion rather than of co-movement: the symmetric part of the grid's movement is large and is dispersion, the asymmetric part is small and is cyclicality. What the grid bounds is therefore the marginal's sensitivity to within-run floor variation of this amplitude, of which at most \$5.29 billion in any cell is attributable to the direction of co-movement. That the concession's residual is bounded by the level sweep already reported holds a fortiori. One correction to the specification as earlier described: the window mean is pinned at the production 4\% only where the floor's non-negativity clip does not bind, and it binds in the two $|\kappa| = 0.5$ stress cells, whose realized window means are 4.0005\% and 4.0828\% rather than exactly 4\% (the artifact flags the clip per cell). Of those two, $\kappa = +0.5$ is the cell that sets the bracket's low end at $+7.09$ points, while $\kappa = -0.5$ sits mid-grid at $+8.90$; the invariant therefore fails in the cell that matters most for the reported range, and the convexity-matched flat floor equivalent to the $\kappa = +0.5$ path is 4.0977\%, not 4\%.

Aggregation from the 75,000-loan sample to the SOMA book applies balance weights: simulated prepaid UPB is scaled by the ratio of the Fed's MBS book to the sample balance, with no reweighting toward Ginnie Mae collateral (20.4\% of book face) or vintages outside 2017--2021; the Freddie-estimated hazards are extrapolated to those segments as-is. This extrapolation is not behaviorally one-to-one: Ginnie Mae pools are FHA/VA collateral with a distinct borrower composition and a servicer buyout channel absent from the conventional book, so Freddie voluntary speeds cannot be mapped onto the Ginnie share as a like-for-like behavioral prediction. The framework therefore does not model Ginnie behavior; it bounds the composition error that treating the Ginnie share as conventional induces. Two bounds constrain this extrapolation. Along the coupon dimension, Section~\ref{sec:robustness-hybrid}'s full-book reweighting (sample WAC 3.9\% mapped to the book's 2.49\%) moves Path B from 107.0\% to 109.1\%, a composition effect of about two percentage points --- a mixed-basis figure, since 3.9\% is the unweighted mean of borrower note rates while 2.49\% is the face-weighted mean of security pass-through coupons; converted to one convention the effect is $+0.2$ points (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-hybrid}, run \texttt{coupon\_convention\_reweight}). Along the agency dimension, an external bound now applies: Ginnie Mae's fixed-rate speeds ran 1.2--2.8 percentage points above Freddie Mac's across the window (5.2\% versus 4.0\% in February 2023; 10.3\% versus 7.5\% in May 2025; Ginnie Mae Global Markets Analysis Report, Recursion data), with the involuntary buyout channel supplying a large minority of that difference rather than most of it. Over the forty-two window months the mean Ginnie--Freddie CPR gap is $+2.14$ points, of which the voluntary component---the conditional repayment rate (CRR), prepayment net of the default-driven channel---supplies $+1.35$ points, or 63\%, against the buyout channel's (CDR) $+0.85$ points, or 40\%; the two shares sum past one hundred because the CPR $\approx$ CRR $+$ CDR extraction carries a small residual. The snapshot that most favours the buyout reading, CDR 2.1\% against 0.4\% in May 2025, is the buyout-dominated month rather than the representative one. Treating the 20.4\% Ginnie face share as conventional therefore mis-states book-wide CPR by at most $0.204 \times (1.2\text{--}2.8) \approx 0.24$--$0.57$ percentage points. At the null-to-central sensitivity of roughly \$83 billion per CPR point---the average book face over the window, times one percentage point of annual prepayment, times the window's three and a half years--- the composition error is bounded at \$20--\$47 billion (2.6--6.2\% of benchmark), signed toward overstating trapped liquidity. The bound is one-sided because the observed differential is: the published speeds run Ginnie above Freddie across the window, so the symmetric lower branch, a Ginnie-slower error signed toward understating trapped liquidity, does not arise in this data.

The vintage dimension carries no bound of either kind: the 2022 vintage alone is 23.1\% of book face (and pre-2017 vintages another 10.6\%; Appendix~\ref{sec:robustness-wal}), lies outside the 2017--2021 Freddie sample. Its lock-in state is not qualitatively different from the sampled vintages': 72.2\% of the 2022 vintage's book face carries a pass-through coupon below 3.0\% (SOMA CUSIP joint coupon $\times$ vintage cells, run \texttt{composition\_shift}), so the vintage is predominantly late-2021 and H1-2022 production sitting four to five points below the window's market rates, with only 13.2\% of it at 4.0\% or above. What the extrapolation lacks is not a different lock-in state but a different \emph{seasoning} state, and neither the coupon reweighting nor the Ginnie speed comparison constrains it. An observed-speed bound in the same template does: the per-quarter cohort cells retained from the Fannie Mae replication ingest carry observed QT-window prepayment for the out-of-sample vintage groups. Over the window the 2022 vintage prepaid at 4.48\% against the sampled universe's 4.30\% (an unrounded differential of $+0.17$ points) and the pre-2017 vintages at 5.26\% ($+0.96$ points; the 2015--2016 cohorts alone are 99.99\% of that tail's exposure and reproduce its speed unchanged), a share-weighted book-CPR error of $+0.14$ points. At the same \$83-billion-per-CPR-point sensitivity, a bound of \$11.7 billion, 1.5\% of benchmark, below the static Ginnie bound's own lower edge and more than a quarter of the headline lock-in marginal (27.6\% of the off-window \$42.6 billion; a sixth, 16.7\%, of the in-sample \$70.3 billion), signed the same conservative direction: out-of-sample vintages prepaid \emph{faster}, so trapped liquidity is if anything overstated. The same template applied as a marginal overlay --- scoring the 33.7\% out-of-window vintage share by its observed Fannie speeds in \emph{both} legs, so that share contributes zero marginal --- confines the identified marginal to the in-window-vintage share: $+3.7$ points, pure share scaling ($0.66\times$ against a nominal retained share of $0.663$, with the placebo within $0.0002$), the vintage counterpart of the Ginnie overlay's $+4.4$ (run \texttt{vintage\_overlay}); the two overlays cannot be composed without the agency-by-vintage cross-tabulation, since 2.4\% of book face is the 2022--Ginnie intersection. Three caveats travel with the bound: Fannie observed speeds proxy the book's, the pre-2017 leg is a seasoned acquisition tail rather than a random draw of old originations, and the 2022 leg is a full-year speed applied to an H1-tilted face.

Burnout is deliberately cohort-level rather than per-loan: a fractional burnout measure is not well-defined for a one-time, absorbing transition (Section~\ref{sec:abm-results}). Instead, each stratum tracks its cumulative prepaid UPB as a share of its original UPB (the $B \in [0,1]$ state defined under \eqref{eq:pathB}) after all prepayment transitions in a given month, broadcast to all of that stratum's remaining active loans before the next month's hazard is evaluated, and entering \eqref{eq:pathB} with the calibrated prior coefficient $\beta_B = -0.5$ (Appendix~\ref{app:params}); Path A's separately estimated burnout coefficient, statistically indistinguishable from zero (Section~\ref{sec:patha}), plays no role here. Because the simulated burnout state is endogenous, I test whether the channel is load-bearing with a $\beta_B = 0$ rerun on the production seed (run \texttt{burnout\_ablation}): trapped liquidity moves by \$6.58 billion ($-0.86$pp of benchmark, to 106.17\%), with the peak lag unchanged at $-3$. Two qualifications travel with that figure. It is measured at the in-window 4.0\% floor; at the 4.991\% off-window floor the paper headlines the same ablation is worth only \$3.48 billion ($-0.45$pp), because the floor's bind share rises from 36.3\% to 68.8\% of evaluated loan-months and the hard maximum makes burnout inert wherever it binds. And because the burnout state is endogenous to realized prepayment, the ablation moves the $\beta_1 = 0$ null as well as the central leg, so the identified marginal is not burnout-invariant: switching the coefficient off raises it from $+9.20$ to $+9.94$ points at the production floor and from $+5.57$ to $+6.51$ at the off-window floor. Both moves sit inside the $\pm 1$-point materiality convention, the second only barely, so I report them rather than resting anything on them. The FICO and LTV covariates receive the same treatment: a $\beta_F = \beta_L = 0$ rerun moves the recovery to 108.3\% ($+1.3$pp), and a rerun under coefficients estimated Path-A-style from the panel moves it to 101.3\% ($-5.7$pp), both at the in-window 4.0\% floor; at the 4.991\% off-window floor the same endpoints span more, not less --- 102.5\% against 94.3\% standalone, an 8.2-point swing --- while the marginal they bracket moves by only $+0.1$ points against $+1.9$ in-window, because both legs shift together where the floor censors more (run \texttt{covariate\_ablation\_offwindow}); Appendix~\ref{app:params} reports the estimation, whose imprecise point estimates are consistent with the priors' turnover-regime signs without rejecting alternatives. Production retains the ex-ante priors (the estimates are too imprecise to license replacing a pre-specified calibration mid-stream), and I flag the covariate block as the specification's largest single sensitivity: the two endpoints correspond to 99.2\% and 92.2\% on the shared accounting basis (the common netting makes the conversion exact), a swing comparable in size to the lock-in marginal itself.

The static Ginnie bound is now extended to a time-varying overlay built from the published series the bound's two snapshots came from. Extracting the full monthly agency CPR series (June 2017 through November 2025) from the December 2025 Global Markets Analysis Report (whose aggregation was revised in that issue to weighted-average unpaid principal balance, one consistent dollar-weighted methodology across the whole window) and scoring the 20.4\% Ginnie face share by the observed Ginnie speed in \emph{both} Path B legs moves the shared-basis central from 97.9\% to 89.3\% and the null from 88.7\% to 81.9\% in the raw construction, both legs at the in-sample calibration point, and confines the identified marginal to the conventional share: $+7.3$ points, positive under every variant tested (total-CPR, voluntary-only, and placebo), scaling with the conventional face share almost exactly ($0.797$ against $0.796$). Composed with the off-window headline calibration (run \texttt{ginnie\_overlay\_offwindow}), the overlay pair is 84.0\%/79.5\% central/null on the shared basis with a $+4.4$-point marginal, and the overlay marginal is pure conventional-share scaling ($0.797\times$) --- a change of estimand, a re-scoring onto the conventional sub-book, rather than a correction to the headline, identical across the primary, voluntary-only, and placebo series; the headline marginal therefore carries the conventional-extrapolation convention wherever quoted, with $+4.4$ points its overlay-scored member. The raw level shift overstates the Ginnie-specific effect, though: a placebo that scores the same 20.4\% share by the observed GSE-mean series moves the level by $+\$27.8$ billion on its own (the model's level calibration plus the full-universe-versus-SOMA composition difference, common to any observed-series overlay), so the Ginnie-specific differential contribution is $+\$38.5$ billion, 5.0\% of benchmark (the placebo and Ginnie-specific components, $\$27.8 + \$38.5 = \$66.3$ billion, reconcile with the raw 8.6-point shared-basis shift on the \$764.7 billion benchmark, $\approx\$65.8$ billion, to within rounding of the 0.1-point recovery shares): inside the static \$20--\$47 billion bound above, and consistent with the window-mean Ginnie--Freddie differential of 2.1 points the series itself shows against the bound's 1.2--2.8-point snapshots. The full-universe series if anything overstates the correction for SOMA's seasoned, low-coupon Ginnie holdings, the same signed direction as the static bound; and structural Ginnie inclusion remains out of scope for the modeling reason above: the overlay treats the buyout-inclusive Ginnie speed as data, which is what runoff accounting requires of a passthrough holder. This overlay and the age-standardized floor read of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor} cut the same way, and the joint cell has now been run under a pre-committed landing rule (run \texttt{b5\_joint\_cell}): composed, they return $+2.9$ points ($+\$22.4$ billion), within $0.1$ points of the proportional projection, and that measured point is the assembly's lower member.

Two Danish counterfactual mechanisms exist in this framework's pipeline, and only one is live in production. The first is a \emph{rate-gap swap}: holding the baseline hazard, rate-gap coefficient, covariates, and random draw logic fixed, the Danish leg expresses the market-value buyback in rate-equivalent units, so that the buyback discount offsets the locked-in spread; $g_{\mathrm{DK}} = g_{\mathrm{US}} + \Delta y_{\mathrm{buyback}} \approx 0$ whenever the loan is out of the money, and $g_{\mathrm{DK}} = g_{\mathrm{US}}$ when rates have fallen and the buyback price is capped at par (at the representative 3.0\% coupon / 6.8\% market state the U.S. gap is $-380$ bp while the Danish effective gap is zero, so the U.S. hazard is suppressed by the full locked-in spread while the Danish hazard resets toward its baseline seasoning curve). This swap defines the \emph{mechanism-substitution} variant (the specification behind the superseded 47.1\% Danish CPR of Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}), and it is \emph{not} the production mechanism. The production Danish leg is the Berger-recalibrated specification adopted in Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}: it replaces the rate-gap swap with the imported flat moving elasticity, so the production Danish hazard does not respond to the buyback discount at all. The swap machinery remains in the codebase and its PV-versus-par classification is still computed each month, but no production quantity consumes its output, which is why the discount-rate diagnostic of Appendix~\ref{sec:method-abm} moves the Danish leg by exactly \$0.00 billion. Table~\ref{tab:discount} reports that inertness: shifting the buyback discount rate down by 25 to 150 bp (through the primary--secondary spread range) flips thousands of monthly present-value-versus-par classifications while moving the Danish CPR path, roll-off path, and trapped-liquidity dollar by exactly zero on every scoring basis. Two limits apply to that result. I state both because together they retract the immunity I previously claimed from it. First, the diagnostic executed on the Danish-level bracketing leg, not on the production U.S.-intercept leg: its own artifact records a mean Danish CPR of 3.39\%, the bracketing anchor's speed. Under the production anchor the same independence does hold, but by construction rather than by measurement, because that branch replaces the rate gap with zeros before the hazard is evaluated. Second, and more important, the result is about the hazard, not about the accounting. The Danish leg credits every retired balance at par: its dynamic-balance loop books roll-off as balance times drain, with no price term anywhere in it. Netting the roughly \$199 billion scheduled component from the leg's \$669.3 billion of Danish roll-off leaves on the order of \$470 billion of buyback-triggered retirement credited at par, against a proxy discount of 32 to 34\% at the representative 3.0\% coupon / 6.8\% market state (36 to 38\% at the book's 2.49\% weighted-average coupon). A market-value credit would reduce the Danish leg's cash receipts by an amount larger than the $+\$61.2$ billion gap itself, and in the direction that shrinks it. The pipeline does not distinguish a cash haircut from a balance adjustment, and the incidence depends on which the buyback is, so a pre-committed bracket prices both readings from the committed pair rather than choosing one (run \texttt{buyback\_credit\_bracket}; gates and the interpretive rule fixed in the script header before execution): under the balance-adjustment reading face retirement is unchanged and the gap stands at $+\$61.2$ billion, while under the cash-haircut reading receipts fall by the proxy discount on the leg's \$470.7 billion of early face and the gap reverses, to $-\$89.4$ to $-\$117.7$ billion across the committed 32--38\% discount grid. The two readings are not symmetric answers to one question. The benchmark is a face-value object---Section~\ref{sec:method-benchmark} constructs it from the SOMA current face value series against the par-denominated caps---so the balance-adjustment reading is the benchmark-consistent one, and on the benchmark's own denomination the gap stands. The cash-haircut reading answers a different question, reserves actually drained, and its reversal has a name: the $1-P$ wedge on bought-back face is exactly the par windfall a moving household pays the bondholder under the U.S. rule and keeps under the Danish one, so the cash-flow cost is the disappearance of a household-to-bondholder transfer, not an added resource cost. The gap's sign is therefore incidence-conditional, the channel is priced rather than un-priced, and the discount-rate diagnostic still should not be read as immunity to the pricing critique.

Simulated on the 40,234 window-start survivors of the 75,000-loan stratified sample drawn from the same Freddie Mac 2017--2021 origination universe (Appendix~\ref{app:params} reports the full attrition accounting), this specification recovers \$818.5 billion, or 107.0\%, of the empirical benchmark, with a raw CPR correlation against the empirical path of $r = +0.190$ at zero lag and a peak cross-correlation of $r = +0.404$ at lag $-3$. Both are descriptors of the published series, not credentials: the monthly benchmark cannot adjudicate timing, since four clip-induced zero months dominate its differenced signal (Appendix~\ref{sec:robustness-seasonalfloor}). Throughout, the cross-correlation at lag $k$ pairs the simulated series at month $t$ with the empirical series at month $t+k$ (equivalently, the empirical series at month $t$ with the simulated series at month $t-k$; both series demeaned over the overlap, boundary months dropped), so at the peak $k=-3$ the simulated value at month $t$ is matched with the empirical value from three months earlier. That definition was inverted in print---the two series interchanged, which would have implied the opposite direction---but the direction claims themselves were always computed under the implemented convention (Appendix~\ref{app:ledger}). A direction check with synthetic data confirms that under this implementation a peak at $k=-3$ means the simulated path \emph{trails} the empirical path by three months. At this peak, the empirical SOMA-derived CPR path moves approximately three months ahead of the simulated path (a peak selected across the seven examined lags, so its nominal significance is overstated; no inference rests on this single coefficient). The level and band reported here follow the rate-gap coefficient's unit convention documented in Appendix~\ref{sec:robustness-pipeline} (the superseded figure is catalogued, with provenance, in Appendix~\ref{app:ledger}).

Importing Path B's elasticity unmodified, rather than fitting it to the benchmark, has two consequences. First, unlike Path A, whose rate-gap and burnout coefficients are estimated directly from the Freddie Mac sample, Path B's rate-gap elasticity is imported unmodified from \citet{liebersohn2024} and never touches the \$764.7 billion benchmark during calibration. The two are mutually reinforcing rather than redundant: Path A's fitted rate-gap coefficient is an in-sample estimate of the same suppression channel on the same loans ($+0.29$ per 100 bp; Section~\ref{sec:patha}), and the Fannie Mae replication reproduces its sign on a second agency's loan book at roughly $3.6\times$ the per-basis-point magnitude, so the two in-sample fits are directionally consistent with the imported band---but no more than that: a pre-committed test of $H_0\!: \beta_g \le 0$ that respects the diagnosed bootstrap bias (run \texttt{patha\_sign\_test}: BCa with a 296-refit jackknife acceleration, $p = 0.093$ stratum scheme and $0.412$ temporal; a 200-draw month-permutation of the rate-gap series, $p = 0.241$) does not reject at any conventional level, so the in-sample estimates are not statistically distinguishable from zero under bias-respecting constructions and the elasticity's evidential basis is the external literature alone. Within that basis, \citet{graybill2026} corroborate the adopted band's location independently: their transaction-record estimate puts the sale-hazard response at roughly 7--11\% per percentage point of rate gap, in a housing-market-equilibrium setting whose records touch no credit file. Like the Fonseca--Liu anchor below, it corroborates from at or above the adopted band rather than from its centre: only the 7\% low end falls inside the 5.5\%--7.7\% range and the midpoint sits above the high edge, so the adopted central calibration is the conservative choice against this anchor as well. A prepayment-hazard coefficient is also not unit-comparable to \citepos{liebersohn2024} quarterly-mobility decline, and Path A measures prepayment rather than pure mobility, so what remains is directional consistency, not triangulation of sign or direction, not magnitude. A second literature anchor corroborates from outside the band: propagating \citepos{fonseca2024} roughly 9\% moving-rate reduction per 100 bp through the same transform ($\beta_1 \approx 0.096$) and the same band machinery yields a marginal of $+\$87.9$ billion ($+11.5$ points) at the production floor, above the Liebersohn--Rothstein high edge ($+\$79.5$ billion, $+10.4$ points) yet inside the pre-committed floor-and-band box, so the adopted central calibration is the conservative choice among the available literature estimates; because that estimand is a moving-rate reduction rather than a quarterly-mobility decline, I read it as directional corroboration, not a competing point estimate.

Path B is accordingly not a second attempt to fit my benchmark but a structural verification exercise: it asks whether an elasticity estimated in a different empirical setting, dropped into a PSA-baseline hazard with competing risks and no tuning against my target, reproduces the Federal Reserve's actual QT runoff shortfall. Because the aggregate level is floor-dominated, landing within 8 percentage points of the benchmark is not, by itself, evidence for the imported elasticity, since the residual over the $\beta_1 = 0$ null that the elasticity must explain is itself a function of the floor calibration (the marginal spans $+2.1$ to $+13.2$ points across the calibration box of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}). The defensible verification claim is the bounded interval the calibration box implies; the marginal is also positive at every floor, band, and floor-form point tested, but under this window's rate configuration that positivity verifies the specification rather than identifying the mechanism (Section~\ref{sec:identification}), and I do not claim the level validates the elasticity's magnitude. But the timing is not part of that evidence: as Section~\ref{sec:hazard-interp} reports, a rerun with the elasticity set to zero reproduces the same three-month lead, so the lead cannot discriminate the elasticity from the mechanical model.

Second, propagating the full Liebersohn--Rothstein 5.5\%--7.7\% sensitivity band through the simulation yields a recovery range of \$809.9 billion to \$827.7 billion (105.9\%--108.2\% of benchmark under the standalone scorer; 96.8\%--99.1\% on the shared accounting basis of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-hybrid}), with the peak cross-correlation lag stable at $-3$ across the entire band (peak $r$ between $+0.371$ and $+0.423$; given the moving-block intervals above and the seven-lag search, these differences carry no evidential weight). Stronger assumed mobility suppression therefore maps monotonically to more trapped liquidity, as expected, and the central 107.0\% estimate should be read as a point within this interval; Figure~\ref{fig:band} plots the band. That band is a calibration interval, not sampling error, and the sampling dimension is now quantified separately: a stratified loan-level bootstrap specified ex ante in its script header (200 replicates resampling the 75,000-loan draw with replacement within strata, central and null legs paired per replicate, engine seed held at production) puts the central recovery's 95\% interval at $[107.0, 107.1]\%$ under the standalone scorer (replicate standard deviation 0.03 points), the null's at $[97.8, 97.9]\%$, and the paired lock-in marginal's at $[+9.17, +9.23]$ points (standard deviation 0.016); re-run under the cluster scheme at this same floor, the paired marginal reads $[+8.27, +10.19]$ points (sd 0.52; run \texttt{bootstrap\_pathb\_cluster}), an understatement of 30.8$\times$ in-window against 37$\times$ off-window. Those intervals are narrow because the scheme holds the design fixed: it resamples loans within each stratum and preserves every stratum's count, so between-stratum composition variance is never drawn. Drawing the 130 strata themselves with replacement, at the off-window floor the paper headlines (run \texttt{bootstrap\_pathb\_cluster}, 200 replicates), widens the marginal's interval to $[+4.63, +6.92]$ points with a standard deviation of $0.60$ points---a width of $2.29$ points against the within-stratum scheme's $0.062$, so the committed scheme understated loan-sample uncertainty by roughly a factor of $37$. Two properties of that interval are not corrected for and should be stated: drawing strata with replacement makes the loan count itself random---it runs from 54,134 to 101,404 across replicates against a fixed-design 75,000---so every composition weight is a ratio of random totals rather than of fixed ones, and the percentile interval carries no correction for the resulting ratio bias. A fixed-$n$ variant that rescales weights to the design total would settle how much of the width is that bias; it is not run here. The committed point marginal falls inside that interval. Two limits on the wider figure: the strata are very unequal, so the effective cluster count is $25.8$ rather than $130$ (the largest single stratum is $9.77\%$ of sample balance) and a percentile interval on $26$ effective clusters is itself noisy; and the resampled loan count varies from roughly $54{,}000$ to $101{,}000$, which is harmless here only because the engine renormalises the pool to Fed holdings each month, so the level is set exogenously and only composition varies. Even at the wider figure the sampling layer is less than half the width of the floor-read interval of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}, so the operative uncertainty statements for Path B's level and marginal remain the calibration box and floor-form results there rather than sampling intervals; the shared-basis equivalents follow by the common 9.1-point netting, which cancels from the marginal entirely.
