The peak alignment does \emph{not} show the simulated path leading SOMA cash flow. The empirical path moves first: a settlement-delay mechanism, in which loan-level prepayment decisions show up in SOMA cash two to three months later, predicts the \emph{opposite} direction of offset. I therefore attach no settlement interpretation to the timing alignment. The direct input-timing diagnosis behind that reading is in Appendix~\ref{app:patha-detail}.

\subsection{External Replication on Fannie Mae Loan-Level Data}\label{sec:pathb-fannie}

Beyond the Freddie Mac sample on which Path B is built, the same construction admits a direct external replication on a second agency's loan book. I re-ran the full Path B construction on Fannie Mae's single-family loan performance data (24 acquisition quarters, 2017Q1--2022Q4; 17.6 million loans and 826 million loan-months, staged into the identical origination/performance layout so that the panel builder, the estimator, and the stratified 75{,}000-loan sampling design run unchanged on the same 2017--2021 origination universe) under a spec fixed and committed before the run: zero-balance code 01 maps to voluntary prepayment as for Freddie; codes 06 and 16 (non-credit removals with no Freddie counterpart) are censored, with a worst-case bracket of 0.38\% of removals if every such removal were instead counted as a prepayment; and the pre-committed acceptance gate required the Fannie lock-in marginal to be positive and inside the Freddie floor/band envelope of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}. The result replicates: central \$821.0 billion (107.4\% of benchmark), $\beta_1 = 0$ null \$754.6 billion (98.7\%), lock-in marginal $+\$66.4$ billion ($+8.68$ points of benchmark) against Freddie's $+\$70.3$ billion ($+9.20$ points): inside the envelope, within half a point of the Freddie estimate. The envelope was the calibration box of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}, which spans 2.11 to 13.17 points---an 11.06-point window around a 9.20-point Freddie estimate, admitting a replication 77\% below it or 43\% above. A gate that wide cannot fail against anything but a sign error, and I do not treat its passing as evidence. The agreement is a different matter: 17.6 million staged Fannie loans, run through the same pipeline end to end, land 0.52 points from the Freddie point, which is a good deal more than the gate demanded and is the part of this exercise that is reassuring. What that agreement certifies needs the same care: every behavioral parameter is fixed a priori and identical across the two runs, and the two stratified samples are compositionally indistinguishable (population-stability indices at or below 0.018, Appendix~\ref{app:composition}), so the 0.52-point agreement certifies pipeline determinism and cross-agency compositional stability; the external-validity content lives in the sign reproduction and the staging inputs, not in the closeness itself. The Path A fixed-effects refit on the Fannie cohort panel reproduces the rate-gap coefficient's sign (its per-basis-point magnitude is about $3.6\times$ the Freddie fit's, and its holdout diagnostics carry the same no-forward-precision character as Section~\ref{sec:patha}'s, so it retains aggregate-corroboration status only). Two scope notes are part of the claim, both fixed ex ante: whether Freddie's code 01 absorbs exits that Fannie books under 06/16 is not settled by public documentation, so the replication claim is sign and envelope-conditional marginal consistency rather than a hazard-level match; and Fannie's terms prohibit redistribution, so the replication package ships the Fannie ingestion and replication code only, with no Fannie data and, conservatively, no derived cells; the committed artifact carries headline statistics alone.

\subsection{Path A: Stratum-Month Discrete-Time Hazard Model}\label{sec:patha}

Path A is the aggregate corroboration leg: a stratum-month discrete-time hazard fit to Freddie Mac loan-level performance data. Its aggregate recovery is \$928.9 billion, 121.5\% standalone --- reported as corroboration only and excluded from every headline figure --- and its estimation detail, diagnostics, and cross-correlation exhibits are collected in Appendix~\ref{app:patha-detail}.

The same caution applies to the paths themselves. Path B's $+0.190$ lag-0 levels correlation is carried by the window's shared trend: in first differences it is $-0.23$ and after linear detrending $-0.20$. The same constructions for the other estimators give $-0.20$ and $-0.30$ for the ABM (from $-0.32$ in levels) and $-0.21$ and $-0.29$ for Path A (from $-0.44$ in levels): detrending collapses the three estimators' apparent differences into a cluster between $-0.20$ and $-0.30$. Against the i.i.d.\ $1.96/\sqrt{n}$ reference ($\pm 0.30$ at $n = 42$ for the detrended series; $\pm 0.31$ at $n = 41$ for first differences), Path A ($-0.29$) and Path B ($-0.20$) are inside the band under both empirical-series conventions, while the ABM's detrended value straddles the band's edge across them ($-0.301$ common-series, $-0.303$ against its own back-out). Indistinguishability verdicts therefore rest on the wider moving-block bands of Table~\ref{tab:estimators}, under which none of the three is distinguishable from zero. Once the shared trend is removed, no estimator's contemporaneous co-movement is positive; Path B's distinction is the accuracy of its aggregate level, not its timing. (In levels, which are trend-contaminated over the window and reported only for completeness, the simulated path co-moves most strongly with the rate from four months earlier while the empirical path is contemporaneous.) No single channel carries the offset: the $\beta_B = 0$ and $\beta_1 = 0$ ablations both leave the peak at $-3$, and re-running the simulation with the rate input shifted by one to three months in either direction never moves the peak to lag 0 (it reaches $-1$ under a two-to-three-month input lead) while moving recovered trapped liquidity by less than \$1.5 billion across the entire scan. The offset is therefore a property of the fitted path's smooth aggregate dynamics set against a seasonal empirical series (mechanism not isolated), and Path A's seasonal re-estimation moving its own peak from $-3$ to $-2$ (Section~\ref{sec:patha}) is consistent with seasonality carrying part of it. Figure~\ref{fig:ccf} plots the full cross-correlation functions for all three estimators. Extending this input-timing diagnosis to the cohort level confirms the residual is structural rather than compositional: across the six 30-year coupon cohorts spanning the SOMA low-coupon mass (2.0--4.5\%), every simulated cohort CPR tracks the contemporaneous rate change with near-mechanical strength (first-difference correlation between $-0.87$ and $-0.94$; five of six cohorts cross-correlation phase-locked at lag~0, the 4.5\% coupon peaking at lag $-6$ with a weaker $r=-0.29$, and reproducing the aggregate $-0.318$), whereas no realized cohort speed, measured independently in the Freddie 2017--2021 performance panel and in the Fannie replication cohorts, shows a rate response distinguishable from zero at any lag within $\pm 6$ months (Freddie lag-0 first-difference correlations of $-0.05$ to $-0.16$, Fannie $-0.03$ to $-0.14$, all inside the $\pm 2/\sqrt{n}$ band, and a maximum magnitude of $0.34$ over all cohorts and all lags, with no coherent sign or lag). The aggregate empirical non-response is not a composition artifact, and the simulated lead is not a datable phase shift at the cohort level. One honest scope limit travels with this exhibit: realized cohort-level monthly speeds exist only in the loan-level agency panels that anchor Path B, not in the SOMA holdings from which the aggregate empirical CPR is backed out, so the cohort split is run in the Path B universe rather than on the SOMA book itself, which is why the estimator-level timing falsification above stays at the SOMA aggregate. A cumulative counterpart completes this exhibit (run \texttt{episode\_confrontation}). Because monthly differences are seasonality-dominated, the cross-section is also read in cumulated levels: over the window, the age-matched Freddie panel's realized prepayment runs from 9.44\% annualized CPR (28.1\% of balance cumulatively) in the shallowest surviving gap bucket ($[-1,0)$ points, mean coupon 6.02\%) down to 5.24\% (16.4\%) at gaps of $-3$ points and deeper (mean coupon 3.37\%): a gradient of $+4.20$ CPR points, with a stratum-cluster 95\% interval of $[+3.59, +4.66]$ and a one-sided permutation $p$ of 0.005---the one realized-data exhibit in this paper that moves in the lock-in direction. It is not corroboration of the imported elasticity's magnitude: the production hazard's implied gradient across the same buckets is $+0.94$ points at the central $\beta_1$, so the realized gradient is roughly $4.5\times$ the model's, and the excess carries everything else that travels with coupon across cohorts---credit composition, within-window refinancing on the high-coupon shallow buckets, residual seasoning past the age cut. Detection power for the implied gradient is 0.68: the design could have resolved $\beta_1$'s implication, and what it found instead was larger and confounded. The composition limb of that confound has now been tested rather than asserted: standardizing the deep bucket to the shallow bucket's vintage $\times$ FICO $\times$ LTV composition leaves the gradient at $+3.44$ points (95\% CI $[+1.17, +5.14]$, imputed weight share $0.3\%$), and an exact decomposition attributes $+0.77$ of the raw $+4.20$ to composition and $+3.43$ to differences within common cells (run \texttt{episode\_confrontation\_within}) --- against the production hazard's implied $+0.94$, the excess is not composition in the dimensions the panel can hold fixed. I therefore read the cross-section as a signed, significant level gradient whose composition component is measured and small, consistent with lock-in operating but still unusable as a measurement of its size: what remains unresolved is within-window refinancing on the high-coupon shallow buckets, which no field in this design separates from moving, and a cross-sectional level gradient is not a dollar marginal. The monthly-timing nulls above stand unchanged.

\subsection{Identification: the Marginal over the Null}\label{sec:identification}

The design does not identify the aggregate recovery level: the level is floor-dominated, and the $\beta_1 = 0$ null (the elasticity switched off, baseline turnover held at its observed level) already recovers 85.7\% of the benchmark on the shared accounting basis at the off-window floor this paper headlines, and 88.7\% at the in-sample calibration point (Section~\ref{sec:hazard-interp}; standalone figures in Table~\ref{tab:bases}). What it identifies is the \emph{marginal} of the central Path B run over that null, writing $R$ for recovered trapped liquidity,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:marginal}
\text{lock-in marginal} \;=\; R_{\text{central}} - R_{\beta_1 = 0},
\end{equation}
which is basis-invariant because the shared-accounting netting is common to both legs (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-hybrid}). Its value moves with the baseline turnover floor, and because that floor is the design's dominant level-setter and is calibrated in-window on the very cohorts the marginal decomposes, the headline value is the one obtained with the floor measured off that population: $+\$42.6$ billion, $+5.6$ points of the benchmark, at the defensible off-window floor of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}, and $+4.3$ to $+6.8$ points across that floor's range. At the in-window production calibration the same difference is $+\$70.3$ billion, or $+9.2$ points. The recovery \emph{level} moves with the floor for the same reason the marginal does, and I now report it at the same two calibrations rather than quoting a level from one floor beside a marginal from another: the central leg recovers 91.3\% of the benchmark on the shared basis at the off-window floor (88.2--93.7\% across its range) against 97.9\% at the in-window calibration, and the null 85.7\% against 88.7\%. Only the central and null legs were re-run off window, so those are the two levels quoted at both calibrations; the concave-gap variant was also re-run there, at 99.8\% central against a 94.8\% null on the standalone scorer; every remaining estimator level in this paper---Path~A, the full-book variant, the composed estimator, the covariate-prior and elasticity bands, the Ginnie overlay and the Danish legs---was run only in window, and any level appearing without a floor label should be read at the in-window 4.0\% calibration. I call the in-window configuration the \emph{in-sample calibration point} throughout. Differencing does not, however, immunize the marginal against non-stationary baselines: because the baseline multiplies the very rate-gap channel the null switches off, a structural break in $h_0$ scales the marginal rather than cancelling from it. Re-running both legs under a $\pm 20\%$ multiplicative break from January 2023 onward, imposed identically on both legs and fitting nothing to the benchmark, carries the marginal from $+5.0$ to $+12.1$ points around the committed $+9.2$ (reproduced bit-exactly at the unit factor), while a $\pm 20\%$ break in the turnover floor alone moves it the other way, to $+6.6$ through $+10.4$ points. Every perturbation leaves the marginal inside the calibration box and positive. What survives a baseline break is the box membership, not the point value.

Three qualifications fix its evidential content. First, the identified content is the marginal's \emph{bounded range}, and neither its point magnitude nor its sign: the box spans $+2.1$ to $+13.2$ points, and across the empirically plausible floor range the marginal spans $+5.0$ to $+12.6$ points over the full band, or $+10.8$ to $+5.5$ points at the central elasticity, moving to $+11.25$ points under the competing-risks floor form. The share of the sample the floor censors moves with its level: at the 4.991\% off-window floor the paper headlines, the central leg's hazard is pinned to the turnover floor in 68.8\% of the 1,683,124 evaluated loan-months, against 36.3\% at the in-sample calibration point and 60.0\% to 77.3\% across that floor's defensible range. In most evaluated loan-months at the headline calibration the elasticity therefore no longer sets the hazard, and its contribution to the marginal there is truncated at the floor rather than carried in full; these are unweighted loan-month counts, not balance-weighted shares, so they do not say what fraction of the dollar marginal the uncensored months carry. The $\beta_1 = 0$ leg is pinned less often---35.8\% of loan-months at the headline floor and 14.3\% at the in-sample calibration point---and wherever it is pinned, switching the elasticity off cannot raise the hazard at all. The marginal is thus floor-level sensitive under the production max form, while the form-robust statement is roughly $+9$ to $+11$ points at the production floor (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}); no form-robust point exists at the off-window anchors, where the two forms return $+5.6$ and $+11.2$, so the defensible statement there is the form-conditional range $+3.5$ to $+13.1$ points, whose endpoints are now the endpoints of a measured mixture curve in the floor's strictly-involuntary share: the curve rises to $+9.7$ points by $s = 0.25$ and plateaus near $+11.2$ (run \texttt{floor\_form\_mixture}), so the paper's own floor semantics point at the hull's upper region, and the $s = 0$ headline is the curve's conservative endpoint. Its upper end comes from the additive form at the 4.695\% floor and the band's 7.7\% high edge, whose central leg recovers 61.2\% of the benchmark, and Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor} states that it should not be read as an equally credentialed member of the range. That hull is conditional on each floor read being taken at its point estimate, and it is therefore not the paper's widest disclosed interval: propagating the floor reads' own cluster-bootstrap sampling error puts the max-form member at $+2.9$ to $+8.7$ points after wild-cluster correction (the percentile read is $+3.0$ to $+8.0$; Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}) and leaves it open below $+4.3$ on an age-standardized read (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}). The floor read and the loan/stratum resample are reported side by side rather than maximized over: convolved as independent draws the pair spans $+2.8$ to $+9.0$ points (run \texttt{layer\_convolution}), which is the interval a reader who wants one number for sampling error should use, and which I do not substitute for the floor read because independence between a 2018 cohort-month ratio and a window-period composition resample is an assumption rather than a measurement. The hull's lower edge is thus narrower than the layer the paper calls binding, and the two must be quoted together rather than the hull alone.

The floor's provenance now has an out-of-agency read as well as an out-of-window one, and it does not narrow the range. Applying the committed off-window selection rule unchanged to the Fannie Mae cohort-month panel---the same fourteen columns, the same 2017 start, staged for the external replication of Section~\ref{sec:pathb-fannie}---returns 5.52\% on the headline cell against Freddie's 5.19\%, on 421 cohort-months against 438 (run \texttt{fannie\_floor\_read}; the Freddie panel replays every committed cell of that grid through the identical code path first). That is above the clean band's 5.334\% top, but it is not a second read of the clean leg: the committed rule applied off window covers the pooled 2017--2019 period, whose Freddie counterpart is the 5.19\% just quoted rather than the 2018 leg's 4.991\%, so the agency difference is 0.337 points of CPR (5.522\% against 5.185\%) and the rest of the distance from the clean read is period. The age-standardized read of 5.51\% that the same table already flags lands 0.01 points away, but it perturbs something else again---an age correction to the 2018 leg itself, on 84.0\% imputed weight---so the near-coincidence of the two is not a second measurement of either. What they share is direction: both sit above the clean band's top, and both say the clean read understates the mature book's turnover. That direction is what the clean leg cannot test on its own. The 2018 leg holds no out-of-the-money cohort-month past the twenty-four-month age cut, so its mature-seasoning test is not computable rather than passed (Appendix~\ref{app:floormech}), and the one age contrast it does support is confounded by the PSA ramp. Where a mature contrast is measurable at all---two agencies, two rate regimes, three depth cuts---it runs one way in twelve cells of twelve, the age-$\geq$-24 read the higher one in each: in window, where the rate configuration suppresses refinancing as it did on the 2018 leg, by 0.08 and 0.19 points of CPR on Freddie and Fannie; on the refi-contaminated pooled 2017--2019 period, by 3.74 and 4.61 points, which bounds the correction from above rather than measuring it (run \texttt{fannie\_floor\_read}). A floor read taken entirely on cohort-months aged twelve to twenty-four therefore sits below the mature book's baseline turnover, and the marginal it implies sits above the one a mature book would give: the error is signed against the headline, and its size is not priced anywhere in this paper. Read against the committed floor-to-marginal grid, a 5.52\% floor brackets the marginal between the 5.334\% row's $+4.27$ points and the 6.0\% row's $+2.28$, that is, below the $+4.3$ lower edge this paper quotes. I do not restate the headline on the Fannie floor, because the production calibration is the Freddie one and the two books differ in composition as well as in this read; what the out-of-agency read establishes is that the band's \emph{lower} edge is the soft one, which is the direction the age-standardized caveat already pointed.

Because that sensitivity is what makes the floor's provenance decisive, Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor} re-measures the floor outside the evaluation window and re-runs both legs there: the marginal lands at $+4.3$ to $+6.8$ points across the defensible off-window range, inside the box and well clear of either edge, and stays positive at all twenty-seven floor-by-band cells run. The covariate-prior swing of Section~\ref{sec:pathb} is the design's largest single level sensitivity at both calibrations, and at the headline floor it exceeds the marginal itself: re-run at the 4.991\% floor, the two covariate endpoints span 8.2 points of recovery against the $+5.6$-point marginal (run \texttt{covariate\_ablation\_offwindow}). The marginal is the counter-fact: switching the covariate block between its endpoints moves the off-window marginal by only $+0.1$ points, against $+1.9$ in-window, because both legs shift together where the floor censors more.

That the marginal is positive in all twenty-seven cells needs a word of its own, because it reads as the robust identified content, and is not. Over the forty window months the cohort panel covers (June 2022 through September 2025; the panel ends two months before the window does) the exposure-weighted mean coupon on the surviving book is 3.134\%, with 97.40\% of exposure at or below 4.79\% and 99.53\% at or below 5.09\%, against a window-minimum 30-year rate of 5.2311\% (the August 2022 monthly mean --- the window's rate dip --- not a June 2022 figure; \texttt{sign\_forcing\_stats}). The two uncovered months cannot overturn this: the book is closed at 2017--2021 originations, so no higher-coupon loan can enter it, and the out-of-the-money share can only hold or rise as the low-coupon cohorts season. Essentially the entire book is out of the money for essentially the entire window, so the rate gap is negative in every cell of every sweep reported here, and a specification in which a more negative gap suppresses prepayment cannot return a negative marginal at any floor or band in the box. The sign is fixed by the rate configuration rather than by the estimated elasticity. The twenty-seven-cell check accordingly verifies the specification---it would catch a wiring error that flipped a coefficient or crossed the central and null legs, and that is worth having---rather than identifying the mechanism, and I retain it on that footing. This is why the reported range, and not the sign, carries the paper's identified content. The band's monotone response is forced the same way: \eqref{eq:beta1} is monotone in $\delta$ and the hazard is monotone in $\beta_1$ at that gap, so the band's ordering is a composition of monotone maps. I retain that ordering as a wiring check on the same footing, not as identified content; the sweep's endpoints stay the convention range they are.

Second, timing carries no credential: the $\beta_1 = 0$ null also peaks at lag $-3$ (Section~\ref{sec:hazard-interp}), so the lead cannot discriminate the elasticity from the mechanical model. Third, the marginal's sampling precision is not the operative uncertainty, and the premise is now the cluster scheme's rather than the retracted one's: the stratum-cluster bootstrap puts the paired marginal at $[+8.27, +10.19]$ points at this calibration (the within-stratum $[+9.17, +9.23]$ understates by 30.8$\times$; Section~\ref{sec:pathb}), and the calibration box, at 11.1 points of width against the cluster read's 1.9, still governs the interval.

A fourth qualification concerns composition. This subsection's decomposition exhibits are computed at the in-sample calibration point, so the total they partition is the $+\$70.3$ billion figure; the composition findings are shares and intensities, which the floor's level shifts far less than it shifts the total. A group-ablation decomposition of the marginal itself (zeroing $\beta_1$ cell by cell over a vintage $\times$ coupon grid, with everything else held at production) shows the $+\$70.3$ billion is broad-based rather than cohort-specific: all fifteen cells contribute positively, the cells sum to within 1.03\% of the paired-run total, no single cell contributes more than 21\%, and the 2020--2021 vintages carry 72\% of the marginal on 73\% of sample balance, with per-balance intensity ranging from $0.82\times$ to $1.75\times$. Extending the same group-ablation engine to the dimensions that grid does not cover, the marginal is broad-based on all three: every cell of the FICO ($<$680 / 680--740 / 740$+$), LTV ($\leq$80 / $>$80), and Census-region partitions contributes positively, the per-grid cell sums reproduce the $+\$70.3$ billion total within interaction residuals of 0.8, 0.4, and 0.5 percent, and every cell's per-balance intensity lies in 0.88--1.26, tighter than the vintage $\times$ coupon grid's span. That verdict does not, on its own, discriminate, and I say so rather than leave the reader to assume otherwise: running the identical engine and the identical pre-committed decision rule over twelve random three-way partitions of the same 75,000 loans returns \emph{broad-based} in all twelve, on per-balance intensities of 0.995--1.007 and additivity residuals of 0.49 percent (\texttt{subgroup\_marginals\_placebo}). A partition with no economic content earns the label, so the label alone carries no evidential weight. What the comparison does establish runs in the opposite direction. The random partitions' intensities concentrate on unity --- the widest of the twelve spans 0.011 --- while the economic groupings span 0.381, more than thirty times wider. The informative statement is therefore not that these cells are homogeneous, which chance alone delivers, but that they disperse considerably more than chance: the tilts below are structure, not sampling noise, and the generality claim rests on the sign homogeneity and on the bounded magnitude of that dispersion rather than on the verdict label. The mild tilts run toward below-740-FICO ($1.23\times$--$1.26\times$) and above-80-LTV ($1.23\times$) borrowers, with regional intensities spanning only 0.93--1.06. The marginal is a book-wide property of the rate-gap distribution interacting with seasoning and the floor; Figure~\ref{fig:marginalcells} maps the cells. The benchmark-basis question is likewise answered by direct computation: Section~\ref{sec:method-benchmark}'s expectations-based complement shows the Federal Reserve's own ex-ante projection already implied 75.6--88.5\% of the realized cap-shortfall. The 0.2-point distance between the 88.5\% upper end and the null's 88.7\% benchmark-consistent recovery is not corroboration: the coincidence is doubly conditional, holding only at the in-sample 4.0\% floor \emph{and} under the uniform-spread allocation, which Section~\ref{sec:method-benchmark} itself judges the less faithful of the two constructions. At the headline off-window floor the null recovers 85.7\%, 2.8 points \emph{below} the uniform-spread projection rather than 0.2 above it; against the settlement-aware allocation's 75.6\% the null sits 13.2 points above in-sample and 10.1 points above at the headline floor. Two quantities that move by several points under either condition cannot be reported as converging, so I report the projection range and the null separately and draw no agreement claim from their proximity. Every percentage-point magnitude quoted for this decomposition is a cap-basis quantity, with dollars and rankings basis-invariant.

The monthly-path and coupon-bucket timing exhibits are collected in Appendix~\ref{app:patha-detail}. Because neither simulated leg carries calendar-month seasonality, seasonal structure enters these exhibits only through the empirical comparison, and per the pre-committed posture of Section~\ref{sec:hazard-interp} they are fit diagnostics, not timing credentials.

A fifth qualification states what kind of claim ``identifies'' makes. The marginal is a within-model counterfactual decomposition: the difference between two runs of a fully disclosed hazard structure that differ only in the elasticity term. It is not causal identification in the econometric sense: no quasi-experimental variation in rate gaps across otherwise-comparable loans is exploited anywhere in this paper, and none is claimed. The causal content of $\beta_1$ itself is inherited rather than asserted: the elasticity is imported unmodified from \citepos{liebersohn2024} estimated band, with \citet{fonseca2024} and the transaction-record band corroboration of \citet{graybill2026} as independent support, and no Path B parameter is estimated on the benchmark (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}). What the switch-off adds is accounting, at a stated price: the marginal inherits any misspecification of the held-fixed structure, which is why the channels I can name (floor level and functional form, the rate-gap transform, the covariate priors) are each swept directly (Sections~\ref{sec:pathb} and \ref{sec:robustness-floor}), and why Table~\ref{tab:uncertainty} reports calibration, not sampling, as the operative uncertainty.

A sixth qualification concerns three readings I decline in three separate places. Path A's fitted rate-gap coefficient is $+0.29$ per 100 bp against the imported $\beta_1$'s $0.069$, though the two are on different scales and I compare them on sign rather than magnitude (Section~\ref{sec:patha}); the realized cross-cohort gap gradient runs at roughly $4.5\times$ the gradient the central $\beta_1$ implies (run \texttt{episode\_confrontation}); and the real-covariate cross-design variant recovers 59.3\% of the benchmark, 76.3\% once reweighted to the book's own composition, against the production ABM's 13.6\% seed mean (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign}). I stand by each reason for declining: the first is not unit-comparable and does not survive a bias-respecting sign test; the second survives composition standardization ($+3.44$ against the implied $+0.94$, run \texttt{episode\_confrontation\_within}) and is declined on the narrower ground that remains --- within-window refinancing on the shallow buckets is inseparable from moving, and a level gradient is not a dollar marginal; the third is a recovery level, which this design does not identify, and the same reweight moves the frozen cross-design variant down to 12.6\%. Stated together rather than three sections apart: none of the three points toward a smaller lock-in channel. I keep the imported value for the reason Section~\ref{sec:pathb} gives---Path B is a structural verification exercise whose elasticity is estimated in a different setting and never touches this benchmark, so re-fitting it to any in-sample reading would end that exercise---and among the literature estimates available the adopted band is already the conservative one, since \citepos{fonseca2024} anchor puts the marginal at $+11.5$ points against the central calibration's $+9.2$ at the production floor. Other disclosed variants move the marginal the other way, the Ginnie-speed overlay to $+4.4$ points and the age-standardized floor read to $+3.8$ (Table~\ref{tab:uncertainty}), so I do not read the three as a case for a larger headline.

A seventh qualification assembles those readings against the ones that run the other way, because this paper states each correction where it arises and never lines them up, and a reader who lines them up sees something the sections do not show one at a time. Holding the production hard-maximum form and the imported elasticity fixed, every correction to the floor or to the accounting basis moves the headline down. The in-sample calibration returns $+9.2$ points; re-anchoring the floor outside the window returns the headline $+5.6$; the Ginnie-speed overlay at that same floor returns $+4.4$; the age-standardized floor read of 5.51\% implies a marginal near $+3.8$; and the Fannie Mae read of the same selection rule on the pooled off-window period, 5.52\%, brackets the marginal below the $+4.3$ edge of the clean band (above), its like-for-like Freddie counterpart 5.19\% rather than the 2018 leg's 4.991\%. The anchor convention does not flatter the headline: extending the depth ladder to the five reads the 10\% support rule admits puts the middle of them at 4.869\%, below the adopted 4.991\%, and a lower floor implies a marginal above the headline rather than below it (run \texttt{matched\_depth\_reconciliation}). Carrying the overlay's $0.797$ scaling up to the age-standardized floor projected a marginal near $+3.0$; the joint cell has now been run under a pre-committed landing rule (run \texttt{b5\_joint\_cell}) and measures $+2.9$ points, an interaction within $0.1$ of proportional --- where the first joint cell this paper ran for two adjustments to the marginal returned an interaction of $-1.47$ points (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}): corrections here do not compose additively, and a composed figure would carry a precision nothing in the design supports. Nor are they independent of each other. The floor-level corrections and the censoring share are one mechanism read twice, since raising the floor is what crowds the elasticity out: at the 4.991\% headline floor the central leg is already pinned to the floor in 68.8\% of evaluated loan-months, against 36.3\% at the in-sample calibration point, so a further increase in the floor has progressively less of the hazard left to act on. That share is not a statement that the estimate rests on a third of the data, and it should not be read as one: a pinned month's contribution to the marginal is truncated at the floor rather than erased, the $\beta_1 = 0$ leg is pinned in only 35.8\% of loan-months at the same floor so the two legs still separate wherever the null clears it, and these are unweighted loan-month counts rather than balance-weighted shares, so nothing follows from them about what fraction of the dollar marginal the uncensored months carry. What the assembly settles is where in the interval the mass sits, not whether the finding holds. Within the production form the widest disclosed layer is the baseline level: sweeping the seasoning ramp over 75 to 150 PSA on both legs carries the marginal from $+0.9$ to $+15.6$ points at the off-window floor and from $+3.3$ to $+16.5$ at the in-sample calibration (run \texttt{psa\_level\_sweep}), a span with no coverage property --- it is a range over a convention I did not estimate, and its endpoints are not draws from anything. Aggregate fit is not available as a defense of the 100~PSA convention: at the off-window floor the $\beta_1 = 0$ null recovers 101.9\% of the benchmark under the standalone scorer at 75~PSA against 94.8\% at the production 100, while the central leg's recovery is closer to the benchmark at 100 (100.4\%) than at 75, so the two legs rank the ramps opposite ways. A conditional estimate of the ramp's \emph{shape} now exists, and it does not favour the headline. Transporting the age profile of Path~A's own estimated seasoning spline onto $h_0$---level held fixed by construction, because the spline's level is absorbed by its fitted constant and the stratum fixed effects and is not separately identified---returns a median marginal of $+9.44$ points, with a 5th-to-95th-percentile spread of $+8.99$ to $+9.81$ across the 107 of 200 bootstrap replicates whose normalised profile stays inside the usable range (run \texttt{h0\_reanchor}). Every one of those replicates lies above the $+5.6$ this paper headlines, and all but one lies above the $+8.7$ upper edge of the binding interval quoted below, though all of them lie inside the $75$--$150$~PSA span just given. With 46.5\% of replicates discarded it is a conditional estimate and not the ramp's sampling interval, so the convention span above stays labelled unestimated. I had pre-committed to the opposite sign: the prediction recorded before the run was that re-anchoring would push more loan-months onto the floor and so \emph{lower} the marginal. The bind share did rise as predicted, from 35.8\% to between 50.4\% and 58.2\% on the null leg, and the marginal rose anyway---a failed prediction, reported as one. The floor read itself transports in two directions, and both were fixed before either ran (run \texttt{floor\_transport\_two\_sided}). Standardizing its calendar raises it: the 2018 leg is read in August--December, on the declining side of the committed seasonal profile, so re-weighting to the window's own calendar mix carries 4.991\% to 5.2156\% and the marginal to $+4.70$ points. Matching its housing-activity level lowers it: active listings averaged 1.27 million over those 2018 months against 0.80 million over the window, so the same multiplicative transport carries the floor to 3.1533\% and the marginal to $+10.69$. I report the two at their own floors and do not net them---two floor-side adjustments composed with an interaction of $-1.47$ points the one time this paper ran them jointly, so a cancellation would be a finding requiring its own cell rather than an arithmetic consequence of these two. The activity leg is a listings-based proxy for an argument about existing-home \emph{sales}, which is not constructible here: the sales series carries no observations over the 2018 leg months at all. Its magnitude also inherits a one-for-one mapping from activity level to turnover level that nothing here estimates; the sign is what it establishes. Post-stratifying the draw onto the book's composition moves the marginal, and by less than the paper's own share arithmetic implies. Raking the pool's balance weights to the SOMA coupon marginal and to the book's vintage mix within the 2017--2021 support the draw has---both margins hit to better than $10^{-10}$ (run \texttt{book\_composition\_marginal})---returns $+5.11$ points against the unweighted $+5.57$, a move of $-0.47$. The vintage margin alone returns $+4.67$, so the coupon margin offsets about half of the vintage correction rather than compounding it, and the vintage overlay's $+3.7$, which is pure share scaling, overstates the composition correction by roughly a factor of four. Two limits travel with the number. The book's coupon-by-vintage \emph{joint} distribution is not observable---the SOMA cohort tabulation collapses vintage to a single value-weighted origination date per coupon bucket---so this matches two margins and not a cell grid. And the pre-2017 and 2022 vintages, 33.7\% of book face, have no support in the draw at all and are not re-weighted here; they remain covered by the vintage overlay. I pre-committed both a direction and a band: the direction held and the marginal fell, while $+5.11$ lies just outside the $[+2.5, +5.0]$ band I fixed before the run, which I report rather than re-draw. The widest layer that does have a coverage property is the floor reads' own sampling error, $+2.9$ to $+8.7$ points after wild-cluster correction of the percentile read's under-coverage (run \texttt{floor\_inference\_correction}; the percentile read is $+3.0$ to $+8.0$), and that is the interval this paper quotes as binding; the headline sits just below that interval's midpoint, while every correction listed above falls in its lower half and the uncomposed pair falls near its bottom. The convention span and the sampling interval are nested rather than alternative: the seasoning-ramp sweep is the outer statement, and $+2.9$ to $+8.7$ is a sampling interval on the floor read alone, nested inside it at the production 100~PSA ramp. Which rung of that inner layer to quote is a further choice the rule does not settle: the Webb wild-$t$ read quoted here is the third-narrowest of the nine undemoted rungs, and the widest rung with both endpoints interior to the floor grid---CR2 at Bell--McCaffrey degrees of freedom, $+2.4$ to $+9.1$ points---stands beside it rather than behind it. I attach no posture to where $+5.6$ sits among these readings: with the downward specification variants lined up below beside the upward counterweights, no interior member is privileged, and the range rather than any point is what the design delivers---with the form-conditional hull of $+3.5$ to $+13.1$ points remaining the wider statement, since the form dimension is not a floor correction and is not disciplined by this list. Three things stop the list from becoming a retraction. The sign is fixed by the window's rate configuration rather than by any of these calibrations, as the sign-forcing argument above establishes, so nothing here bears on the direction of the result; the interval's exclusion of zero is deliberately not among the three, because a forced sign makes zero-exclusion a restatement of that configuration rather than independent evidence for it. Specification choices that are not floor corrections run both ways, and this assembly now lines both directions up. Upward: the additive form returns $+11.2$ points at the same off-window anchors, nearly floor-invariantly, and it is what sets the hull's upper end, while \citepos{fonseca2024} anchor would put the marginal at $+11.5$ points at the production floor against the adopted band's $+9.2$. Downward: the concave transform alone returns $+5.06$ at the off-window point floor against the production $+5.57$ (run \texttt{concave\_marginal}), the sub-band elasticity at $\delta = 3.25\%$ returns $+3.47$ (Table~\ref{tab:lowband}), and the moving-share bracket re-run at the headline floor returns $+3.36$ points at $s = 0.5$ and $+1.82$ at $s = 0.25$ against $+5.57$ at $s = 1$ (run \texttt{moving\_share\_bracket\_offwindow}) --- a convention bracket rather than an estimate, and mildly super-proportional because censored contributions do not scale with $s$. The survival-selection attenuation of the same imported coefficient is a bracket of the same kind and belongs on this side: $a = S^{\theta}$ at the measured pre-window survival share carries the marginal from $+5.1$ at $\theta = 0.25$ to $+3.7$ at $\theta = 1$ (run \texttt{attenuation\_sensitivity}), with $\theta$ free (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}) --- a different axis from the elasticity band, not a widening of it. The assembly is not exhaustive over runs that score a marginal at a labelled floor: the baseline-level PSA sweep, the covariate ablation and the floor-form mixture do so as well and are reported at their own sites rather than as rows here, and I state no rule that would admit these rows while excluding those. A calibrated companion prices the omitted housing-activity level term (Table~\ref{tab:specbox}) from the paper's own catalogued anchor: \citet{aladangady2024} attribute 44\% of the 2021--2022 mobility decline to rate-gap lock-in, and scaling the seasoning baseline in both legs until the non-rate channel traps $0.56/0.44$ times the rate channel's trapped liquidity puts the root at $\phi^{*} = 0.754$ --- effectively 75.4 PSA --- and the marginal at $+0.9$ points at the headline floor and $+3.5$ at the in-sample calibration (run \texttt{scaled\_null\_housing\_activity}; the $\phi = 1$ cells reproduce the committed values bit-exactly). The variant documents an upward bias; the corrected member lies below the headline, at the bottom of the seasoning-ramp span just swept, and at the headline floor it falls outside the quoted $+2.9$ to $+8.7$ interval---an externally anchored calibration the quoted interval does not cover, not a challenge to the sign; at the in-sample calibration it falls inside. That the floor side runs one way is also an absence: the floor is a level of total turnover rather than of a strictly involuntary component of it (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}), so it carries the activity of the period it is read on, and re-reading it at the window's own level enters at the floor rather than at the seasoning ramp just scaled, so it cuts the other way --- a lower anchor raises the marginal. It is the one floor-side correction I can name that would move the headline up, and it turns on whether the window's turnover level is the lower of the two, which I do not measure here. And the three readings of the preceding paragraph point to a larger lock-in channel, not a smaller one. I decline those three for the reasons given there, and I decline to compose these for the reason given here: the identified content is the range; the floor and basis corrections concentrate in its lower half, and the form-side counterweights sit above it. Table~\ref{tab:assembly} tabulates this assembly.

The table row above reports the same floor's full-window marginal, so that every row is comparable; the two quantities are different objects and should not be read off one another. \quad b The 2018 leg's age-bucket reads reweighted to the in-window deep-discount population's age mix, its empty mature buckets imputed from the in-window age gradient: 84.0\% of the weight is imputed, so this is an indicative bound and not a measurement (run \texttt{floor\_uncertainty}). \quad c The committed off-window selection rule applied to the Fannie Mae panel at gap $\leq -0.0025$ over the pooled 2017--2019 period, not to the 2018 leg: the like-for-like Freddie read of that cell is 5.185\%, so the agency difference is 0.337 points of CPR and the rest of the distance from the 2018 leg's 4.991\% is period (run \texttt{fannie\_floor\_read}). \noindent{\footnotesize \emph{Notes to Table~\ref{tab:uncertainty}.} Seed noise: the marginal varies by less than \$0.01 billion across ten paired draw seeds (Section~\ref{sec:hazard-interp}). The Fannie Mae external replication reproduces the marginal at $+8.68$ points against Freddie's $+9.20$; it clears the pre-committed envelope, but that envelope is 11.06 points wide and the agreement, not the gate, is what carries the information (Section~\ref{sec:pathb-fannie}). \quad Both cluster bootstraps above rest on few clusters: 31 carry the floor read, and the loan/stratum scheme's 130 strata are unequal enough to be worth 25.8 effective ones. Percentile cluster bootstraps are known to under-cover at cluster counts in that range, and this rung's printed lower edge additionally turns on an unlabelled convention: 26 of its 1{,}000 draws fall outside the committed floor grid, all 26 above the 6.0\% edge and therefore all on the low-marginal side, and the row excludes them---truncating them to the edge instead would print $+2.28$ to $+8.01$ in place of $+3.0$ to $+8.0$; run \texttt{floor\_inference\_correction} and its Webb-weight successor \texttt{floor\_inference\_correction\_v2} correct the floor read, Table~\ref{tab:ladder} sets the corrected ladder out layer by layer with its degrees of freedom, and the headline row quotes the wild-$t$ interval as the binding layer among those with a coverage property. That interval prices one layer: the floor read's own sampling error around the 4.991\% point read, propagated through the frozen floor-to-marginal grid at the production hard-maximum form, the 100~PSA ramp and the central elasticity $\delta = 6.5\%$, so none of its width comes from the elasticity, which is swept separately in the calibration column and in Table~\ref{tab:lowband} --- swept over the published 5.5\%--7.7\% range as a convention, not estimated, so no layer of this paper's accounting prices sampling error in the elasticity itself. No such correction is run for the loan/stratum scheme, whose interval is still better read as a lower bound on sampling uncertainty than as calibrated 95\% coverage; one input to the convolved line being a lower bound makes the convolved line a lower bound as well. The ladder's reads agree in location and none crosses zero, and the closest agreement among them carries no information: the Rademacher and Webb constructions land with both endpoints within a tenth of a point, a re-printing rather than a substantive move, because both are sign-symmetric reweightings of the same residuals, so each bootstrap distribution turns on the draw's sign for the one cluster carrying a third of the total leverage---nearly twice the next largest (Table~\ref{tab:ladder}). Their coincidence re-prints that cluster's influence instead of testing it. The small-sample degrees of freedom and the restricted inversion are wider. The bootstrap imposes the point estimate as the data-generating truth over unrestricted residuals (B $= 9{,}999$), studentizes on the floor's own scale, and maps endpoints through the frozen floor-to-marginal grid. \quad The within-run floor dispersion range is the $\kappa$ grid of Section~\ref{sec:pathb}. It bounds the marginal's sensitivity to floor variation within the window; it does not bracket floor cyclicality, and should not be read as doing so. Scrambling the floor path's time order leaves 85.0\% of its span standing, and switching the combination rule from the hard maximum to the additive form eliminates 98.0\% of it (the $\kappa$ grid then spans $+11.22$ to $+11.27$ points). Appendix~\ref{sec:robustness-seasonalfloor} diagnoses the Jensen mechanism responsible. The co-movement component that does survive is monotone in $|\kappa|$ and sign-consistent, but reaches at most \$5.29 billion in any cell.\par}

\noindent{\footnotesize \emph{Notes to Table~\ref{tab:ladder}.} The Bell--McCaffrey degrees of freedom are data-driven and estimator-specific, so one value cannot serve all three cluster-robust variants: the 6.2 and 5.1 shown are CR1's and CR2's, and CR3's own is smaller still. CR1 applies no leverage adjustment --- it is the narrowest of the three sandwiches --- and is the standard error the wild rows studentize with, so its two rows are the ladder's baseline rather than competing reads; its Bell--McCaffrey row printing CR3's conventional-df interval is an accident of this leverage profile, not an identity. The effective cluster count is 5.9 by the same Herfindahl-inverse convention as the loan/stratum scheme's 25.8, against 31 nominal clusters, and the run's recorded maximum single-cluster leverage is 0.33, ten times an equal 31-cluster share. That concentration is why the wild-cluster correction is applied and the percentile read demoted. It does not rank the wild construction against the Bell--McCaffrey degrees of freedom, and I do not claim it does: the wild rows re-draw cluster-level weights at that same leverage profile, the Bell--McCaffrey rows keep the sandwich and correct the reference distribution's degrees of freedom for it, and at 5.9 effective clusters neither correction dominates. So the Webb row is the primary read and the interval this paper quotes, but it is the third-narrowest of the nine undemoted rungs, and the widest rung with both endpoints interior to the floor grid---CR2 at Bell--McCaffrey degrees of freedom, $+2.4$ to $+9.1$ points---belongs beside it rather than behind it. The two rungs printed wider still understate their own width: CR3 at Bell--McCaffrey degrees of freedom and the restricted inversion have \emph{lower} endpoints at floors above the committed grid's 6.0\% end and are censored to it, and the marginal falls as the floor rises, so both run below the $+2.3$ printed; their upper endpoints are interior.\par}

\noindent{\footnotesize \emph{Notes to Table~\ref{tab:estimators}.} The table reports the three primary estimators, the two Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign} cross-design variants, and the Fannie Mae external replication of Path B, whose path-correlation cell is dashed because the committed replication artifact carries headline statistics only (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}); the Danish counterfactual specifications are collected in Table~\ref{tab:danish}. Dashes denote quantities that are not applicable to the row. The benchmark row's ``30+15yr'' scope is not symmetric across estimators: 15-year holdings enter the empirical back-out and the ABM through real weights and real scheduled amortization, with voluntary CPR for those cohorts drawn from the same-coupon 30-year surface (the structural-only fold-in of Appendix~\ref{sec:robustness-pipeline}), while the hazard rows are 30-year-collateral estimates from which 15-year face is excluded (Table~\ref{tab:composition}, Term row). Peak-lag entries select the maximum-magnitude cross-correlation (max $|r|$) within a $\pm$3-month window; under a most-positive-$r$ convention the production ABM's best alignment is $+0.19$ at lag $-3$ (Section~\ref{sec:abm-results}); the two conventions summarize the same lag profile, and Path B's lag $-3$ peak satisfies both. \quad Path B is the only estimator whose aggregate recovery is close to the benchmark-consistent basis (97.9\%, and 100.0\% composed as committed --- 98.1\% converted to a single coupon convention --- at the in-sample calibration point; 91.3\% at the off-window floor the paper headlines). Its raw lag-0 correlation ($+0.190$) is carried by the window's shared trend: in first differences ($-0.23$) and after linear detrending ($-0.20$) the simulated--empirical co-movement is indistinguishable from zero ($\pm 0.31$ at $n = 41$), as it is for every estimator. The two cross-design rows substitute real Freddie Mac structural covariates into the ABM under two calibration choices; neither reproduces the empirical path's timing (no estimator does; see above), but the recalibrated variant's dollar recovery exceeds my own ex-ante threshold for undercutting the paradigm interpretation (Sections~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign} and~\ref{sec:conclusion}). Moving-block bootstrap 95\% intervals (block length 6): ABM lag-0 $[-0.60, +0.04]$; Path A lag-0 $[-0.68, -0.02]$; Path B lag-0 $[-0.13, +0.44]$, peak lag $[-0.21, +0.49]$ (no positive lag-0 coefficient is distinguishable from zero; block-length sensitivity at 4/6/8 changes no zero-inclusion verdict in this table except Path A's marginally negative levels endpoint, detailed in Section~\ref{sec:pathb}). Path A's contemporaneous levels correlation is negative on the levels construction, which strengthens the offsetting-errors reading of its aggregate fit (detrended, all three estimators cluster at $-0.20$ to $-0.30$; Section~\ref{sec:pathb}), and no timing evidence is claimed for any estimator. Table~\ref{tab:theil} decomposes each estimator's monthly error into bias, variance, and covariance shares and reports cumulative dollar-error profiles; it is a fit diagnostic and changes no verdict in this table. \quad Correlation-column convention: the ABM row's correlations are computed against the ABM pipeline's own empirical back-out (cohort-weighted, term-aware scheduled amortization), while the hazard rows use the hazard-side back-out; against the common hazard-side series the ABM lag-0 value is $-0.316$, a third-decimal, no-verdict difference. Section~\ref{sec:method-benchmark} notes the two constructions. \quad Shared-accounting-basis equivalents, together with the full-book and composed bases, are collected in Table~\ref{tab:bases} (basis defined in Section~\ref{sec:method-benchmark}, derived in Section~\ref{sec:robustness-hybrid}; the common curtailment netting is a flat 9.1pp for every U.S. hazard leg). Path A's point estimate carries no useful forward precision and is excluded from headline figures; its refit-and-resimulate interval (since rerun jointly at both specifications; the joint interval is floor-censored and reported beside, not in place of, the committed one), and the spec v3 provenance of every Path A inferential object, are reported in Section~\ref{sec:patha}.\par}

\noindent{\footnotesize \emph{Notes to Table~\ref{tab:bases}.} The book-WAC and composed columns are the committed mixed-basis measurements (note rates bucketed against pass-through coupons); converted to a single pass-through convention they read 107.2\% and 98.1\% for Path B central, with the identified marginal bit-invariant (run \texttt{coupon\_convention\_reweight}). \quad A prior fact frames the whole table. The engine renormalises the simulated pool to realized Fed holdings every month, so a leg's dollar roll-off is a \emph{rate} applied to an exogenous level and its balance path cannot drift from the realized one. No recovery percentage here is a balance-path fit, and none can compound error across months: each is close to a restatement of that leg's window-mean CPR against the empirical 5.14\%. That is why the sampling intervals are as tight as they are, and it is a limit on what any of these levels can corroborate. The final column is each leg's terminal cumulative runoff error, realized minus simulated roll-off (equivalently, simulated minus realized trapped liquidity), and it is the sign-transparent statement of the same information: recovery is an affine transform of it, $\text{recovery} = 1 + \text{error}/764.7$, so a large additive constant sits inside every percentage in the first four columns and makes all of them look better. Two things the percentages hide. Path~B's in-sample 107.0\% is an \emph{over}-prediction of trapped liquidity of \$53.8 billion, which is an 8.2\% \emph{under}-prediction of the \$652.8 billion of runoff actually realized---the referent flips even though the map does not. And the headline off-window central leg, at 91.3\% shared, carries the smallest error of any leg here on the standalone scorer: $+\$2.8$ billion, 0.4\% of realized runoff. Errors as a share of realized runoff: $+8.2\%$, $-2.5\%$, $+0.4\%$, $-6.1\%$, $+25.1\%$, $+6.7\%$ down the rows. The lock-in marginal is unaffected by any of this, being a difference of two trapped aggregates from which the constant cancels; that is the same fact as its basis-invariance. Standalone and shared are related by the flat 9.1-percentage-point curtailment wedge of Section~\ref{sec:method-benchmark} (\$69.56 billion over the window), derived in Section~\ref{sec:robustness-hybrid}. Path B's central elasticity band is 105.9--108.2\% standalone (96.8--99.1\% shared); the covariate-prior band is 92.2\%--99.2\% shared (approximately 94.3\%--101.3\% composed). The lock-in marginal is $+9.2$ points ($+\$70.3$ billion) on the U.S. leg at the in-sample calibration point, and $+5.6$ points ($+\$42.6$ billion) at the off-window floor (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}); both are basis-invariant because the netting is common to the central and null legs. The off-window rows carry the same flat netting: the layer is a constant curtailment flow over a constant cap benchmark, identical across all five committed estimators spanning 29.6 points of standalone recovery, so it is path-invariant and therefore floor-invariant (\texttt{calibration\_reconciliation}). Full-book and composed columns were run only at the in-sample calibration and are not extrapolated to the off-window floor. Path A, the concave-gap variant, the elasticity band, and the covariate-prior band are all in-sample-calibration quantities. The Danish leg's counterfactual image, run at the production floor and so comparable to the in-sample point, is $+\$61.2$ billion, or 8.0 points, the difference reflecting the Danish leg's dynamic-balance compounding (Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}). The ABM rows of Table~\ref{tab:estimators} run through the accounting layer natively (Figure~\ref{fig:recovery}) and are not restated here.\par}

The two hazard paths, despite sharing no household-level decision machinery with the ABM, together land closer to the empirical benchmark (97.9\% and 112.4\% of it on the benchmark-consistent shared accounting basis of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-hybrid}, 107.0\% and 121.5\% under their standalone scorers, both at the in-sample calibration point; Path B is 91.3\% shared at the off-window floor the paper headlines, and Path A was not re-run there) than any synthetic-population ABM specification tested in this paper, including the falsified burnout attempt. Overshooting less, however, is not validation: Path A overshoots the benchmark by \$164.1 billion---its terminal cumulative runoff error, and a 25.1\% under-prediction of the \$652.8 billion of runoff actually realized---with a monthly path fit no better than the ABM's, and Path B's recovery, reported across its full elasticity sensitivity band (\$809.9--\$827.7 billion, 105.9\%--108.2\%; Section~\ref{sec:pathb}), reflects an elasticity imported unmodified from \citet{liebersohn2024} rather than one fit to this paper's own benchmark, an import whose verification content Section~\ref{sec:pathb} limits to the marginal's bounded interval. The comparative claim I defend is therefore narrower than ``the hazard framework fits'', and narrower again than ``lands within 2.1\% of the benchmark'': that 2.1-point miss belongs to the in-sample calibration point, and quoting it beside a marginal measured at the off-window floor mixes two calibrations. At the calibration this paper headlines Path B (a loan-level survival structure with no household-utility content) recovers 91.3\% of the aggregate benchmark on the benchmark-consistent accounting basis, a miss of 8.7 points, and 88.2--93.7\% across the off-window floor's defensible range, a miss of 6.3 to 11.8 points; the 2.1-point miss (97.9\%; the composed companion is basis-dependent --- 100.0\% as committed on the mixed coupon basis, 98.1\% converted to a single pass-through convention, Section~\ref{sec:robustness-hybrid}) is the in-sample figure, retained as the model-fit diagnostic it is, with the ex-ante covariate priors spanning 92.2\%--99.2\% and approximately 94.3--101.3\% composed at that same in-sample calibration and not re-run off-window. What survives the reconciliation is an order-of-magnitude comparative statement rather than a precision one. Path A, whose forward simulation carries no useful propagated precision and whose own rate-gap coefficient is not statistically distinguishable from zero under any bias-respecting construction (Section~\ref{sec:patha}), lands at 112.4\% under a specification that shares data but no estimated parameters. I read that coincidence as order-of-magnitude agreement and nothing stronger: the same resampling pass puts Path A's mean at 78.3\% of the benchmark with a percentile interval spanning zero, so it cannot corroborate a sign. No synthetic-population household-choice specification I tested lands within 45\% of it. I attach no timing credential to this: Path B's raw $+0.190$ lag-0 correlation is carried by the window's shared trend (in first differences and after detrending the co-movement is indistinguishable from zero; Section~\ref{sec:pathb}), and the moving-block intervals of Table~\ref{tab:estimators} caution against weighing any single timing coefficient, including Path B's lag $-3$ peak, whose interval spans zero, and the benchmark side carries its own disqualification: its four exact-zero months are clip-induced boundary artifacts of the reporting series (Appendix~\ref{sec:robustness-seasonalfloor}, run \texttt{h1\_zero\_months\_diagnosis}). Timing alone, however, does not identify the lock-in channel: the $\beta_1 = 0$ null also peaks at lag $-3$ while still recovering 88.7\% of the benchmark on the shared basis at the in-sample calibration point and 85.7\% at the off-window floor (Table~\ref{tab:bases}), both under the production floor form; under the additive form the null recovers 35.6\% at that floor (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}). The elasticity's identified contribution is its marginal recovery over that null, defined and bounded once in Section~\ref{sec:identification}; the design identifies the marginal, not the lag structure. At the in-sample calibration point, across 10 paired reruns with different draw seeds (central and null sharing each seed), the marginal is \$70.3 billion and varies by less than \$0.01 billion, because the production engine drains prepayments fractionally and the draw seed touches only the small delinquency channel; the uncertainty that matters for the marginal is the calibration band and the structural assumptions, not simulation noise. I restrict this claim to synthetic-population specifications: Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign} reports a real-covariate cross-design variant of the ABM that does land within 45\% of the benchmark (59.3\%, and 76.3\% once that covariate population is reweighted to the book's own coupon $\times$ vintage composition, which is within 24\%), a result that complicates rather than confirms the paradigm interpretation this paragraph would otherwise support---and the reweighted reading makes the complication larger, not smaller.

Because the hazard framework's only behavioral content is loan-level covariates, a cohort-level burnout term, and literature-calibrated elasticities (with no analogue to mobility desire, loss aversion, or utility maximization), its comparative success might be read as evidence for the loan-level and institutional mechanisms (heterogeneity in FICO, LTV, and geography; servicer forbearance and pipeline delay; and cohort-level burnout) that the synthetic-population ABM of Section~\ref{sec:abm} does not represent. The paper's own measurements cap that reading: the named mechanisms ablate to a point or less each (burnout 0.86 points at the in-sample floor and 0.45 at the off-window floor, FICO/LTV 1.3 points, the delinquency pipeline \$0.39 billion, with no servicer channel beyond that pipeline modeled), the synthetic companion of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-synthesis} recovers a comparable aggregate level with no real loan-level structure at all, and the data-source asymmetry of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-scale} remains unresolved---so what the level supports is the mechanical decomposition, with the named mechanisms bounded small and remaining candidates only for the residual. The scale-sensitivity replication in Section~\ref{sec:robustness-scale} rules out the ABM/hazard sample-size difference as the driver of this gap; the data-source difference is addressed only partially by the cross-design test in Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign}, which finds that endowing the ABM with real structural covariates changes its explanatory share substantially: enough, under one calibration choice, to raise the question of whether ``cannot represent'' is the right description at all. The rate-gap coefficient's sign stability across both hazard specifications does not indicate that the underlying par-payoff penalty is doing real work, and I restate it. Only Path A estimates that coefficient; Path B imports it from \citet{liebersohn2024} as a signed elasticity band, so it cannot come out the other way, and an agreement between one estimate and one import that has no freedom to disagree is an orientation check on the two specifications' unit and sign conventions, not corroboration. It is worth running---it is what would catch a transposed sign between the paths, and it passes---but the evidence that the penalty has a real effect is the external literature; the estimated Path A coefficient is directionally consistent without being statistically distinguishable from zero under bias-respecting constructions (Section~\ref{sec:patha}). Household rationality is not irrelevant, but the aggregation structure of loan-level survival is what a model of this shortfall must get right at minimum, and Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign} leaves open how much more it must get right. This sharpens the trade-off named in this paper's title, with the qualification Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign} adds: the price of the TBA market's homogeneity appears to be paid at least in part through loan-level and institutional frictions that the synthetic-population household model does not represent and that a market-value repurchase design, as Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}'s counterfactual suggests, would substantially relieve.

\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}

Sections~\ref{sec:abm} and~\ref{sec:hazard} presented the paper's central contrast: household simulation, rational or behaviorally extended, does not close the gap to the empirical benchmark, while a structurally distinct hazard framework (discarding household utility altogether, on a substantially larger and non-synthetic sample) recovers 91.3\% of it on the benchmark-consistent accounting basis in its microsimulation form at the off-window floor this paper headlines (88.2--93.7\% across that floor's range, a miss of 6.3 to 11.8 points), and 97.9\% at the in-sample calibration point, with the caveat that no estimator's monthly path co-movement, Path B's included, survives removal of the window's shared trend (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}). The contrast is easy to over-read. The $\beta_1 = 0$ null recovers 85.7\% on the same basis at the headline floor (88.7\% in-sample), so the components common to any prepayment model (scheduled amortization, the seasoning baseline, and the turnover floor) deliver the large majority of the level; the caps sat far above what any plausible prepayment environment would have produced. The named loan-level mechanisms are measured small wherever I can ablate them: switching burnout off moves Path B by \$6.58 billion ($0.86$ points) at the in-sample floor and \$3.48 billion ($0.45$ points) at the off-window floor this paragraph's other figures use, zeroing the FICO/LTV covariates moves it $1.3$ points, and the delinquency transition matrix is bounded at \$0.39 billion (Appendix~\ref{app:params}); no servicer channel beyond that pipeline is modeled at all. Three mechanisms therefore remain \emph{candidates} for the residual---modest at the in-sample calibration (2.1 points) but 8.7 points, roughly 1.6$\times$ the headline marginal itself, at the off-window calibration the paper headlines---and for the timing failure every estimator shares, not demonstrated drivers of the level; servicer behavior beyond the delinquency pipeline, updated-equity dynamics, and home-equity extraction carry no bound of any kind here, and each is plausibly rate-cycle-correlated. First, loan-level heterogeneity in credit score, geography, and life circumstance means that mobility decisions are not a smooth function of coupon and market rate alone; the hazard framework's 295 stratum fixed effects are one mechanism that lets Path A track pool-level heterogeneity the ABM's coupon-cohort buckets cannot represent. Second, servicer behavior (loss mitigation, forbearance, and loan modification practices that vary by servicer and were unusually active during the higher-rate, higher-delinquency-risk period of the QT window) operates on prepayment and default outcomes through channels outside a borrower-side decision model; Path B's explicit competing-risks structure and Markov delinquency pipeline are a direct, if partial, representation of this channel. Third, prepayment burnout is representable at the cohort level (as in both hazard paths) but not within a closed, per-agent household population, as Section~\ref{sec:abm-results}'s falsified survivor-selection experiment showed.

\subsection{The Dual Systemic Fallout}\label{sec:discussion-fallout}

While the macroeconomic literature frequently focuses on the housing mobility implications of the lock-in effect, its transmission into the fixed-income sector creates a two-pronged systemic risk.

The first channel runs through the central bank itself: the extension of MBS durations deprives the SOMA of its scheduled cash flows, breaking the predictability required for effective Quantitative Tightening; Appendix~\ref{sec:robustness-wal} expresses this extension in approximate weighted-average-life units. The \$764.7 billion shortfall-versus-cap is, per the decomposition above, mostly mechanical: a gap to a cap schedule that no plausible prepayment environment would have met. The duration extension itself (a 9.4-year portfolio WAL under the empirical path against 3.4 years at 2021 realized speeds, Appendix~\ref{sec:robustness-wal}, where the 3.4-year comparator is a refinancing-boom baseline) is the rate-driven object, and this extension, not the cap arithmetic, feeds the fragility channels below.

The second channel runs through private banks, which, unlike the Fed, must mark their portfolios to market. The structural duration extension of these low-coupon MBS assets generated hundreds of billions in unrecognized losses across the banking sector. \citet{jiang2023} show that duration extension alone was not sufficient to precipitate failure; rather, it was the interaction of mark-to-market losses with a high share of uninsured deposits that made specific regional institutions vulnerable to self-fulfilling runs, as uninsured depositors withdrew funds upon recognizing that reported asset values no longer covered liabilities. The duration extension that is a necessary precondition for this fragility channel is generated by the below-market coupon stock meeting rate normalization under any payoff rule---the rule-only counterfactual of Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish} shortens the portfolio by only 0.6 of the roughly six extension-years (Appendix~\ref{sec:robustness-wal})---so the elasticity channel this paper identifies is a second-order contributor to that precondition, and neither it nor the extension is, on its own, a sufficient condition for bank failure. What this paper prices on the institutional side is therefore cash-flow timing alone---principal arriving later than the cap schedule assumed---and not a market-value loss. No mark-to-market or DV01 image of that 0.6 of an extension-year is put on the portfolio, because Appendix~\ref{sec:robustness-wal} declines option-adjusted duration and convexity as out of scope, so the bank-side losses \citet{jiang2023} price have no counterpart here and the fragility channel is invoked as context rather than as something this design measures.

Beyond its transmission into fixed-income markets, the lock-in effect imposes a real economic cost by preventing households from relocating toward higher-productivity labor markets. \citet{hsieh2019} estimate that housing supply constraints in high-productivity U.S. cities impose substantial aggregate output losses through this kind of spatial misallocation of labor; their headline magnitudes are cumulative level effects over 1964--2009 and were revised in a published correction, so I cite the mechanism rather than a point estimate. The per-move surplus that would turn this leg into a magnitude is not quantified anywhere in this design: no per-household or per-move welfare estimate enters the paper, so the labor-reallocation leg of the mobility argument is directional only. While their analysis concerns supply-side constraints on housing availability, the demand-side mobility constraint I quantify here operates through the same channel: households who cannot economically justify relocating cannot respond to geographic differences in labor demand, regardless of housing supply elsewhere. I do not translate the \$764.7 billion shortfall into welfare terms: it is an accounting gap against a cap schedule, mostly mechanical by the decomposition above, and neither a lower nor an upper bound on the welfare cost of the par-payoff rule. The welfare-relevant objects are the constrained moves themselves, whose fiscal image is the lock-in marginal ($+\$42.6$ billion of trapped roll-off at the headline off-window floor, $+\$70.3$ billion at the in-sample calibration point), and the forgone labor-reallocation gains this literature identifies, which my accounting framework does not measure.

\subsection{Redemption-Cap Design: The Ex-Ante Arithmetic}\label{sec:discussion-capdesign}

That the caps would sit above achievable runoff is not my observation: New York Fed staff projected \$20--\$30 billion in monthly principal payments in the month the \$35 billion ceiling took effect \citep{nyfed2022}. What this paper adds is the quantification and its ex-ante construction. The New York Fed's May 2022 baseline path supplies the test: it implied agency MBS runoff of \$740.6 billion over the window against the caps' \$1{,}417.5 billion, so the \$35 billion cap was set roughly 1.7 to 1.9 times as high as the Federal Reserve's own contemporaneous projection of achievable runoff, the multiple depending on the same disclosed intra-2022 allocation choice as the anticipated share (Section~\ref{sec:method-benchmark}). The decomposition measures the same distance from the other side: both components of the $\beta_1 = 0$ null (scheduled amortization from the book's coupon, age, and term composition, and baseline turnover at any assumed floor) recover 88.7\% of the realized shortfall at the in-sample calibration point and 85.7\% at the off-window floor under the production floor form (35.6\% under the additive form), which is how far the fixed \$35 billion cap sat above the book's mechanical principal path. Table~\ref{tab:wal} prices that path in maturity units: under the empirical CPR the book's approximate weighted-average life is 9.4 years against 3.4 years at 2021 realized speeds, and the 3.4-year comparator is a refinancing-boom baseline rather than a turnover norm, so the speed that would have filled the cap is the one the window's rate configuration had already shut off.

The paper's own out-of-window turnover measurement is the floor read (Table~\ref{tab:oosfloor}): defensible 2018 reads of 4.70\%, 4.99\% and 5.33\% by depth of out-of-the-moneyness, against refi-contaminated pooled 2017--2019 and 2019 anchors at 6.07\% and 6.91\%. The turnover-floor row prices the middle read --- the paper's headline floor --- through the identical calculator (run \texttt{wal\_normal\_turnover}): it falls between Path B's 4.76\% and the empirical 5.14\% rows, and monotonicity in CPR puts the band ends on either side of it.
