The design question that follows is speculative, and I flag it as design space rather than as a finding these estimates support. Whether this episode generalizes is a question this design cannot answer but can pose precisely. \citet{du2024}'s cross-country record covers seven central banks running active and passive runoff, and the mechanism identified here---a fixed nominal cap set above the mechanical principal path of a book whose coupons sit below market---is not specific to the Federal Reserve. What would settle it is narrow and public: each bank's announced cap or redemption schedule set against its realized redemptions, month by month, over its own tightening window. I do not assert that the other six were non-binding; I record that the comparison is constructible from published data and is not made here. Pre-committing a substitution instrument---a standing policy of substituting Treasury runoff or outright MBS sales when passive runoff undershoots---would convert extension risk from a silent shortfall into an explicit, priced policy choice, and it is the option consistent with the objective just stated, because it leaves the cap free to communicate the path instead of forcing it. Indexing the cap to the observable state of the outstanding stock (for example, to the share of the book sitting deeply below market coupon) is the weaker alternative, since it still works through the instrument the shortfall defeats. What the decomposition supplies such a design is computable in advance of the cycle, but as a band rather than as a point: scheduled amortization is fixed by the book's composition, while the turnover floor is measured, and its defensible out-of-window reads run from 4.70\% to 5.33\% depending on the depth at which out-of-the-moneyness is cut, carrying the central-elasticity marginal from $+6.8$ points at the deepest cut to $+4.3$ at the shallowest (Table~\ref{tab:oosfloor}); the floor read's own sampling error widens the identified margin further, and expressed in the units a cap designer would have to plug in, the mid-cut read's wild-cluster interval runs from 4.177\% to 5.800\% (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}). What the cap is for bears on whether binding is desirable at all. A redemption cap paces and communicates the portfolio's composition path, and Section~\ref{sec:method-benchmark} concedes that these caps never bound over this window; choosing a level that would bind therefore trades the communication objective for a speed the schedule was not built to deliver, rather than improving on it. Read on its own terms, a cap intended to bind would be set at or below the lower edge of the book's projected scheduled-plus-turnover principal path, with the identified elasticity margin ($+2.1$ to $+13.2$ points of the shortfall across the calibration box) as the behavioral band around it --- which is the form the Federal Reserve's own contemporaneous projection took, a range rather than a point. Nothing in my estimates identifies the welfare or market effects of such designs; active sales in particular would realize the mark-to-market losses that passive runoff defers. I record the option to mark where the design space lies, not to recommend a point in it.

\subsection{Evaluating Alternative Securitization Policies}\label{sec:discussion-policy}

In response to the mobility collapse, U.S. policymakers, including the Federal Housing Finance Agency (FHFA), proposed expanding assumable and portable mortgages. Fixed-income analysis suggests these proposals address only part of the problem: while portability eases consumer mobility constraints, it explicitly prevents the loan from prepaying upon relocation. The same holds for assumability, with one added wrinkle: an assumed loan also fails to prepay (the below-market coupon transfers to the buyer and the extension stays on the investor's book), and the buyer must separately finance the gap between the purchase price and the assumed balance, typically at prevailing rates, which limits take-up. Both instruments therefore relocate rather than eliminate duration extension risk, solving the microeconomic mobility constraint while cementing the macroeconomic extension risk onto the investor's balance sheet.

\subsection{The Danish Market-Value-Repurchase Alternative}\label{sec:abm-danish}

A third alternative acts on the payoff rule itself rather than on the mortgage contract's portability or the redemption cap. Section~\ref{sec:abm-results} shows that no synthetic-population household-choice specification tested here explains the majority of the trapped liquidity---a finding Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign} confines to the synthetic population, its recalibrated real-covariate variant having crossed the pre-committed threshold past which the paradigm reading cannot be cleanly maintained---so the question is how much of that trap is a consequence of the par-payoff rule itself, rather than of household behavior under any institutional regime. The Danish market-value repurchase system, which severs the link between market rates and payoff cost, gives me a direct way to ask that question quantitatively. The operative specification calibrates each framework's Danish response against \citet{berger2026}'s separately estimated moving and refinance-in-place channels---a January 2026 SSRN working draft, and the single unrefereed source this channel rests on---and transplants them onto the U.S.\ loan population so that only the payoff rule changes; it yields the paper's finding on this question, an institutional gap of $+\$61.2$ billion (Table~\ref{tab:danish}). The rest of this subsection develops that specification and its sensitivity.\footnote{\label{fn:manifest}CPR means below are aggregates over the 42 active QT months (June 2022--November 2025), and the institutional wedge is the mean over those months of the cohort-weighted Danish-minus-U.S.\ simulated CPR difference. This operative specification supersedes an initial mechanism-substitution that had implied a much larger gap; that superseded figure, and the frozen run manifests underlying every ABM headline, are catalogued in Appendix~\ref{app:ledger}.}

The recalibration rests on two behaviorally distinct channels that \citet{berger2026} (my source for the Danish institutional mechanism itself) estimate separately, and that a single extrapolated mechanism would conflate. The first is a moving channel: does removing the par-payoff penalty change how often households relocate? The second is a refinance-in-place channel: does the option to buy back debt at a market discount induce discount-buying behavior independent of moving? Danish administrative data show the two channels behave very differently under a U.S. tax transplant. The moving channel is well-identified and close to flat in the coupon gap: Danish households move at an unconditional rate of 3.2\% per year regardless of how far their coupon sits below market \citep[\S4.6, structural parameters in tab.~3]{berger2026}, versus a U.S. moving-hazard slope of 0.57--1.20 percentage points per year per 100 bp of coupon gap \citep{fonseca2024}. The refinance-in-place channel, by contrast, is substantially a Danish tax artifact: discount buybacks are attractive there because mortgage interest is deductible at a flat 33\% rate and realized capital gains on the buyback are tax-exempt. Under U.S. tax treatment (a 22\% mortgage-interest deduction and a 15\% capital-gains tax on the discount, per Berger et al.'s own counterfactual --- a characterization I read differently, though I do not adjudicate it: on the ordinary reading a discount extinguishment is cancellation-of-debt income at ordinary rates, IRC \S 61(a)(11) and Treas.\ Reg.\ \S 1.61-12(c), while \S 108(a)(1)(B) and \S 108(a)(1)(E) exclude it for insolvent borrowers and for qualified principal-residence indebtedness respectively, so the treatment is borrower-state-dependent rather than uniform in either direction; post-TCJA the assumed interest deduction also overstates most households' marginal benefit. Read as a directional bracket rather than a correction, both considerations push the U.S.-transplant channel \emph{below} Berger et al.'s already-small estimate, and neither touches the realized Danish evidence, which is measured under Danish tax), this channel loses its shield, and their general-equilibrium counterfactual \citep[\S4.9.1]{berger2026} finds it shifts the equilibrium mortgage rate by only about one basis point, economically negligible.

Recalibrating both frameworks against these two estimated channels, rather than against a single extrapolated mechanism, first requires a choice: what to do with the moving channel's \emph{level}. Berger et al.'s identified fact about the moving margin is one of slope: Danish moving is flat in the coupon gap under market-value payoff; the 3.2\% per year is Denmark's descriptive unconditional moving rate, not an estimate of how often U.S. households would move under a Danish payoff rule. Importing that level onto the U.S. loan population (the \emph{Danish-level} anchor) therefore bundles two changes, the payoff rule and Danish baseline mobility itself, and produces Danish legs whose mean CPR (3.39--3.40\%, Table~\ref{tab:danish}) sits \emph{below} the 4\% turnover floor both frameworks impose on the U.S. side (Appendix~\ref{sec:method-frictions} and Section~\ref{sec:pathb}). Death, divorce, and forced relocation do not become rarer because the payoff rule changed, so a rule-only counterfactual on the same U.S. households cannot prepay below that floor. The construction that isolates the rule is the \emph{U.S.-intercept} anchor: keep the identified flatness, but anchor the level at the production U.S. hazard evaluated at zero rate gap, with the turnover floor, seasoning baseline, burnout, and covariate terms all retained and only the rate-gap suppression switched off, plus the same U.S.-transplant refinance-in-place channel. I adopt the U.S.-intercept anchor as the production reading (it is the only one consistent with the floor's stated semantics) and retain the Danish-level anchor as a labeled bracketing case in which U.S. households are assumed to adopt Danish baseline mobility along with the Danish rule. Holding the floor fixed while importing only the Danish moving slope treats baseline turnover and rate-elastic moving as separable channels; that separability is motivated by the floor's semantics rather than identified in the data available here, which is why the Danish-level anchor, importing the two jointly, is retained as the bracketing case.

The anchor choice determines the sign of the institutional gap. Under the shared-accounting hybrid pipeline (specified in Section~\ref{sec:robustness-hybrid}: the ABM's macro-accounting layer with Path B's microsimulated CPR as the micro-foundation), the U.S.-intercept anchor yields a Danish mean CPR of 5.61\% against the locked-in U.S. leg's 4.76\%, and an institutional gap of $+\$61.2$ billion (8.0\% of the benchmark) at Berger et al.'s general-equilibrium best estimate of a negligible refinance-in-place contribution (the U.S. leg reproduces the committed production artifacts exactly). That estimate is a price statement --- an equilibrium-rate effect of about one basis point --- and reading it as a near-zero buyback \emph{volume} is an inference realized Danish data contradict: over 2022M07--2023M12, deep-discount ($\leq 2\%$ coupon) Danish callable bonds redeemed at 26.4\% per year against a 4--5\% baseline, an extraordinary component of at least 22\% per year after a generous scheduled-amortization allowance (run \texttt{danish\_redemption\_validation}; Danmarks Nationalbank securities statistics, exact stock--transaction closure on the coupon-blocked universe). The transplant imports the payoff rule alone: it does not import the Balance Principle's series-level match funding, the tap-issued joint-and-several bond series that carry Denmark's liquidity, the advisory distribution that prompts conversions, or the Danish product mix --- in particular the large interest-only share, which would cut scheduled amortization, the null's largest component, and so move the shortfall in the opposite direction from the payoff rule. Each omission is a held-fixed feature of the U.S. leg, not a measured invariance. I therefore report the rule-only gap as a band over the refinance-in-place contribution rather than at the zero point: swept in 0.25\%-CPR steps under the production anchor, the gap runs from $+\$61.2$ billion at 0\% to $+\$256.8$ billion at 3\% (run \texttt{danish\_refi\_finegrid}; slope $\approx \$65$ billion per CPR point). The realized-implied anchor sits above the swept range entirely, pricing at $+\$1{,}212$ billion beyond the rejected partial-equilibrium ceiling. Four confounds travel with it, and together they are why it is an upper anchor on the transplant rather than an estimate of it: the realized rate is gross, so conversions bundle in; it includes non-household collateral; it is environment- rather than book-matched; and it is a Danish-tax realization of an option whose U.S.-transplant value the tax attenuation is meant to shrink. This is a return to a construction the pipeline already contained: the U.S.-intercept hybrid coincides numerically, leg for leg, with the NPV-identity hybrid the replication package's technical documentation records from before the Berger recalibration, because the buyback-at-market-value identity zeroes the effective rate gap, which is computationally the same object as the zero-gap U.S. hazard; what that earlier construction lacked, and Berger et al.'s moving-flatness estimate now supplies, is empirical evidence that Danish-rule moving is flat in the gap. The sign is invariant under this anchor, and it could not have come out otherwise. The specification defines the Danish moving hazard as the production U.S. hazard evaluated at a zero rate gap; the U.S. book carries a negative gap in essentially every cell over essentially the whole window (99.53\% of exposure below the window-minimum market rate, Section~\ref{sec:identification}); and the hazard is monotone in the gap. The zero-gap leg therefore must prepay faster, the Danish trapped balance must come in lower (\$687.8 billion against \$749.0 billion), and the gap must be positive. Sweeping the refinance-in-place input from 0\% to the rejected partial-equilibrium ceiling ($\approx$18\% CPR) only adds Danish prepayment on top of that, so the sweep's sign-robustness---positive at every point, rising to $+\$1{,}038.9$ billion at the ceiling (Figure~\ref{fig:gapsweep})---is forced by construction as well. I retain both as wiring checks and read neither as evidence. That premise is an inequality about this episode, not a property of the design. It holds because the simulated book is closed at 2017--2021 originations and the window's market rate stood above essentially all of its coupons in essentially every month; where a book's rate gap is mixed in sign---another cycle, a portfolio open to new originations at the prevailing rate---the inequality fails and the sign becomes a composition question this design does not answer, since the elasticity is imported rather than estimated. Neither this sign nor the marginal transports without re-checking that inequality. What is not forced is the magnitude: \$61.2 billion, 8.0\% of the benchmark, is the quantity the recalibration actually delivers, and the Danish-level anchor below shows that a different and defensible transplant choice reverses the sign outright. This positive gap is the lock-in marginal of Section~\ref{sec:hazard-interp} seen from the counterfactual side: switching off the rate-gap suppression is what the $\beta_1 = 0$ null does, so the rule-only Danish counterfactual and the paper's lock-in contribution at the in-sample calibration point are one object measured on two accounting legs ($+\$61.2$ billion here, where the Danish leg compounds its own counterfactual balances; $+\$70.3$ billion as the U.S.-leg marginal). At the 0\% edge of the swept band the two nearly coincide; under the band presentation that near-equality is a property of that edge and of the in-sample calibration on which both legs were run, not of the counterfactual. Being one object on a second accounting leg also means the Danish magnitude inherits every conditionality the marginal carries---the baseline seasoning convention and the floor's age transport among them---so the transplant supplies no independent information about the payoff rule. It re-denominates a quantity this paper has already conditioned, and it is conditional in exactly the same ways. That near-equality is a statement about the demoted in-sample point and not about the paper's headline: against the $+\$42.6$ billion off-window marginal the Danish rule-only gap is about 44\% larger, not equal, and both legs were run at the production floor; re-run at the off-window floor itself (run \texttt{danish\_offwindow\_floor}; the U.S. leg reproduces the committed off-window central exactly), the rule-only gap is $+\$28.2$ billion, 3.7\% of the benchmark, 34\% below the off-window marginal, so the near-equality is specific to the in-sample calibration and no equality survives at matched floors. Under the Danish-level anchor, by contrast, the gap is $-\$99.9$ billion (U.S. 4.76\%, Danish 3.39\% mean CPR) and crosses zero at a refinance-in-place contribution of 1.4\% CPR; the fragility reflects the bundled mobility-culture change. The ABM's own recalibrated run under the Danish-level anchor shows $-\$728.4$ billion (crossing at 12.6\%), but its U.S.-side leg is over-predicted relative to the empirical benchmark (11.76\% simulated mean CPR under the Berger run's own calibration-stage freeze, versus 5.14\% empirical; cf.\ the production tag's 11.68\%, Section~\ref{sec:abm-results}), and under the U.S.-intercept anchor the ABM's direction follows a fortiori without a run: the ABM's lock-in penalty only ever suppresses moving, so its zero-gap intercept exceeds its locked-in U.S. leg month by month and its rule-only gap is positive by construction. Table~\ref{tab:danish} collects all four specifications.

Gap $=$ U.S. leg $-$ Danish leg; a negative gap means the Danish regime traps more than the U.S. regime. \quad a Superseded: applies the U.S.-calibrated mobility function to a Danish-style rate gap it was never estimated to describe (see text). \quad b The Berger recalibration's U.S. leg is re-simulated under that run's own calibration-stage freeze (native 15-year gate), not the \$91.0 billion production headline, so both legs and the gap share one frozen calibration. Its sign is not robust: the sweep of the refinance-in-place input crosses zero at a 1.4\% CPR contribution. \quad d The paper's production estimate (rule-only counterfactual): Berger et al.'s moving-flatness fact anchored at the production U.S. hazard's zero-gap intercept, turnover floor retained, refinance-in-place at the general-equilibrium best estimate ($\approx$0). The U.S. leg reproduces the committed production artifacts exactly. The gap is positive at every point of the refinance-in-place sweep (Figure~\ref{fig:gapsweep}), which under this anchor is forced by construction rather than found: see Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}. This row is face-denominated and ex-post on the U.S. coupon stack: under the cash-haircut reading its gap reverses sign, and the wedge that reverses it is a transfer from the SOMA portfolio to moving households rather than a resource cost (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}); and no ex-ante repricing of the repurchase option into the coupon is netted from either leg, an omission signed against the reported relief (see text).\par}

The initial mechanism-substitution that this specification corrects had implied a far larger gap, \$925.5 billion, by feeding a U.S.-calibrated mobility function a Danish-style rate gap it was never estimated to describe; it is an extrapolation rather than a measurement, catalogued in Appendix~\ref{app:ledger}. The case for the Danish alternative is modest and anchor-signed: roughly $+\$61$ billion, 8\% of the benchmark, at the in-sample calibration point ($+\$28$ billion, 3.7\%, at the off-window headline floor), the counterfactual image of the lock-in marginal. Its positivity throughout the refinance-in-place sweep is a property of the zero-gap anchor rather than a finding, so the magnitude is the informative part. The earlier ``sign we cannot identify'' reading survives only as a statement about the bracketing Danish-level anchor, which asks a different question (what if U.S. households also moved like Danes) and answers it with a Danish leg that violates the floor both frameworks impose on the U.S. side. This does not alter Sections~\ref{sec:abm} and~\ref{sec:hazard}'s central finding (that loan-level and institutional mechanics, not household choice, explain the U.S. empirical shortfall) since that comparison never depended on the Danish counterfactual. What it does revise is the payoff-rule question: the par-payoff rule's institutional cash-flow cost is real but an order of magnitude smaller than the original extrapolation implied, and of the same origin as the lock-in marginal the paper identifies directly---and of the same size as that marginal at the in-sample calibration point where both legs were run, though about 44\% larger than the off-window marginal the paper headlines. Both legs credit retired balance at par; under cash accounting the market-value buyback credit reverses the gap's sign, so the case as stated here stands on the benchmark's own face denomination, with the incidence bracket priced in Section~\ref{sec:pathb}. The $1-P$ wedge the U.S. rule collects from a moving household accrues to the bondholder, and here the bondholder is the SOMA portfolio, whose earnings reach the Treasury as remittances: the cash-haircut reading hands that wedge to the households who move and takes it from general taxpayers. So the reversal prices a transfer changing direction, not a resource cost, and its welfare sign turns on the marginal value of public funds against that of household liquidity---neither of which this framework measures. One further omission is a price rather than a held-fixed feature of the U.S. leg, and its direction runs against the reported relief. Every figure here is ex-post, on a coupon stack originated under the U.S. payoff rule; under a market-value payoff rule the repurchase option is written into the bond series and priced into the coupon ex ante, so the same households would have borrowed at a higher rate for the life of the loan \citep{campbell2013,berg2018}. That premium is netted nowhere, and the only ex-ante rate term the transplant carries---Berger et al.'s roughly one-basis-point equilibrium shift---prices the refinance-in-place channel alone. So $+\$61.2$ billion is gross institutional relief under face accounting, not a net social gain.

\section{Robustness Checks}\label{sec:robustness}

This section collects the methodological checks referenced throughout the paper. Two of them changed reported results rather than confirming them. The recalibrated cross-design variant of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign} exceeds my ex-ante threshold and qualifies the household-choice reading at the aggregate level; and the off-window floor re-measurement of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor} is where the paper's headline marginal and level are actually derived ($+9.2 \to +5.6$ points; 97.9\% $\to$ 91.3\% shared), demoting the in-window calibration to an in-sample point. On the shared basis the paper quotes throughout, the hazard framework undershoots the benchmark at the headline calibration (91.3\%); it overshoots only on the standalone basis (107.0--121.5\%). The remaining checks confirm rather than change the central decomposition.

Four of this section's checks are mechanics rather than results and are collected in the online appendix: the behavioral-extension mechanics (Appendix~\ref{sec:robustness-extensions}), the weighted-average-life quantification (Appendix~\ref{sec:robustness-wal}), the fifteen-year fold-in and the rate-gap-units correction (Appendix~\ref{sec:robustness-pipeline}), and the seasonal-floor program (Appendix~\ref{sec:robustness-seasonalfloor}), whose verdict the calibration box already carries: what a seasonally varying floor moves is floor dispersion under the hard maximum, not cyclicality, and monthly timing earns no credential there---the frozen timing rule is cleared by a wrong-month rotation and by the $\beta_1 = 0$ null itself.

\subsection{Scale Sensitivity}\label{sec:robustness-scale}

The ABM's 10,000-household population and the hazard framework's 75,000-loan sample differ in both scale and data source. To test whether population size alone drives the gap between the two frameworks, I re-ran the ABM's core household mechanic (exponential mobility-desire heterogeneity---the distribution the production code draws, on a dollar scale---mapped through a rate-gap-driven CPR function) at N = 10,000 and N = 75,000 across 50 seeds each. The mean simulated CPR is 15.616\% at both population sizes (difference 0.0003 percentage points), while the 95\% confidence interval narrows by a factor of 2.4$\times$ at the larger $N$, of the same order as, though about 12\% short of, the 2.74$\times$ an idealized $\sqrt{N}$ law would predict ($\sqrt{7.5} \approx 2.74$), consistent with a non-i.i.d.\ component of seed noise; I do not lean on the exact ratio. Population size changes the precision of that mechanic's estimate, not its central tendency. Two limits on what the check shows. Both are large, so I state the conclusion conditionally. First, it exercised the household cost-benefit mechanic \emph{in isolation}: the production specification adds a 43\% front-end DTI wall, a discrete twenty-percent wait-and-see freeze trait, income-scaled curtailment, eleven-cohort multi-vintage weighting with a fifteen- and thirty-year term split, and a settlement-lag kernel, none of which are present here. The isolated mechanic's 15.616\% mean CPR accordingly runs 3.9 points---about a third---above the production ABM's 11.761\%, so the object whose scale-invariance is demonstrated is not the estimator whose recovery is at issue. Second, that check had no committed script, artifact or gate behind it; the figure survived from an earlier draft. Both limits are now addressed by a pre-committed rerun on the full production specification (run \texttt{production\_scale\_test}), fifty seeds at each population size, carrying the DTI wall, the freeze trait, curtailment, the eleven-cohort weighting with its term split and the settlement-lag kernel. It recovers 12.66\% of the benchmark at $N = 10{,}000$ against 12.18\% at $N = 75{,}000$---a difference of $0.48$ points against a fifty-seed standard deviation of $3.25$ points, so well inside one standard deviation---at mean simulated CPRs of 11.60\% and 11.64\%, close to the production run's 11.76\% rather than to the isolated mechanic's 15.6\%. Seed precision improves at the larger population, the standard deviation falling from $3.25$ to $1.05$ points (a ratio of 3.1 against the $\sqrt{7.5} \approx 2.74$ an idealized law would give), and again I do not lean on the exact ratio. Population size therefore does not drive the gap between this estimator and the hazard framework, and that statement now rests on the production specification rather than on a fragment of it. It does not follow, however, that the gap is attributable to modeling paradigm alone: that distinction cannot be closed by resampling, and Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign} takes it up directly with the completed cross-design pair.

\subsection{Benchmark Sensitivity to Data Source and Cap-Schedule Assumptions}\label{sec:robustness-benchmark}

The \$764.7 billion empirical benchmark (Section~\ref{sec:method-benchmark}) is constructed from the New York Fed's SOMA current-face-value series under the actual phased redemption cap schedule, netting realized roll-off against the cap within the active QT window. I treat net accumulation, rather than one-sided accumulation of shortfall months only, as the correct construction because it is the only version comparable to the Danish counterfactual of Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}, where simulated roll-off routinely exceeds the cap; a one-sided construction would overstate the empirical benchmark relative to that counterfactual and bias the recovered percentages reported for both estimators. Researchers extending this work with alternative SOMA vintages or a different cap-schedule assumption should expect the benchmark level, and therefore the recovered percentages in Sections~\ref{sec:abm} and~\ref{sec:hazard}, to shift; the qualitative ranking of the three estimators reported in Table~\ref{tab:estimators} is not sensitive to this choice, since the ABM's shortfall against any plausible benchmark construction remains an order of magnitude smaller than either hazard path's. Section~\ref{sec:method-benchmark} separately addresses the conceptual status of the cap-relative construction itself. One such alternative is now measured rather than anticipated. The benchmark nets cash arriving in month $t$ against the cap in force in month $t$, which is what the cap constrains: the Committee set a ceiling on principal not reinvested in a given month, and that is a statement about cash. Agency remittance, however, means the cash arriving in month $t$ was generated by prepayment decisions in months $t$, $t-1$ and $t-2$, on the settlement kernel this paper already applies to simulated roll-off (0.10, 0.60, 0.30 at lags zero through two). Agency remittance is itself a deterministic lag of roughly one month, so that profile is not a description of the remittance cycle: it is an empirical smearing kernel over the months in which a given month's prepayment decisions arrive as cash, and the alternate kernels below price the choice. Convolving the cap schedule forward with that same kernel and re-netting the unchanged realized series gives a settlement-aligned benchmark of \$722.7 billion against the calendar \$764.7 billion, a shift of $-5.5$\% (run \texttt{settlement\_months\_benchmark}). The shift is mechanical, and its size is exactly accounted for: the QT window is half-open, so cap convolved past its final month is dropped---October 2025 loses its two-month tap and November 2025 its one- and two-month taps, \$42.0 billion of the \$1{,}417.5 billion schedule---and because the realized series is untouched and both legs sum over the same months, the benchmark moves by exactly that \$42.0 billion. Two alternate kernels bracketing that profile put the shift at $-\$28.0$ and $-\$52.5$ billion. I retain the calendar convention, for the reason Section~\ref{sec:method-benchmark} gives for retaining the cap at all: it is the only policy-committed quantity available, and it is committed in cash-arrival months. What the variant establishes is that re-basing moves no dollar quantity in this paper---the identified marginal is \$42.6 billion on either denominator---and moves the percentages only by the common factor $1.058$: the central Path~B level reads 96.6\% rather than 91.3\%, the $\beta_1 = 0$ null 90.7\% rather than 85.7\%, and the marginal $+5.9$ rather than $+5.6$ points. The decomposition is unchanged in substance, and the null still recovers the large majority of the benchmark under either convention. The variant also bears on the path-level timing question every estimator here leaves open. If the monthly series each estimator's path is scored against is a cash-arrival object---reported roll-off smeared over the months in which a month's prepayment decisions arrive as cash and, in its CPR form, clipped by a back-out whose non-negativity rule pins four months to exactly zero and posts the displaced mass into the following month's reading (Section~\ref{sec:method-benchmark})---then a monthly path is not a behavioral object, and the timing question may be ill-posed rather than a failure every estimator shares. I state that as a candidate account, not a finding: reporting mechanics and behavior arrive in the same series, and nothing here separates them. A related window-construction pitfall merits a reproducibility note: accumulating from the QT start date without an upper bound admits eight post-QT months (December 2025 onward). Where the dollar aggregation already applies the bounded active-QT mask, as it does throughout this paper, only the path statistics drift---the headline CPR means by roughly 0.3--0.5 percentage points; where it does not, the benchmark level itself moves. That asymmetry (path statistics silently sensitive to a window filter the masked dollar aggregates are robust to) is why every headline figure in this paper cites a single tagged run manifest (footnote~\ref{fn:manifest}).

\subsection{Cross-Design Test: Real Covariates in the ABM}\label{sec:robustness-crossdesign}

Section~\ref{sec:method-estimators} flags an unresolved confound between this paper's two estimators: they differ in modeling paradigm (household choice versus loan-level survival) and in data source (synthetic population versus real Freddie Mac loan records) simultaneously, so a low ABM share and a high hazard-framework share are equally consistent with either explanation. To make partial progress on separating them, I re-ran the ABM's decision rule with structural covariates (coupon, loan age, and original LTV) drawn directly from the same 75,000-loan Freddie Mac sample used throughout Section~\ref{sec:hazard}, in place of the log-normal, FRED-median-calibrated draws used elsewhere in this paper. Behavioral draws (mobility desire, loss aversion, patience, transaction cost) remain synthetic throughout, since the Freddie Mac data contains no analogue for these constructs; this test therefore varies real structural heterogeneity---and, until the post-stratification below, book composition jointly---not a fully real population.

Because the ABM's mobility-scale parameter is calibrated to hold a real-world baseline turnover floor fixed (Appendix~\ref{sec:method-abm}), swapping in a population with different LTV and coupon moments raises a calibration question that a naive covariate swap does not resolve on its own: should the turnover-floor anchor be re-derived against the new population's moments, or held fixed at its original value? I report both variants. The recalibrated variant applies the recalibration rule of Appendix~\ref{sec:method-frictions}, re-anchoring the mobility-scale parameter to reproduce the same 4--5\% turnover floor under the real population's moments; the frozen variant suspends that rule and holds the original synthetic-population calibration unchanged.

Before running either variant, I specified ex ante the following interpretive thresholds: a recovery above 50\% of the empirical benchmark would undercut the paradigm interpretation advanced in Sections~\ref{sec:abm}--\ref{sec:conclusion}; a recovery below 35\% would corroborate it, consistent with the synthetic-population ABM's own specifications; and a recovery between 35\% and 50\% would be ambiguous.

The recalibrated variant recovers \$453.5 billion, or 59.3\%, of the benchmark, above my ex-ante 50\% threshold. The frozen variant recovers \$159.5 billion, or 20.9\%, within the corroborating band. Both are single draws, so a pre-committed fifty-seed sweep varying both randomization layers (the behavioral draws and the covariate subsample; run \texttt{cross\_design\_seeds}) tests whether the threshold crossing is a property of the estimator or of the seed. It is the estimator: the recalibrated variant averages 60.2\% with a standard deviation of 3.3 points and a 95\% band of 55.1--66.0\%, the committed draw sits at the 48th percentile of its own distribution, and all fifty seeds classify as undercutting. The frozen variant averages 22.0\%. The band is wide enough to report---10.9 points, against the 7.7-point disclosure threshold fixed before the run---but not wide enough to reach the calibration swing, so the operative uncertainty here remains the calibration choice rather than seed noise, now measured rather than assumed. The same sweep records the deep-gap floor each variant implies, and the two variants separate cleanly on it. The recalibrated variant's CPR at an 8\% market rate averages 4.59\% and stays inside the observed 4--5\% turnover band on all fifty seeds. The frozen variant averages 7.05\% and lies outside that band on all fifty (range 6.31--7.63\%). This is the same admissibility test I apply to the externally anchored variant earlier in this subsection, and applied consistently it counts against the frozen leg, not the recalibrated one: the leg the paper reports as falling within the corroborating band is the leg that misses the turnover moment. I therefore read the 20.9\% as the less admissible of the two calibrations rather than as corroboration, which strengthens the undercutting reading rather than qualifying it. One further caveat on the word ``frozen'': re-deriving that variant's mobility-scale anchor per seed gives a mean of 43{,}493 with a standard deviation of 1{,}181 and a minimum of 39{,}984, against the committed 43{,}883, which sits at the very top of its own range. The anchor is frozen by convention, not because the data pin it there. Table~\ref{tab:crossdesign} summarizes both against the synthetic-population control, and Figure~\ref{fig:crossdesign} plots the paired comparison.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig5_cross_design}
\caption{The cross-design test as paired panels. Panel (a): dollar recovery for the two real-covariate variants against the pre-committed thresholds (above 50\% undercuts the paradigm interpretation; below 35\% corroborates it) and the synthetic-population control band; the recalibrated variant's 59.3\% crosses the undercutting threshold while the frozen variant's 20.9\% stays within the corroborating band. Panel (b): lag-0 path correlations, all negative, so neither variant reproduces the empirical path's timing regardless of calibration choice, and after Section~\ref{sec:pathb}'s detrending result no estimator in this paper does.}
\label{fig:crossdesign}
\end{figure}

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\small

\label{tab:crossdesign}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{4.0cm}lll@{}}
\toprule
 & Recalibrated (primary) & Frozen (robustness) & Synthetic control \\

Under the alternative most-positive-$r$ convention, the synthetic control's best alignment is $+0.195$ at lag $-3$, the ``$+0.19$ at the three-month lead'' of Section~\ref{sec:abm-results}; the two conventions summarize the same cross-correlation profile, and the substantive conclusion (no positive contemporaneous alignment, which, after Section~\ref{sec:pathb}'s detrending result, no estimator including Path B shows) holds under either.
\end{tablenotes}
