Two results stand out. First, by my own ex-ante criterion, the primary recalibrated variant's 59.3\% recovery, 60.2\% averaged over fifty seeds, moves against the paradigm interpretation: with real structural covariates and a like-for-like turnover anchor, the household-choice ABM explains far more of the benchmark than the synthetic-population specifications throughout Sections~\ref{sec:abm} and~\ref{sec:robustness-scale} suggested. Second, the \$294 billion gap between the recalibrated and frozen variants (produced solely by how the turnover-floor anchor is set, with the underlying covariates held fixed) indicates that the ABM's aggregate share is highly sensitive to a calibration choice that is orthogonal to data source. The present test cannot, on its own, cleanly attribute Sections~\ref{sec:abm} and~\ref{sec:hazard}'s contrast to modeling paradigm rather than to data source: recalibration choice is doing comparable work to whichever of paradigm or data source the test was designed to isolate.

A pre-committed post-stratification separates composition from heterogeneity (run \texttt{cross\_design\_reweight}). Reweighting the real-covariate population to the SOMA coupon-by-vintage-group composition---the book the benchmark is measured on---with the mobility scale re-derived on the reweighted population under the same floor rule moves the recalibrated variant from 59.3\% to 76.3\% (importance weights; effective sample size 1{,}332 of 10{,}000, disclosed; a seed-42 re-draw within SOMA-weighted cells gives 70.6\%) and the frozen variant from 20.9\% to 12.6\% (re-draw 36.0\%). The composition mismatch therefore understated rather than manufactured the recalibrated recovery: at book composition, real-covariate household choice with a like-for-like floor anchor explains roughly seventy to seventy-six percent of the benchmark, the ex-ante undercutting verdict survives composition adjustment, and the calibration-choice spread this subsection flags widens to roughly \$488 billion. The reweight is a level exercise; the path-level failure of Table~\ref{tab:crossdesign} is not revisited by it.

A third variant constrains the calibration question from the other side: it drops the turnover-floor anchor entirely and parameterizes the two author-calibrated behavioral elements from the same external source Path B's elasticity imports, pinning the mobility-desire scale to \citepos{liebersohn2024} zero-gap moving level (a 1.5\% quarterly ZIP-code moving hazard when the average rate gap was essentially zero, 5.9\% annualized) and the wait-and-see freeze share to the magnitude their 6.5\%-per-quarter-per-100 bp mobility decline implies at the production 150 bp trigger (18.3\% against the author-calibrated 20\%; the DTI wall and the loss-aversion coefficient are already externally sourced). Recovery does rise without the floor recalibration (to 150.6\% of benchmark, overshooting it by half), but it does so by predicting essentially zero prepayment at QT-window rate gaps: the variant's CPR at an 8\% market rate is 0.0\%, against the observed 4--5\% discount-cohort turnover and against the roughly 1.44--1.45\% quarterly moving hazard \citet{liebersohn2024} themselves observe at 2022--2023 gaps. The externally anchored gate structure can match the zero-gap moving level or the observed deep-gap turnover, but not both: the exponential mobility-desire distribution that fits the former (scale 4{,}472 against the recalibrated 36{,}086) shuts moving down completely once the par-payoff penalty binds. I read this as clarifying the floor anchor's role: it is what keeps the ABM on the observed turnover data, not an artifact that suppresses the model's rate response. The anchored dimension itself is never claimed as fit: matching the floor is tautological by construction (Appendix~\ref{sec:method-frictions}), and what this variant measures is what that tautology purchases---empirical admissibility at deep gaps---one more instance of the deflate-as-well-as-inflate indeterminacy this subsection already carries (same harness as the two variants above). Scored against the pre-committed bands, as this variant's own specification required, its 150.6\% classifies as undercutting the paradigm reading, and the frozen artifact records that classification. I do not read it as informative about the paradigm question, and I label that reading post hoc rather than pre-committed, because this is the kind of distinction the paper's pre-commitment design is meant to surface. The pre-run specification committed only to reporting the deep-gap floor diagnostic ``alongside whatever the recovery number is, whichever direction it moves''; it did not commit to treating a failed diagnostic as grounds for removing the recovery from the band. The admissibility precondition---that a variant must reproduce an empirically admissible deep-gap turnover level before its recovery is interpretable---was formulated after the result was seen, and it is reported here as a labelled post-run qualification, not as a pre-registered exclusion. A reader who declines the qualification should score the variant at 150.6\% against the 50\% band. That strengthens the undercutting reading this subsection already concedes rather than weakening it, and no headline quantity turns on which way it is read.

To test whether a minimal simulated-minimum-distance calibration could reconcile the two external moments, I ran one: a joint two-parameter fit, the mobility-desire scale together with a forced-move point mass added to the structural family, with the moment set, admissibility bands, distance, and verdict vocabulary all fixed before execution and a parity gate replaying the committed 59.303\% first. The verdict is joint infeasibility, now with two degrees of freedom rather than one: at the distance-minimizing point (scale 1{,}000, forced-move mass 3.9\%) the zero-gap moving level overshoots its band just as the deep-gap turnover reaches its own (0.0644 against $[0.055, 0.063]$; 0.0394 against $[0.040, 0.050]$), because the forced mass lifts both moments together once the scale is at its floor. The near-fit, scored through the same harness, recovers 107.1\% of benchmark, far above the pre-committed cross-design bands, so external anchoring pushes this structural family past the benchmark, not toward it. I deliberately do not fit QT-window CPR moments: the recovery statistic is a functional of that path, and matching it would convert the test into a fitted quantity, the overfitting the pre-commitment architecture exists to prevent. The hazard-side headline quantities are not functions of ABM calibration and cannot move under any outcome of this run.

Both variants retain the ABM's central path-level failure: the monthly CPR path correlation stays negative at lag 0 ($-0.336$ and $-0.311$), as all estimators' are once the window's shared trend is removed (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}); Path B's distinction is level accuracy, not timing. The timing critique of Section~\ref{sec:abm} therefore survives regardless of which calibration variant is preferred, even as the dollar-recovery critique does not straightforwardly survive under the recalibrated variant.

\subsection{Cross-Design Synthesis: The Symmetric Companion Test}\label{sec:robustness-synthesis}

The symmetric companion test (estimating the hazard framework's survival structure on a synthetic population, to check whether that structure alone recovers the benchmark independently of real data) materially updates the cross-design picture: Path B recovers 106.0\% of the benchmark on a fully synthetic population with no real Freddie Mac data at all (96.9\% on the shared accounting basis, whose common netting applies equally here), close to its 107.0\% real-data recovery at the same in-sample calibration point, though the raw levels correlation drops from $+0.190$ to $-0.06$ at lag 0 (a levels comparison; neither value survives detrending, Section~\ref{sec:pathb}). That 106.0\% is a recovery level, however, and this paper demotes recovery levels everywhere else for a reason. Running the mechanism-removed leg on the same synthetic population --- $\beta_1 = 0$, nothing else changed --- recovers 95.6\%, so what the companion test identifies is a differential of 10.4 points, or \$79.3 billion, and not the 106.0\% level (\texttt{synthetic\_companion\_null}). That differential is of the same order as the 9.2-point real-book gap between the central leg's 107.0\% and the $\beta_1 = 0$ null's 97.8\%, which Section~\ref{sec:identification} already treats as too small to license a level-based reading; a superseded cross-basis figure put that gap at 18.3 points, which was an error of basis rather than of arithmetic --- it differenced a standalone-scorer central leg against a shared-basis null --- and on any single basis the real-book gap is 9.2 points at the in-sample calibration point and 5.6 points at the off-window floor, so the synthetic differential slightly exceeds its real-book counterpart rather than falling short of it; the synthetic recovery is floor-dominated in the same way the real one is. This decomposes the tension rather than resolving it uniformly: at the aggregate level, the household-choice paradigm required real covariates to reach 59.3\%, while the loan-level paradigm reaches a comparable aggregate recovery with none. At the path level, both paradigms still depend on real structure for correct timing. I treat the aggregate-level paradigm claim of Sections~\ref{sec:hazard} and~\ref{sec:conclusion} as corroborated by this result on its differential rather than substantially strengthened by its level, and the path-level claim as still open.

\subsection{Cross-Foundation Checks: Hybrid Pipeline and Full-Book Weighting}\label{sec:robustness-hybrid}

The hybrid pipeline referenced in Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish} holds the paper's accounting fixed while swapping the micro-foundation for loan behavior. Concretely, it runs the ABM's macro-accounting layer unchanged (SOMA balance tracking, phased-cap netting, income-scaled curtailment, term-aware scheduled amortization, and the Danish dynamic-balance loop), but replaces the ABM's CPR surface with Path B's microsimulated CPR path; no settlement kernel applies, since Path B settles prepayments in the month they occur. Holding the accounting fixed, the U.S.-side recovery is 97.9\% (the shared layer adds the income-scaled curtailment flow that Path B's standalone scorer omits: \$69.56 billion over the window), and re-running the Danish leg under the Berger-recalibrated regime yields, under the production U.S.-intercept anchor, the U.S. 4.76\% / Danish 5.61\% mean-CPR pair and the $+\$61.2$ billion institutional gap that Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish} adopts as its production estimate (the Danish-level bracketing anchor gives 3.39\% and $-\$99.9$ billion). The construction removes accounting differences between frameworks as a confound of the institutional comparison: both regimes flow through one accounting layer with one empirically grounded micro-foundation.

The same layer now scores every estimator, which is what makes the recovery figures commensurable with the benchmark: the actual SOMA roll-off underlying the empirical shortfall contains borrowers' curtailment dollars, a flow no hazard simulation models, so the shared layer adds it to each simulated roll-off. The netting is identical across estimators (\$69.56 billion for every run), and identical \emph{by construction}, not by coincidence, with the conversion architecture stated per leg: each estimator's hazard dynamics evolve on its own internal balances (Path A drains cohort balances, Path B drains loan balances, and the Danish legs deliberately compound a dynamic counterfactual balance, Appendix~\ref{sec:method-abm}), but every \emph{U.S.-leg} dollar conversion is common (simulated roll-off is rescaled month-by-month to the actual holdings path (WSHOMCB) by the book-to-sample ratio), so the curtailment flow, computed on those same actual holdings, is a common flow rather than a per-run quantity. It is a flat 9.1-percentage-point wedge between the two bases; the invariance argument covers U.S. legs only. Danish legs compute curtailment on their own counterfactual balances inside the Danish loop; the slower Danish drain leaves higher balances and hence more curtailment dollars, a differential I quantify and print: Danish-leg curtailment is \$70.33 billion against the U.S. legs' common \$69.56 billion for the central hybrid (+\$0.77 billion), already embedded in every printed gap and immaterial to its sign or size.

Table~\ref{tab:bases} collects every hazard leg's recovery on all four accounting bases (the lock-in margin is basis-invariant at either floor because the netting is bit-identical across legs and cancels exactly, an invariance that holds at every cell of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}'s calibration box). The fully corrected figure comes from a joint run (the book-coupon-reweighted simulation scored through the shared layer in one pass), which recovers \$765.1 billion (unrounded: \$765.077 billion): 100.0\% of the \$764.7 billion benchmark at one decimal, and exactly 100.04\% as the ratio of the two unrounded figures, $765.077/764.748$. That is a mixed-basis measurement: the reweight matches borrower note rates to security pass-through buckets, and converting the sample side to the pass-through basis first (note rate less the vintage guarantee fee and base servicing, $\Delta = 0.80$ points, with legs at $0.60$--$0.90$) puts the like-for-like composed figure at 98.1\% (97.6--99.1 across the legs; run \texttt{coupon\_convention\_reweight}) --- so 100.0\% is the committed mixed-basis reading, not the corrected number. Path A's joint run reads \$905.1 billion, 118.4\%. The composed figure is conditional on the ex-ante covariate priors (the covariate band spans 92.2\%--99.2\% on the shared basis, approximately 94.3\%--101.3\% composed (Section~\ref{sec:pathb})), so its precision is a property of that calibration, not a tuned result. Because the netting is common, the joint results equal the one-at-a-time deltas composed ($107.0 + 2.1 - 9.1$) exactly; I report the identity as a cross-check on the joint run, not as its source. The abstract and Sections~\ref{sec:hazard-interp} and~\ref{sec:conclusion} state recoveries on this basis.

The curtailment layer invites one further stress, since its assumed rate is an external input rather than a fitted quantity. The monthly curtailment flow is the product of an income-driven fraction, which is independent of the assumed curtailment rate, and that rate, applied to the actual holdings path (WSHOMCB), so the netted total is exactly proportional to the assumed rate: it does not interact with any estimator's dynamics. A $\pm 25\%$ stress on the assumed rate (whose realized window average is approximately 0.84\% annualized, Section~\ref{sec:method-benchmark}) therefore scales the \$69.56 billion netting to \$52.17 and \$86.95 billion and the 9.1-point wedge to 6.8 and 11.4 points, a uniform shift in every U.S. leg's shared-basis recovery that leaves the lock-in marginal and every cross-estimator gap unchanged to the decimal, because both legs of each comparison net the identical flow. The same argument holds month by month for any time-varying curtailment process: the netting is the month-by-month product of a shared curtailment rate and the shared actual-holdings path, and the trapped-liquidity metric is a linear signed sum against a fixed cap-target path, so a seasonal profile, or an income elasticity that differs across 2022--23 and 2024--25, subtracts out of the marginal term by term whatever its shape; only the common level wedge and the Danish leg's sub-\$1 billion differential can move. A demonstration run makes the identity concrete: a $\pm 25\%$-amplitude seasonal profile and a 2022--23 versus 2024--25 regime split move the netted total to \$70.06 and \$68.09 billion and the wedge to 9.16 and 8.90 points, while the lock-in marginal is unchanged to below $10^{-13}$ points on both legs and the simulated CPR paths are bit-identical. A fourth profile makes the servicer case explicit: a deterministic two-servicer mix (fixed 0.60/0.40 shares, front-loaded and flat group factors aggregating to one monthly rate) moves the wedge to 9.30 points (\$71.12 billion netted) with the marginal again unchanged, because any servicer-heterogeneous pattern that aggregates to a monthly rate is a special case of the same identity. The Danish legs, which compute curtailment on their own counterfactual balances, scale only to first order, and an exact rerun of the accounting layer across the same range (the simulated CPR paths are bit-identical across scales, since curtailment enters only the accounting layer) puts their differential at $+\$0.76$, $+\$0.77$, and $+\$0.66$ billion at the $0.75\times$, $1.0\times$, and $1.25\times$ points respectively. Those figures are measured on the \emph{Danish-level bracketing} leg, because the committed microsim cache the scaling run consumes was built under that anchor, and they do not carry over to the production leg. Re-scoring the same range on the U.S.-intercept cache (run \texttt{curtailment\_danish\_scaling} with the production anchor) reverses the sign and crosses the materiality threshold: the differential is $-\$1.68$ billion at the unit point and $-\$2.39$ billion at $1.25\times$. The mechanism is the one this paragraph already names, read the other way. Curtailment accrues on retained balance, so the sign follows which leg drains faster: the bracketing Danish leg is \emph{slower} than the U.S.\ leg (3.39\% against 4.76\% mean CPR) and so retains more balance and accrues more curtailment, while the production Danish leg is \emph{faster} (5.61\%) and accrues less. I therefore withdraw the claim that the curtailment differential is immaterial to the Danish comparison. On the production leg it is not: at $-\$1.68$ billion it is 2.7\% of the $+\$61.2$ billion in-sample gap and 6.0\% of the $+\$28.2$ billion off-window gap, small enough not to threaten either sign but large enough that it belongs in the figure rather than in a dismissal.

The second check reweights the sample to the book. The Path A and Path B headline recoveries are balance-weighted estimates on the composition of the Freddie object each path uses: Path A the full 2017--2021 origination universe, Path B the 75,000-loan draw, whose unweighted mean note rate of 3.86\% sits above the book's 2.49\% face-weighted pass-through coupon. The origin of the Path B reweight is that draw; the unindented bucket shares Table~\ref{tab:composition} prints beside the book are the universe's, exposure-weighted at the window open, and the two are different populations. Reweighting the simulated hazards to the book's actual coupon mix shifts Path B from 107.0\% to 109.1\% of the benchmark and Path A from 121.5\% to 127.5\%, a composition effect of roughly two percentage points for Path B that bounds the sample-to-book extrapolation error along the coupon dimension --- on the committed mixed coupon basis; on a single pass-through convention the same reweight moves Path B only from 107.0\% to 107.2\%, so the like-for-like composition effect is $+0.2$ points and the bound tightens by an order of magnitude (run \texttt{coupon\_convention\_reweight}) (the full sample-versus-book composition comparison is Appendix~\ref{app:composition}). The agency dimension (Ginnie Mae collateral) has no internal bound; it is externally bounded and now scored directly by a published-series overlay in Section~\ref{sec:pathb}, whose Ginnie-specific contribution ($+\$38.5$ billion) lands inside the static bound.

\subsection{Floor and Elasticity-Band Sensitivity: The Calibration Box}\label{sec:robustness-floor}

Path B's dominant level-setter is the baseline turnover floor, and Section~\ref{sec:pathb} concedes that its 4\% calibration is measured inside the evaluation window. To quantify what rides on that choice, I swept the floor across seven values from 2\% to 6\% annual CPR jointly with the full Liebersohn--Rothstein elasticity band (5.5\%, 6.5\%, 7.7\%), rerunning the production central and $\beta_1 = 0$ null legs at every floor under the production convention: same committed 75,000-loan sample, same seed and regime tuple, one shared macro frame. Both sweeps were pre-committed: grid, parity gates, and interpretive thresholds entered the repository before any run executed (commit ordering recorded in Appendix~\ref{app:ledger}); the fresh runs at the production point reproduce the committed artifacts exactly (central \$818.530 billion, null \$748.185 billion, marginal $+\$70.345$ billion), and the instrumented floor-bind share reproduces the 36\% of 1,683,124 evaluated loan-months quoted in Section~\ref{sec:pathb}.

The sweep mechanics and the contamination separation behind the off-window anchors are collected in Appendix~\ref{app:floormech}; the exhibits they produce --- the calibration box (Table~\ref{tab:floorband}), the marginal as a curve in the elasticity (Table~\ref{tab:lowband}), and the out-of-sample anchors (Table~\ref{tab:oosfloor}) --- are in Section~\ref{sec:hazard}, and what follows are the readings the headline interval rests on.

The box sweeps the floor's level but never its functional form---the choice that censors the elasticity. A form test specified ex ante (spec and interpretive threshold fixed in the script's header before its runs executed, the ex-ante convention Appendix~\ref{app:ledger} records, with the script and its artifact entering the repository in one revision; the max-form pair at the production floor reproduces the committed artifacts exactly, as its parity gate) reran the central and null legs with the hard maximum of \eqref{eq:pathB} replaced by a competing-risks combination on the survival scale, $h = 1 - (1 - \underline{h})(1 - h^{\mathrm{vol}})$, under which involuntary and voluntary hazards add and the elasticity is never inert. The result changes the reading in two ways. The marginal moves to $+11.25$ points at the production floor, a $+2.05$-point shift that exceeds the $\pm 1$-point immateriality threshold fixed ex ante, so the form-dependence is real. But under the additive form the marginal is nearly floor-invariant, spanning only $+11.29$ to $+11.21$ points across the 3--5\% floors whose max-form span ($+10.8$ to $+5.5$) failed the stability threshold of Appendix~\ref{app:floormech}: the failed gate was measuring the censoring mechanics of the max form, not instability in the elasticity's contribution. The aggregate level, by contrast, is strongly form-dependent (the additive central leg recovers 67.2\% at the production floor, versus 107.0\% under the max form, because adding a 4\% involuntary hazard to moderate voluntary hazards raises book-wide prepayment), and the max form remains production because its floor semantics, a minimum total turnover matching the observed 4--5\% on deep-discount cohorts where the voluntary hazard is near zero, is the reading the calibration anchor supports; at that anchor the two forms nearly coincide. A pre-committed completion of this design (run \texttt{floor\_form\_offwindow}) runs the additive form at the off-window anchors themselves: the additive marginal is $+11.22$/$+11.21$/$+11.19$ points at the 4.695/4.991/5.334\% floors, within 0.06 points of its production-floor value, so the entire in-window-to-off-window demotion of the max-form marginal operates through the censoring channel this paragraph diagnoses, and the near-coincidence defense of the max form at the deep-discount anchor does not extend to the shallow-gap off-window anchors. The form dimension is now a measured curve rather than a two-point hull (run \texttt{floor\_form\_mixture}; both endpoints reproduce the committed forms bit-exactly). Let $s$ be the share of the measured floor entering as an additive competing involuntary cause, with $1-s$ remaining a floor on total turnover: $s = 0$ is the production hard maximum and $s = 1$ the additive form. The curve rises steeply and plateaus --- at the off-window anchors the marginal is $+7.5$ points by $s = 0.1$, $+9.7$ at $s = 0.25$, $+10.7$ at $s = 0.4$, and flat near $+11.2$ from $s = 0.6$ up (peak $+11.24$ at $s = 0.8$, a 0.03-point plateau dip to the additive endpoint) --- while the central leg's standalone-scorer recovery falls from 100.4\% to 55.9\% along the way, the level cost the additive end pays. Section~\ref{sec:pathb}'s reading that the strictly-involuntary share is plausibly well under half therefore does \emph{not} pull the marginal toward the max end: even modest additive shares close most of the gap to the additive form, because an additive component acts wherever the max form censors. What keeps the headline at the $s = 0$ endpoint is the max form's semantics, now displayed as the conservative endpoint of a measured curve rather than defended by fit. The level cost of that form-robustness carries over to the off-window anchors too, and it is large enough to report beside the hull rather than only at the production floor: at the 4.991\% headline anchor the additive central leg recovers 55.9\% of the benchmark against a 44.7\% null, and at the 4.695\% and 5.334\% anchors 59.3\% and 52.0\% at the central band point, so the additive form undershoots the max form's aggregate level by 43.5 to 45.3 points at every off-window anchor. The hull's upper endpoint is produced by the least accurate of these cells: $+13.10$ points comes from the additive form at the 4.695\% floor and the band's 7.7\% high edge, whose central leg recovers 61.2\% of the benchmark it is scored against. The form-conditional range is therefore a range over specifications that differ sharply in aggregate fit, and its upper end should not be read as an equally credentialed member. The max form remains production for its semantics --- the floor as a minimum on \emph{total} turnover, the reading the deep-discount calibration anchors --- and because it is the measured form curve's conservative endpoint (below); the aggregate-level comparison is context for the level, not the selection rule for the marginal's form, since the design does not identify levels. One further run measures the cell the hull leaves implicit. The form and transform dimensions were reported side by side without being crossed, which presumes they add. A pre-committed run of the joint cell (\texttt{concave\_additive\_marginal}) shows they do not. The concave transform under the additive form puts the marginal at $+9.25$ points at the production floor and $+9.22$ at the 4.991\% anchor---inside the hull at both, so the range itself stands---but the interaction is $-1.47$ points at the headline anchor, past the same $\pm 1$-point convention used throughout. The reason is the censoring this paragraph describes: the concave transform costs only $-0.52$ points under the max form, which absorbs most of it into the floor, against $-1.99$ under the additive form, which never censors and so passes the whole transform effect through. Two consequences. A successor run closes that grid (run \texttt{concave\_hull\_band\_ends}): the concave transform's remaining sixteen off-window cells put the form $\times$ transform product at thirty-six of thirty-six, and the hull does not survive intact. Seventeen of the eighteen concave cells fall inside it, but concave under the max form at the highest defensible floor and the band's low edge returns $+3.53$ points, below the $+3.89$ lower edge the log-linear cells set, so the form-conditional hull widens at the bottom to $+3.5$ to $+13.1$ points. Its upper edge is unchanged, because the concave transform is signed downward and its highest cell reaches only $+10.83$. Any reading that composes a form adjustment with a transform adjustment additively is unsupported at the calibration this paper headlines. The form-robust statement of the paper's identified quantity is a lock-in marginal of roughly $+9$ to $+11$ points at the production floor, positive under every level, band, and form combination tested.

Re-running the central and $\beta_1 = 0$ legs with the floor set to each defensible off-window value, under the production convention in every other respect (the fresh runs reproduce the committed production artifacts exactly at the 4\% floor: central \$818.530 billion, null \$748.185 billion, marginal $+\$70.345$ billion, floor-bind share 36\% of 1,683,124 loan-months), gives Table~\ref{tab:oosfloor}. Across the 2018 leg's defensible band the marginal runs $+6.8$ to $+4.3$ points ($+\$51.8$ to $+\$32.6$ billion), centered at $+5.6$ points ($+\$42.6$ billion) at that leg's mid-grid 4.99\% reading, and $+3.9$ to $+7.5$ points once the full Liebersohn--Rothstein band is swept jointly with it. The contaminated anchors are what produce the small numbers: the pooled 6.07\% read (evaluated at the box's adjacent 6.0\% row) gives $+2.3$ points, and the 2019 leg's 6.91\% read gives $+0.7$. The direction of the in-window concession stands---an off-window floor is higher than the in-window 4\%, and because the marginal decreases in the floor, a higher anchor shrinks the identified contribution rather than reversing it---but the conclusion that the plausible contribution therefore sits at the calibration box's lower edge does not. That lower edge was an artifact of the refinancing-contaminated pooled read; the defensible off-window floor places the contribution in the box's interior, at roughly $+5.6$ points, not at $+2.1$ to $+2.4$. Every read in that band is taken on cohort-months aged twelve to twenty-four, the only ages the 2018 leg contains, so the leg's mature-seasoning test is not computable rather than passed (Appendix~\ref{app:floormech}); where the mature contrast is measurable it runs one way in every cell (Section~\ref{sec:identification}), which puts the band below the mature book's baseline turnover and the marginal it implies above what that book would give.

The move from the in-window anchor to the off-window one changes two things at once, and until now the paper charged both to the window. The in-window anchor is measured at gap $\leq -2$ points and reads 3.972\%; the off-window anchor is measured at gap $\leq -0.25$ points and reads 4.991\%. Mapping both through the committed floor-to-marginal grid (PCHIP on the frozen sweep, not re-derived, and reproducing the committed grid to within \$0.69 billion at every read inside it), that is a move of $-\$28.312$ billion, or $-3.702$ points. Decomposed at matched depth, the move splits: the window component, measured with depth held at gap $\leq -0.25$ points on both sides, is $-\$20.874$ billion ($-2.729$ points, 73.7\% of the move); the depth component, measured within the in-window panel by moving from gap $\leq -2$ points to gap $\leq -0.25$ points, is $-\$7.438$ billion ($-0.973$ points, 26.3\%). Re-anchoring the same decomposition at the deepest depth both panels support (gap $\leq -1$ point) gives 66.9\% window and 33.1\% depth. The window dominates either way, so the demotion is not primarily a depth artifact. But depth owns a real and previously unattributed \$7.4 billion, and the consequence is a correction to how the demotion should be described: at matched depth the in-sample comparison point is $+\$63.5$ billion, not the $+\$70.3$ billion this paper narrates the demotion against. Roughly \$20.9 billion of the \$27.7 billion demotion is the window; the remainder is a change in the discount depth at which the anchor is defined, and has nothing to do with out-of-sampleness. The reverse ordering---window first at matched deep depth, then depth---is not computable: it would have to read the off-window leg at gap $\leq -2$ points, where that leg retains 0.02\% of its exposure, so no average of the two orderings is formed and the depth-first path is reported alone. Extending the 2018 depth ladder from three well-supported depths to five leaves the committed clean band exactly [4.695\%, 5.334\%], because the two additional reads (4.722\% at gap $\leq -0.75$ points, 4.869\% at gap $\leq -1$ point) fall strictly inside it, and the marginal band therefore stays \$33.31 to \$51.75 billion around the \$42.58 billion point (the \$33.31 billion grid-read endpoint and the \$32.6 billion direct-run endpoint quoted earlier differ by the PCHIP grid-read wedge, whose committed maximum is \$0.69 billion). Two further pre-committed measurements discipline this band (run \texttt{floor\_uncertainty}). First, the floor reads themselves carry sampling error the depth cuts do not express: a 1000-replicate stratum-cluster bootstrap puts the clean-band reads' standard errors at 0.39--0.46 points of CPR (31 clusters carry the 2018 leg), and propagating the point anchor's replicates through the committed floor-to-marginal grid gives a percentile 95\% interval of $+3.0$ to $+8.0$ points on the off-window marginal---double the depth-cut band's width, so the binding uncertainty on the headline marginal is the floor's sampling error, not the depth convention (the in-window reads, on 226 clusters, carry standard errors of 0.16--0.19 points). The percentile read under-covers at that cluster count and is demoted; the pre-committed corrections (run \texttt{floor\_inference\_correction}, with the percentile layer reproduced bit-exactly as its parity gate, and its Webb-weight successor \texttt{floor\_inference\_correction\_v2}) widen it mostly at the top, and the corrected ladder, with its degrees of freedom, is Table~\ref{tab:ladder}: the Webb wild-$t$ interval of $+2.9$ to $+8.7$ points is the binding layer, with the lower edge above zero on every corrected read. Second, an age-standardization of the off-window floor---reweighting the 2018 leg's age-bucket reads to the in-window deep-discount population's age mix, with the 2018 leg's empty mature buckets imputed from the in-window age gradient (84.0\% of weight imputed, and labeled as such)---reads 5.51\%, above the clean band's top, implying a marginal near $+3.8$ points: an indicative bound rather than a measurement, but signed one way, so the clean band is open below $+4.3$ and that endpoint is not a conservative floor. This read holds the conventional accounting fixed, and the Ginnie overlay of Section~\ref{sec:pathb} holds the floor at 4.991\%: both correct the same $+5.6$-point cell, both cut the same way, and the joint cell is now run (run \texttt{b5\_joint\_cell}). They do not look like the same adjustment counted twice---one moves the floor's level, the other confines the marginal to the conventional face share, and the overlay's share scaling is identical across the three speed series it was tested on---so carrying that $0.797$ scaling up to the higher floor, which the overlay run does not itself establish, would put the marginal near $+3.0$ points, at the bottom of that sampling interval and below the form-conditional hull's $+3.5$ lower end. That composition is now measured rather than projected: the joint cell returns $+2.9$ points, an interaction of $+0.0$ against the proportional projection --- where the first joint cell this paper ran for two adjustments to the marginal---the concave transform under the additive form, above---returned an interaction of $-1.47$ points at the headline anchor, which is why a composed point here would not be credible.
The same matched-depth machinery undercuts the argument by which the 2019 leg was rejected in Appendix~\ref{app:floormech}, though not the rejection itself. The rejection compares the 2019 leg's gap-$\leq$-0 read of 6.910\% against the retained 2018 leg's gap-$\leq$-0.25-point read of 4.991\%: a 1.919-point spread taken across two different depths, which is not a valid comparison as written. At matched depth the two legs do not converge either. Over the four depths at which both legs clear the 10\% support rule, the 2018-versus-2019 spread runs 1.576, 0.491, 2.112, and 0.857 points---non-monotone in depth, and at half a point the matched-depth spread exceeds the mismatched 1.919 points the earlier argument rested on. So neither ``the legs disagree by 1.9 points'' nor ``the legs agree once depth is matched'' is a defensible summary; the second would be a cherry-pick of one depth in four. The pooled 2017--2019 read is not a third independent leg, since it contains both 2018 and 2019 (and 2017), so any appeal to three legs agreeing double-counts; there are two. And the 2019 leg's own exposure collapses from \$1{,}085 billion at gap $\leq$ 0 to \$199 billion by half a point and \$0.29 billion by one and a half, so its deep reads are noise on the same grounds that disqualify the deep off-window cell. The honest verdict is therefore that the refinancing-contamination story is unadjudicable below gap $\leq$ 0 rather than refuted there: the only depth carrying solid exposure for both legs is gap $\leq$ 0, where 2019 sits 1.576 points above 2018, directionally consistent with refinancing contamination and consistent with the seasoning diagnostic that independently disqualifies the 2019 leg. The conclusion stands; the argument as earlier stated does not, and I state the weaker one.

A second instrument addresses the months dimension directly rather than the population dimension, and it is a floor-stability check rather than an outcome holdout: no realized prepayment from the held-out months enters either simulated leg, so the construction can falsify only the floor calibration's temporal stability, never the marginal against realized data. Calibrating the floor on deeply out-of-the-money turnover confined to the window's first nineteen months (June 2022 through December 2023) returns 3.97\%, and the central-minus-null marginal can then be summed over the remaining twenty-three months (January 2024 through November 2025) alone, which enter the floor's calibration nowhere. That held-out marginal is $+\$45.1$ billion, against $+\$44.8$ billion at the production floor over the identical months: very nearly the magnitude, and with it the sign, survives evaluation months that the calibration never touches. The decomposition is exact rather than approximate because trapped liquidity is a monthly sum against the redemption target, so the target cancels from the central-minus-null difference and the monthly marginals add back to the aggregate. Across every floor and band combination run here---twenty-seven cells spanning floors from 3.97\% to 6.91\%---the marginal is positive without exception, a consistency check the window's rate configuration cannot let fail (Section~\ref{sec:identification}) rather than a second identifying result. The identified content of the lock-in channel is therefore a magnitude that survives both instruments as a bounded interval: $+4.3$ to $+6.8$ points at the defensible off-window floor under the production max form ($+3.9$ to $+7.5$ jointly with the elasticity band), $+9.6$ to $+13.1$ under the additive form at the same anchors, a form-conditional hull of $+3.5$ to $+13.1$ points, against the $+9.2$ points the in-window production calibration returns, which this paper now reports as its in-sample calibration point rather than its headline.


\section{Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}

The shortfall of the Federal Reserve's mortgage runoff beneath its Quantitative Tightening caps was not solely a product of high interest rates, but a consequence of the structural design of the U.S. mortgage contract interacting with a prepayment environment mostly mechanical relative to the caps: under the production floor form the $\beta_1 = 0$ null recovers 85.7\% of the benchmark (35.6\% under the additive form) at the headline calibration under any rate response above the baseline turnover it embeds, and how much of the remainder sits outside household-level decision-making is a question the cross-design test of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign} leaves open. This paper's two central empirical findings, established in Sections~\ref{sec:abm} and~\ref{sec:hazard} under the synthetic household population specified in Section~\ref{sec:method}, are complementary and neither is derived from the other, though they are not independent in calibration: both are anchored to a baseline turnover floor read off discount-cohort turnover, and at the in-sample calibration point to the same 2023--24 read (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}).

The ABM (rational, behaviorally extended, or subjected to a direct burnout test) cannot explain the majority of the Federal Reserve's trapped liquidity, while a structurally distinct hazard framework built entirely from loan-level survival dynamics, with no household utility function at all, recovers 91.3\% of the full empirical benchmark on the benchmark-consistent accounting basis in its microsimulation form at the off-window floor this paper headlines, 88.2--93.7\% across that floor's defensible range, and 97.9\% at the in-sample calibration point, so the miss is 8.7 points on the headline calibration and 2.1 points on the in-sample one (a level-accuracy distinction, not a timing one: no estimator's monthly co-movement survives detrending, Section~\ref{sec:pathb}; the estimated cohort-hazard form lands at a comparable aggregate level but does not corroborate it---its own coefficient's sign is not statistically distinguishable from zero---carries no valid production-spec uncertainty, and is excluded from headline figures, Section~\ref{sec:patha}).

The composition of that recovery, however, disciplines what the contrast can mean: under the production floor form the $\beta_1 = 0$ null recovers 85.7\% on the same basis at the headline floor (35.6\% under the additive form) and 88.7\% in-sample, and the paper's own ablations bound the named loan-level mechanisms (burnout $0.45$ points at the headline floor and $0.86$ in-sample, FICO/LTV covariates $1.3$ points, the delinquency pipeline \$0.39 billion; no servicer channel beyond that pipeline is modeled at all). What delivers the level is scheduled amortization, the seasoning baseline, and the turnover floor: the shortfall against the phased caps is mostly mechanical, because the caps sat far above what any plausible prepayment environment would have delivered---though against the Federal Reserve's own ex-ante projection rather than the non-binding cap, the lock-in channel accounts for roughly half the genuine surprise under the central allocation (23--49\% at the off-window floor and 38--80\% at the in-sample calibration point across the two disclosed intra-2022 allocations; Section~\ref{sec:method-benchmark})---and the lock-in elasticity's contribution is a bounded margin over that mechanical base rather than a pinned one: $+2.9$ to $+8.7$ points once the turnover floor is measured off the locked-in population it is meant to bound and that floor's own sampling error is propagated at the central elasticity with a wild-cluster correction (the percentile read is $+3.0$ to $+8.0$; the interval prices the floor read alone, and the elasticity dimension is Table~\ref{tab:lowband}), with $+5.6$ points the value at the mid-grid anchor inside it and every measurable correction to the floor or the accounting basis moving that value down within the interval, and $+9.2$ points at the in-sample calibration point (Section~\ref{sec:identification}; $+11.2$ under the competing-risks floor form). A separate question (whether the estimator contrast reflects the household-choice paradigm itself or the synthetic population it was tested on) receives an asymmetric answer in Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign}: the hazard framework's aggregate recovery survives a fully synthetic population, while the ABM's share moves with calibration choice and data source, its recalibrated real-covariate variant reaching 59.3\% (76.3\% at the book's composition), above the pre-committed threshold at which Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign} concludes the contrast cannot, on that test alone, be cleanly attributed to modeling paradigm; and the path-level timing question remains open for every estimator.

Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}'s Danish counterfactual retires a superseded figure. The mechanism-extrapolated specification implied a \$925.5 billion institutional gap; recalibrating against \citepos{berger2026} separately-estimated Danish elasticities collapses it by an order of magnitude, and anchoring the transplant so that it changes only the payoff rule (the anchor consistent with the baseline turnover floor's semantics) signs it: $+\$61.2$ billion at the band's zero-refinance edge, 8\% of the benchmark, equal in origin to the lock-in marginal the paper identifies directly, and in size to that marginal at the in-sample calibration point on which both legs were run---though about 44\% larger than the off-window marginal the paper headlines; at the off-window floor itself the rule-only gap is $+\$28.2$ billion, 34\% below that marginal, and subject to the same payoff-credit incidence as the in-sample figure, since that leg too credits retired balance at par (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}). The sign is anchored rather than estimated: pinning the Danish leg at the U.S. zero-gap hazard guarantees it against a book that is out of the money almost everywhere, and the refinance-in-place sweep can only reinforce it, while the bracketing anchor that also imports Danish baseline mobility reverses it to $-\$99.9$ billion---on a Danish leg whose 3.39\% mean prepayment sits below the turnover floor the U.S. legs impose, which is the inconsistency that demoted it to a bracket. This does not disturb Sections~\ref{sec:abm} and~\ref{sec:hazard}'s central contrast, which never depended on the Danish comparison, but it leaves the payoff-rule question at a modest magnitude and a stated anchor rather than an unidentifiable sign: a Danish-style payoff rule would have relieved a real but small share of the Federal Reserve's cash-flow problem, not the \$925.5 billion an extrapolated mechanism once implied. One qualification travels with that magnitude, and it is now priced as a bracket rather than left open. The Danish leg credits retired balance at par, so the cash a market-value buyback would \emph{not} return to the Federal Reserve is absent from it; on the paper's own pricing proxy that channel exceeds the gap and moves it toward zero (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}). Because the pipeline cannot distinguish a cash haircut from a balance adjustment, the pre-committed bracket of Section~\ref{sec:pathb} (run \texttt{buyback\_credit\_bracket}) prices both: the gap stands at $+\$61.2$ billion under the balance-adjustment reading and reverses to $-\$89.4$ to $-\$117.7$ billion under the cash-haircut reading at the paper's own proxy discounts, so the honest reading is that the relief stands on the benchmark's own face denomination, while as reserve drain the rule costs the Federal Reserve exactly the par windfall it would otherwise have extracted from moving households---legs (2) and (3) of the trade-off are, on that reading, the same dollars seen from two sides. The mobility relief such a rule would provide remains the better-supported and larger effect.

A plausible reason the U.S. does not simply adopt the Danish payoff rule is the remaining leg of the trade-off, secondary-market liquidity: the Danish market-value repurchase system is difficult to reconcile with the U.S. fixed-income market as presently structured. The Danish system relies on strict one-to-one match funding (the Balance Principle); the United States relies on the To-Be-Announced (TBA) forward market, which requires highly homogenized, pooled mortgages to generate secondary-market liquidity. \citet{vickery2013} show that this homogeneity produces a measurable liquidity premium: TBA-eligible mortgage pools trade at significantly lower cost than otherwise comparable specified pools ineligible for TBA delivery. \citet{gao2017} measure the same differential directly in transaction data, and \citet{campbell2013} frames the U.S.--Danish contrast as a mortgage-design problem in exactly these terms. A market-value repurchase feature of the kind used in Denmark prices each payoff at the market value of the funding bond series --- a series-level price common to every borrower funded by that series on a given day, not a borrower-specific value --- so what it strains is not homogeneity itself but its \emph{locus}: Denmark standardizes at the level of large, tap-issued bond series under one-to-one match funding, where the TBA market standardizes at the level of the deliverable pool. The Danish callable market's own depth shows that market-value redemption and liquid secondary trading coexist under the series convention \citep{berg2018}; the binding cost for the United States is migrating its issuance architecture from pool-level to series-level standardization, not a liquidity impossibility. Adopting the Danish payoff rule without that broader redesign of agency securitization rules would therefore risk sacrificing a documented source of secondary-market liquidity in the transition. A hedging-side literature sharpens what the two payoff rules allocate. In the U.S. structure, prepayment risk is borne and priced by specialized MBS investors rather than by diversified households \citep{gabaix2007}, and the aggregate hedging of the duration swings that prepayment creates feeds back into the level and volatility of long-term rates \citep{malkhozov2016}; the behavioral component of prepayment is the part that cannot be hedged away, since behavioral uncertainty renders the market for the embedded option incomplete and even robust replication strategies can only bound the resulting exposure \citep{perotti2024}. The Danish market-value rule does not remove that behavioral residual (the borrower retains the par call on the rate-decline side), but it removes the rate-rise extension leg on which this cycle's exposure accrued, so the choice between payoff rules is also a choice about which side of the convexity profile the imperfectly hedgeable behavioral risk sits on, and at what feedback cost to the yield curve.

Ultimately, U.S. policymakers weigh three considerations, and this paper's evidence, under its production floor form (the additive form roughly doubles the margin at issue), is that they do not form the strict trilemma a three-way ``trade-off'' would imply: (1) deep, homogenized secondary market liquidity (the TBA market); (2) continuous household mobility (avoiding the lock-in effect); and (3) convexity-neutral duration profiles (predictable institutional cash flows). The evidence of Sections~\ref{sec:abm} and~\ref{sec:hazard} is that the U.S. has prioritized (1) at real cost to (2): household mobility is genuinely constrained, while the cash-flow shortfall against the caps is mostly mechanical --- a composition the Federal Reserve staff's own record and the ex-ante projection corroborate as composition rather than as numerical agreement, and not independently of it, since the projection may already have embedded part of the channel (Section~\ref{sec:lit}) --- with the lock-in channel contributing an identified margin of $+2.9$ to $+8.7$ points at the off-window floor, $+5.6$ at the mid-grid anchor inside it ($+9.2$ at the in-sample calibration point). What I no longer find support for is the assumption that (2) and (3) trade off sharply under face accounting, in which fixing mobility would cost face-value cash flow: under the rule-only transplant, Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}'s recalibrated Danish counterfactual finds a market-value repurchase rule would have relieved (2), while its effect on (3) is incidence-conditional: an $\approx\$61$ billion improvement under face accounting (the figure that images the lock-in marginal at the production floor on which both were run), a $-\$89.4$-to-$-\$117.7$ billion cost under cash accounting at the committed proxy discounts, that cost being the foregone household-to-bondholder transfer (run \texttt{buyback\_credit\_bracket})---so this leg-(2)/(3) reading inherits the marginal's floor-and-band conditionality (Section~\ref{sec:identification}): its magnitude ranges with the $+2.1$-to-$+13.2$-point calibration sweep, it is stated at the in-sample calibration point rather than the off-window floor the headline marginal now uses, and its (3) sign is robust to the floor but not to the payoff credit's incidence, nor to bundling Danish baseline mobility into the transplant (Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}). What this dissolution rests on is thinner than the framing suggests, so I state it plainly. The rule-only Danish gap is the lock-in marginal viewed from the counterfactual side, so ``(2) and (3) do not trade off sharply'' reduces to ``the marginal is small''---and under the production floor form the marginal is bounded small by construction; under the disclosed additive form, which never censors the elasticity, the same counterfactual is about $+11$ points and nearly floor-invariant (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}). Because \eqref{eq:pathB} combines the turnover floor with the voluntary hazard by a hard maximum, switching the elasticity off can raise the hazard by no more than the excess of the $\beta_1 = 0$ hazard over the floor, and by nothing at all where the floor binds on the $\beta_1 = 0$ leg itself, 14.3\% of loan-months at the in-sample calibration point (Section~\ref{sec:identification}). The dissolution is therefore a statement about a calibrated ceiling, not an independent finding, and it is form-conditional. What survives across forms is that the trade-off is modest, not that it is absent. The binding constraint against adopting such a rule is therefore, on either form, not a mobility--cash-flow trade-off but the TBA liquidity premium described above. That last step is an argument from the cited literature rather than a result of this design: nothing here prices a series-level call, so the investor-side cost of the transplant is named and left unpriced, and the constraint is asserted on others' evidence, not on mine. This is the two-sided exchange the paper opened with: (1) purchased in the currency of (2), with a small side payment in (3) whose sign depends on the buyback's incidence. As long as the U.S. maintains the par-payoff MBS structure within the TBA framework, severe duration extension should be expected to recur in future tightening cycles that begin, as this one did, with a large outstanding stock of deeply in-the-money, below-market-coupon mortgages (the state dependence documented by \citet{berger2021} and \citet{eichenbaum2022} makes the extension conditional on that stock, not on tightening per se; the recurrence claim is itself an extrapolation beyond what this design tests, which is one episode). The duration extension itself, however, is mostly the mechanical arithmetic of scheduled amortization and baseline turnover against caps set above any plausible prepayment path; an alternative payoff rule would have trimmed it by the lock-in margin, not eliminated it, and would carry the liquidity cost above.

\subsection{Limitations and Avenues for Future Research}\label{sec:limitations}

Several limitations remain on both sides of the framework.

\textbf{Housing supply as a competing channel}: The mobility margin this paper imports is estimated in an environment of historically tight for-sale inventory, and housing supply enters here only as a friction scaler on the moving decision, never as a channel in its own right. A household that does not move because nothing is listed is observationally identical, in these data, to one that does not move because its coupon is below market, and this design does not separate them. The direction is argued rather than measured: a binding supply constraint would absorb some of what is attributed to lock-in, which is why the scaled-null exercise that removes the non-rate share of the moving decision is reported alongside the headline rather than as a robustness footnote (Section~\ref{sec:identification}; \citealp{aladangady2024,hsieh2019}). No inventory series is used to price it.

\textbf{Distributional silence}: This paper measures a book-wide institutional cost and never asks who bears it. Ginnie Mae borrowers---20.4\% of SOMA face, and the population whose structurally faster speeds Section~\ref{sec:pathb} documents---sit outside the Freddie Mac sample entirely, so nothing here establishes whether the mobility cost falls differently on FHA and VA households than on the conventional borrowers the sample contains. I record that as a distributional limitation of the design, not merely as a coverage bound on the estimate.

\textbf{Sample scale and comparability}: The ABM's 10,000-household synthetic population and the hazard framework's 75,000-loan real Freddie Mac sample differ in both size and data source. Section~\ref{sec:robustness-scale} settles the size question on the production specification (population size changes precision, not central tendency), and the completed cross-design pair of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign} addresses the data-source question asymmetrically: the hazard framework's aggregate recovery is largely data-source-independent, while the ABM's is not.

\textbf{Data:} Freddie Mac 2017--2021 loan-level data (20 origination quarters), used for both hazard paths, is integrated via a dedicated ingest pipeline; where real data files are unavailable, results are validated against a synthetic fixture before real-data estimation. Fifteen-year MBS, approximately 9\% of SOMA face value, remain excluded from cohort modeling on the hazard side only; the ABM side folds them in (Appendix~\ref{sec:robustness-pipeline}).

\textbf{Model:} On the ABM side, the monthly CPR path fit remains weak (raw $R^2$ of $-6.984$) despite a directionally sensible level response to rates, and the production Danish counterfactual is a rule-only transplant onto U.S. households (Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}): it does not estimate how Danish institutional frictions would themselves migrate, and the Danish-level bracketing anchor shows the gap's sign is not robust to bundling baseline-mobility differences into the transplant. The debt-to-income check remains front-end only, without a measure of total household debt. On the hazard side, Path A's Poisson pseudo-maximum-likelihood estimator requires a mild ridge penalty to remain well-conditioned at 295 stratum fixed effects (a stabilization device: Section~\ref{sec:patha} shows the prior-spec (v3) coefficients equal the unpenalized fit on the estimable strata), and its holdout RMSE (spec v3: approximately 38 percentage points unweighted, 2.5 points exposure-weighted; the frozen spec v4 fit re-scored on the same holdout: 37.9 and 3.0) together with a lag-0 CPR correlation of $-0.378$, more negative even than the ABM's $-0.318$, confines its evidential contribution to the aggregate dollar level, as Section~\ref{sec:patha} details; Path B's literature-calibrated hazard magnitudes, while directionally and quantitatively closer to the empirical benchmark than any ABM specification, are simulated on the 75,000-loan real Freddie Mac sample with elasticities calibrated from secondary literature rather than estimated jointly with the empirical benchmark itself, and its recovery percentage is reported across the calibration band (105.9\%--108.2\%) and now also with loan-level sampling uncertainty from an ex-ante-specified stratified bootstrap over the 75,000-loan draw (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}). An external second-agency replication (Fannie Mae loan-level data, same pipeline end-to-end) reproduces the lock-in marginal at $+8.68$ points against Freddie's $+9.20$, inside its pre-committed envelope: a sign-and-envelope replication, not a hazard-level match, and one whose envelope gate is 11.06 points wide, so the 0.52-point agreement, rather than the gate, is what should be read from it (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}). One global fact about the design, each of whose components is disclosed only locally: no outcome-holdout months exist anywhere in this paper. The nearest construction is the marginal's temporal holdout (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}), in which the turnover floor is calibrated on the window's first nineteen months and the central-minus-null difference is then summed over the remaining twenty-three alone, months that enter that calibration nowhere; it returns $+\$45.1$ billion. That construction holds for the marginal, not for the level, and it is an input-stability check rather than an outcome validation: realized prepayment from the held-out months enters neither simulated leg, so it cannot falsify the marginal against data, and the statement above stands without exception. The production baseline turnover floor is still calibrated on 2023--24 turnover inside the evaluation window (the pre-committed off-window re-measurement of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor} finds the defensible off-window level higher than 4\%, so the direction of any anchoring error shrinks rather than inflates the identified marginal, and the headline marginal is now reported at that off-window level rather than the in-window one); Path A trains on 19 of the 42 QT months and its ridge-selection holdout doubles as its out-of-sample diagnostic; and Path B's \emph{level}, as distinct from its marginal, is still set by a floor measured in the window on which it is evaluated. Every recovery percentage in the paper is accordingly an in-sample or calibrated quantity in the months dimension, disciplined by ex-ante-specified sweeps, nulls, parity gates, and bootstraps rather than by clean holdout months. All headline figures are frozen in tagged run manifests, with reproduction details recorded in the run ledger (Appendix~\ref{app:ledger}). The marginal itself, at either floor, is a model-based decomposition conditional on the disclosed hazard structure (Section~\ref{sec:identification}): causal identification in the econometric sense would require exogenous variation in rate gaps across otherwise-comparable loans, which this design does not exploit.

\textbf{Status:} The Danish-regime recalibration (Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}) calibrates both frameworks' Danish CPR response against \citepos{berger2026} separately-estimated moving and refinancing elasticities rather than an extrapolated U.S. mechanism, and anchors the rule-only transplant at the U.S. zero-gap intercept, collapsing the institutional gap by an order of magnitude to a signed $+\$61.2$ billion. Path B's full 5.5\%--7.7\% elasticity band is propagated through the microsimulation (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}), an exercise that also surfaced and corrected a units error in the rate-gap coefficient's application (Appendix~\ref{sec:robustness-pipeline}). The symmetric companion cross-design test is complete (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-synthesis}): the hazard framework's survival structure recovers 106.0\% of the benchmark on a fully synthetic population, closely matching its real-data recovery, while its path-level timing does not transfer to synthetic data. Four items are bound to the pre-submission freeze as a hard gate: (i) executed: the calendar-month specification of Section~\ref{sec:patha} is production Path A as of this freeze, with Table~\ref{tab:estimators} and the WAL table restated under it, while the coefficient-uncertainty table and the refit-and-resimulate interval remain labeled under the prior spec v3 because bootstrap replications under the seasonal design are branch-unstable (Section~\ref{sec:patha}; on the production panel no start reaches a better penalized objective, run \texttt{pathA\_cold\_starts}); (ii) executed for the LaTeX master: it carries no hardcoded cross-references (machine-checked by the replication package's liveness gates), builds clean with zero unresolved references or citations, and the build log ships with the submission bundle; the Word REF-field conversion applies when a Word submission master is cut from this source; (iii) executed: a claims-versus-code liveness audit ran at the freeze (77 production-mechanism claims traced adversarially to their executing code paths; the nine that did not trace as written are corrected in this draft or scoped to their frozen specifications, and the audit artifact ships in the replication package)---the defect class earlier caught in the Danish counterfactual's description; and (iv) the timing-sweep generator's golden-fixture test must pass at the freeze, three matcher generations having each produced a distinct silent defect. What remains open beyond that gate: adding a seasonal multiplier to Path B's baseline hazard (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}); a total-debt measure, or threshold sensitivity, for the ABM's front-end DTI gate (Appendix~\ref{sec:method-frictions}); and the monthly-frequency question the timing diagnosis leaves (the empirical path's variation at that frequency is seasonal, not rate-driven, and no estimator here models it). The second-agency replication is complete (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}; the envelope caveat is in the Model paragraph above). The cyclical-floor variant is likewise complete, with a correction to how its output should be read: a floor co-varying within the run with the window-standardized mortgage rate, nesting the production specification at $\kappa = 0$, moves the marginal between $+7.1$ and $+9.4$ points across $\kappa \in [-0.5, +0.5]$, every cell inside the constant-floor sweep of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}; but 85.0\% of that span survives scrambling the floor path's time order and 98.0\% of it vanishes under the additive combination rule, so the span measures floor dispersion under the hard maximum rather than cyclicality (Section~\ref{sec:pathb} and Appendix~\ref{sec:robustness-seasonalfloor}). The co-movement component that survives is real, monotone in $|\kappa|$, and at most \$5.29 billion. The Ginnie composition bound has been upgraded from two published snapshots to a time-varying overlay built from the published monthly CPR series, which leaves the conventional-share marginal at $+7.3$ points, positive under every variant, with the Ginnie-specific level contribution inside the static bound (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}). Replication alone could not retire the vintage residual: the 2022 and pre-2017 vintages (23.1\% and 10.6\% of book face) lie outside the 2017--2021 universe of both agencies' samples used here. The observed-speed bound of Section~\ref{sec:pathb} now disciplines that extrapolation directly (\$11.7 billion, 1.5\% of benchmark, signed toward overstating trapped liquidity), so what remains open on this dimension is no longer the absence of a bound but the bound's own proxy assumption: observed Fannie segment speeds standing in for the SOMA book's, the same single-agency posture the production calibration itself carries. A modeled overlay in place of that proxy is not constructible under the committed sampler: the staged replication sample enforces the 2017--2021 vintage universe, the pre-QT snapshot rule leaves second-half-2022 originations with no admissible observation month, and the acquisition window closes at 2022Q4, so 2022 originations acquired in 2023 were never staged; the 2022 leg of the observed-speed bound (\$3.3 billion of the \$11.7 billion) therefore stands as that vintage's discipline. Appendix~\ref{app:composition} collects the formal composition comparison behind these bounds: sample versus book by agency, vintage, coupon, and term, with a cross-agency stability check on the dimensions the book's CUSIP-level disclosure cannot supply.

Sections~\ref{sec:abm} and~\ref{sec:hazard} establish that contrast under the synthetic household population this paper specifies. Section~\ref{sec:robustness}'s cross-design pair leaves the attribution asymmetric and inherits Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign}'s own hedge: the hazard framework's recovery survives a fully synthetic population (106.0\% versus 107.0\%), evidence that its survival structure, not its data source, carries the aggregate level, but the recalibrated real-covariate ABM's 59.3\%, and its 60.2\% fifty-seed mean, cross the pre-committed threshold above which the contrast cannot, on that test alone, be cleanly attributed to modeling paradigm rather than to calibration and data source; the path-level timing question remains open for every estimator. That result cuts both ways: recovering 106\% on a fully synthetic population shows the calibration constants, not real loan-level structure, deliver the level, which is consistent with the mechanical decomposition above rather than with a loan-level-mechanisms reading.

\section{Superseded Figures and Run Ledger}\label{app:ledger}

\paragraph{Data and code availability.} The replication package is the public repository at \url{https://github.com/eugeneoCMU/Lock-in-Effect}. It ships all estimation, simulation, and figure code; the frozen 75{,}000-loan Freddie sample and the cohort-month panel that every production run consumes; every committed results artifact this paper cites (the results JSONs, the production coefficient artifact, the exposure-weighted delinquency-transition matrix); the tagged run manifests with SHA-256 pins on their artifact files; and the machine-checked liveness gates and the claims-versus-code audit artifact. Hazards are estimated on the Freddie Mac Single-Family Loan-Level Dataset, which Freddie Mac makes publicly available at no cost after registration; the raw origination and performance files are not redistributed, and the committed code rebuilds every derived object from them under fixed seeds (engine seed 42 with per-regime offsets; every post-freeze run's specification is fixed ex ante in its script header, the convention this ledger records). Macro and portfolio inputs are the public FRED and New York Fed SOMA interfaces; a user-supplied FRED key is the only credential involved. Fannie Mae inputs are code-only, as Section~\ref{sec:pathb-fannie} states: no Fannie data and no derived cells ship, and the committed replication artifact carries headline statistics alone.

Headline figures in this paper changed across drafts as specification errors were found and corrected. Because some superseded values appear in earlier internal drafts and in the accompanying technical documentation, I catalogue them here rather than in scattered footnotes, so that a reader consulting both always knows which figure superseded which, and why.

\begingroup\sloppy
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{U.S. CPR 12.3\%, empirical CPR 5.45\%, wedge 35.1pp}: produced by a window filter that inadvertently included eight post-QT months; superseded by the bounded active-QT window (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-benchmark}).
\item \textbf{17.6pp wedge; 21--27\% Danish CPR}: a pre-multi-cohort, rational-only calibration; superseded by the multi-vintage production specification.
\item \textbf{\$101.2 billion / 13.2\% ABM estimate} (\texttt{run-2026-07-04}): the pre-fold-in, 30-year-only tag, raw path $R^2 = -6.443$; superseded by the 15-year fold-in production tag \texttt{run-2026-07-04-15yr-foldin} (\$91.0 billion / 11.9\%, $R^2 = -6.984$; Appendix~\ref{sec:robustness-pipeline}).
\item \textbf{Danish CPR 47.1\%; wedge $\approx$35.5pp; institutional gap \$925.5 billion}: the mechanism-extrapolated Danish specification; the 35.5pp wedge is the difference between its 47.1\% Danish and 11.68\% U.S. mean CPR. Superseded by the Berger-recalibrated \texttt{run-2026-07-05-berger} (Danish CPR $\approx$3.4\%; institutional gap $-\$728.4$ billion against that run's own \$84.5 billion U.S. leg; Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}).
\item \textbf{``recovers 97.9\%, within 2.1\% of the benchmark'' as an unqualified headline}: the recovery level and the lock-in marginal were quoted at different turnover-floor calibrations after the marginal was demoted to the off-window floor. Both are retained, each labelled with its floor: 91.3\% recovery and an 8.7-point miss at the headline off-window floor (88.2--93.7\% across its range), 97.9\% and 2.1 points at the in-sample calibration point (\texttt{calibration\_reconciliation}). The related 0.2-point agreement between the null's 88.7\% and the Fed's 88.5\% ex-ante projection share is retracted as a corroborating coincidence: it requires both the in-sample floor and the uniform-spread allocation.

\item \textbf{18.3-point real-book central-minus-null gap}: differenced a standalone-scorer central leg against a shared-basis null. The single-basis gap is 9.2 points in-sample and 5.6 points at the off-window floor.

\item \textbf{Path B \$747 billion / 97.7\%}: the pre-unit-correction microsimulation, in which the calibrated lock-in elasticity was numerically inert; superseded by \$818.5 billion / 107.0\% (Appendix~\ref{sec:robustness-pipeline}). Its corrected analogue, the deliberate $\beta_1 = 0$ null of Section~\ref{sec:hazard-interp}, recovers 97.8\%.
\item \textbf{Path A \$915.0 billion / 119.7\%} (spec v3; $r = -0.444$ at lag 0, peak $-3$): the prior production specification, which omitted calendar-month seasonality; superseded by the calendar-month spec v4 adopted at the pre-submission freeze on its timing behaviour, the peak moving to $-2$; the recovery difference between the two specifications is not calendar-attributable and no part of the adoption case rests on it (\$928.9 billion / 121.5\%; \texttt{run-2026-07-14-pathA-seasonal}; Section~\ref{sec:patha}).
\item \textbf{Hybrid Danish gap $-\$99.9$ billion, ``sign not robustly identified''} (Danish-level anchor; Danish mean CPR 3.39\%): superseded as the production reading by the U.S.-intercept transplant (\texttt{danish\_us\_intercept}: gap $+\$61.2$ billion, Danish mean CPR 5.61\%, positive at every refinance-in-place sweep point, that positivity being forced by the zero-gap anchor; the U.S. leg reproduces the committed central artifacts exactly). The Danish-level rows are retained in Table~\ref{tab:danish} as a labeled rule-plus-country bracketing case; their Danish mean CPRs sit below the 4\% turnover floor, the inconsistency that motivated the restatement (Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}).
\item \textbf{Post-freeze robustness artifacts}: the floor functional-form test (\texttt{hazard/floor\_form\_results.json}: additive-form marginal $+11.25$pp at the production floor, $+11.29$ to $+11.21$ across 3--5\% floors; max-form parity pair reproduces the committed artifacts exactly; Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}) and the Path B stratified loan-level bootstrap (\texttt{hazard/bootstrap\_pathb\_results.json}; Section~\ref{sec:pathb}). Neither changes a production headline; both are specified ex ante in their script headers, written before the runs executed. The seasonal-floor run follows the same convention (\texttt{hazard/seasonal\_floor\_timing\_results.json} and the placebo scoring in \texttt{hazard/data/b3\_timing\_scores.json}; Appendix~\ref{sec:robustness-seasonalfloor}), and carries one correction to its own provenance: an earlier module docstring described the twelve-cell calendar profile as estimated in-window, when it reproduces on the full cohort-month panel. Both bases are recorded in the artifact, the full-panel basis drives every run, and no figure elsewhere in this paper consumed the profile.
\item \textbf{``log-normal mobility-desire heterogeneity'' (scale-sensitivity description)}: the production ABM draws mobility desire from an exponential distribution on a dollar scale; the log-normal description was an error of description, not of code, caught by an adversarial claims-versus-code check and corrected in place (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-scale}).
\end{itemize}
\endgroup

Every current headline figure cites a frozen, tagged run manifest; two freezes ran on uncommitted working trees, so manifest git hashes alone do not name an exact reproduction checkout, and the repository's run ledger records the exact reproduction commits. The one that carries a headline quantity should be named here rather than left to the ledger: the fold-in Monte Carlo behind the ABM's seed distribution (\texttt{run-2026-07-04-15yr-foldin}) was frozen about two minutes before its own code was committed, so its manifest's commit field points at the \emph{previous} state of the tree. Its reproduction checkout is recorded in the ledger, and on that run, specifications must be identified by cohort-bucket count---seven for the thirty-year-only specification, eleven for the fold-in---rather than by the manifest commit, which is unreliable for exactly this reason. Anyone reproducing the ABM's 13.6\% seed mean or its 11.9\% frozen draw needs that rule, and it is the reason the two figures were once mistakenly attributed to the native-fifteen-year specification.

\paragraph{Reproducibility conventions.} Provenance details referenced from the main text are collected here. The ABM headline run's manifest lives at \path{abm/data/runs/run-2026-07-04-15yr-foldin/manifest.json} and is reproduced via \texttt{freeze\_run.py -{}-tag run-2026-07-04-15yr-foldin} against the fold-in specification's code; a manifest's recorded \texttt{git\_commit} is the pre-run parent of the freezing commit (the convention the replication package's technical documentation records). Post-freeze runs follow the spec-before-run convention: the grid, parity gates, and interpretive thresholds are fixed in the run script's header and committed before execution (for the floor sweeps, spec commits \texttt{ad52db6} and \texttt{651d1a0} precede result commits \texttt{d0ef130} and \texttt{57181b3}); where script and artifact enter the repository in one revision (the form test \texttt{hazard/floor\_form\_test.py}, the bootstrap), the header is the ex-ante record. Referenced artifact files not named in the main text: \texttt{wal\_anchor\_results.json} (WAL anchor), \texttt{floor\_sweep\_results.json} and \texttt{floor\_band\_cross\_results.json} (the calibration box), \texttt{theil\_data.json} (dynamic-fit decomposition), \texttt{vintage\_residual\_bound\_results.json} (vintage bound), and \texttt{ml\_comparator\_holdout\_results.json} (flexible-learner comparator).
