\section{Agent-Based Model: Specification}\label{sec:method-abm}

I adopt an agent-based approach rather than a representative-agent framework because the phenomenon under study is heterogeneous: households differ in coupon, loan age, and exposure to search and sentiment frictions, and these differences interact non-linearly with the level of interest rates. \citet{geanakoplos2012} argue for, and demonstrate the feasibility of, agent-based models of mortgage prepayment in environments where behavioral and search frictions vary across the population and over time, a property directly relevant here since the frictions I model (housing search costs and consumer sentiment) are themselves time-varying. The broader methodological case for agent-based computational economics in settings with meaningful heterogeneity is set out in \citet{tesfatsion2006}.

The ABM simulates a population of N = 10,000 heterogeneous households. Initial agent endowments (income, home value) are drawn from log-normal distributions calibrated to Federal Reserve Economic Data (FRED) medians. In the production multi-vintage specification of Appendix~\ref{sec:method-frictions}, each household's mortgage coupon and origination date are drawn from the Federal Reserve's actual holdings composition: the live CUSIP-level SOMA parse of Section~\ref{sec:abm-results}, across seven coupon cohorts. A single-cohort variant, used for exposition and mechanism analysis, instead endows every agent with a 30-year fixed-rate mortgage at a 3.0\% coupon aged 60 months at the simulation start; where the text below discusses a single representative coupon, it refers to this pedagogical case, not to the production population.

Section~\ref{sec:robustness} reports a scale-sensitivity replication showing that the gap in explanatory power is not an artifact of sample size; whether it reflects modeling paradigm or data source is taken up in Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign}.

The decision to relocate turns on a comparison of the utility of moving against the aggregate financial penalty of doing so. The monthly payment is calculated via the standard annuity formula
\begin{equation}\label{eq:annuity}
M = P\,\frac{r\,(1+r)^{n}}{(1+r)^{n}-1},
\end{equation}
where $P$ is the outstanding principal, $r$ is the monthly interest rate, and $n$ is the remaining term in months. The mobility penalty evaluates \eqref{eq:annuity} twice: once at the borrower's locked-in note rate on the original principal and term, which gives the current payment (by the amortization identity, equal to the payment on the outstanding balance at the note rate over the remaining term), and once at the prevailing market rate on the outstanding balance over a fresh full original term---the replacement loan re-amortizes, a term-preference persistence the code implements deliberately---which gives the payment the borrower would take on by refinancing, so the penalty scales with the difference between the two; a native short-term variant of the penalty, active only for terms at or below 240 months, floors payment decreases at zero and is specified with the full decision rule in Appendix~\ref{app:abmspec}. Here $r$ is expressed in decimal monthly terms (the annual note rate divided by twelve; a 3.0\% coupon gives $r = 0.0025$), a rate \emph{level} distinct in both role and units from the annualized rate \emph{gaps} $g_{i,t}$ (in 100 bp units) and $\tilde g_{i,t} = g_{i,t}/100$ (decimal) that drive the hazard framework of Section~\ref{sec:pathb}; no rate symbol is reused across the two. Across a three-dimensional grid of market rate, dynamic friction, and rate velocity (and, in the multi-vintage specification, across seven coupon cohorts), the population's move/stay decisions are evaluated household-by-household at each grid point and aggregated to a pool-level annualized Conditional Prepayment Rate (CPR) surface (CPR is a pool-level convention; the household-level object is an annualized move intensity, consumed by the accounting layer at one-twelfth per month, Appendix~\ref{app:abmspec}), which is precomputed once and interpolated month-by-month against the live population of simulated market conditions, rather than re-solved via per-month household loops. This reduces the cost of the full grid by roughly two orders of magnitude and makes fifty-seed Monte Carlo estimation feasible. The surface stores each node's direct household-by-household evaluation, so interpolation reproduces the micro-simulation exactly at grid points; the operative approximation is the trilinear segment between 50 bp-spaced nodes, and a spot-check validates it directly: after an on-grid parity gate reproduced the committed surface values, direct micro-simulation and interpolation at worst-case off-grid midpoints differ by at most 0.23 points of CPR away from the freeze threshold, with the Danish column exact. The one systematic difference is the wait-and-see freeze itself: the engine applies it as a step at the 150 bp trigger while the surface renders it as a ramp across the adjacent velocity nodes, which binds in four realized months (August--November 2022; SOMA-weighted mean absolute difference 0.22 points of CPR over the window): surface semantics that the production figures share, and whose trapped-liquidity consequence the binary-step leg of Appendix~\ref{sec:robustness-extensions}'s freeze sweep prices directly. Figure~\ref{fig:cprsurface} renders one slice of that surface---the 3.0\% cohort of the expository variant---with the realized QT window located on it.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.85\textwidth]{fig8_cpr_surface}
\caption{The precomputed CPR surface: the production surface's slice at the 3.0\% thirty-year SOMA cohort, at its parsed seasoning. Left: the U.S. par-payoff surface over the market-rate $\times$ dynamic-friction grid (no-freeze regime), with the 42 QT-window months overlaid at their realized (rate, friction) coordinates; the window sits entirely in the deep out-of-the-money corner, near the baseline turnover floor. Right: rate slices at the friction grid point nearest the window mean. Velocity enters the grid only through the wait-and-see freeze of Appendix~\ref{sec:method-frictions} (20\% of otherwise-mobile households held in place above a 150 bp trailing six-month rise), so the two regimes shown are its entire velocity structure; the Berger-recalibrated Danish counterfactual (Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}) is near-flat, moving roughly a quarter point across the grid. The U.S. slice is identical across the frozen production surface and the current code head; the Danish curve is the Berger production specification.}
\label{fig:cprsurface}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.9\textwidth]{fig10_abm_cpr_paths}
\caption{Production ABM simulated CPR against the empirical SOMA back-out over the QT window (frozen fold-in run, seed 42). The simulated path runs at roughly twice the empirical level and does not track its month-to-month shape; the annotated means, lag-0 $r$, and $R^2$ are the manifest values quoted in the text. This is the level-and-shape mismatch behind the negative path statistics; the cross-correlation structure across lags is in Figure~\ref{fig:ccf}.}
\label{fig:abmpaths}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.82\textwidth]{monte_carlo_trapped_liquidity}
\caption{Monte Carlo population-draw distribution of ABM trapped liquidity (50 seeds, fold-in specification; CPR surface rebuilt per draw). The seed-level dispersion (SD \$24.5 billion) dwarfs the distance between the frozen seed-42 production draw (\$91.0 billion, 34th percentile) and the seed mean (\$103.7 billion); the empirical benchmark of \$764.7 billion lies far outside the distribution's support, which is the visual form of the ABM's 10--17\% recovery band.}
\label{fig:mc}
\end{figure}

The simulation contrasts two institutional payoff rules. Under U.S. par-payoff, the cost to retire the mortgage is strictly equal to the outstanding principal, the negatively convex case, in which the investor's upside is capped while extension risk is bounded only by the scheduled-amortization profile: a 14.7-year weighted-average life at zero CPR against the no-shock 3.4-year life (Table~\ref{tab:wal}), an order-of-magnitude extension ceiling rather than an unbounded one. Under Danish market-value repurchase, the payoff cost is the present value of the remaining scheduled payments discounted at the current 30-year primary mortgage rate (FRED MORTGAGE30US), capped at par. Two qualifications attach to this proxy. First, it is an option-free present value discounted at the primary rate: it ignores the embedded par call and any option-adjusted spread, and the primary rate embeds the primary--secondary spread (guarantee fee, servicing, originator margin), whereas the bond-market discounting a true buyback would use sits below it; both choices overstate the borrower's buyback discount as rates rise. These biases inflate the mechanism-substitution Danish CPR of Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish} (the since-superseded 47.1\%) but are exactly immaterial to the Berger-recalibrated production finding: under neither transplant anchor does that leg's prepayment hazard consume this pricing proxy (the Danish-level anchor imports the near-flat moving curve; the production U.S.-intercept anchor evaluates the U.S. hazard at zero rate gap), and a replication-package diagnostic that re-runs the Danish leg with the discount rate shifted down by 25--150 bp moves trapped liquidity by \$0.00 billion, while flipping 2,470--92,495 of the 1,683,082 loan-month present-value classifications\footnote{This Danish-leg classification universe of 1{,}683{,}082 loan-months is 42 fewer than the 1{,}683{,}124 evaluated in the U.S.\ production hazard panel (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}): the two figures count active loan-months in the two counterfactual legs, whose prepayment paths differ marginally even under the shared imported elasticity,} that no downstream quantity in that leg consumes. Second, what the Danish rule neutralizes is extension risk for the bondholder \citep{berger2026}: the borrower retains the par call, so the rate-decline side keeps its price cap, and I use ``convexity-neutral'' below strictly in this rate-rise sense. Under the Danish rule, as market rates rise above 3.0\%, the bond's present value falls, allowing the borrower to extinguish the debt at a discount and guaranteeing continuous cash flow generation. The Danish counterfactual balance is simulated forward on a dynamic, declining-balance basis, so that each month's elevated Danish roll-off is applied to the already-shrunk balance carried over from the prior month and the simulated payoff path compounds correctly over the multi-year QT window.

\section{Macroeconomic Friction and Structural Extensions}\label{sec:method-frictions}

To quantify the behavioral friction gap in standard models, I scale base transaction friction using two FRED series: (1) search friction, measured as the shortfall in Active Listing Count (ACTLISCOUUS) against a 2017--2019 baseline, the last stable pre-pandemic period before the 2020/21 rate-driven frenzy and the 2022 inventory collapse; and (2) psychological friction, indexed from University of Michigan Consumer Sentiment (UMCSENT) against a baseline of 85.0, with readings below that line scored as incremental friction at +5 bp per point short, capped at +150 bp during acute sentiment stress. This produces a dynamic friction parameter (a one-time moving transaction cost expressed as a share of the household's current home value) that scales between 8.23\% and 9.96\% of home value over the 42 active QT months.

Three behavioral extensions were added to the household decision function, none with any parameter tuned to match the empirical benchmark, though their grounding is uneven (the first two are parameterized from external sources, the third an author calibration): a debt-to-income (DTI) constraint that vetoes any move in which the new housing payment would exceed 43\% of gross monthly income, a front-end heuristic motivated by the pre-2021 General QM debt-to-income limit (12 C.F.R. \S{} 1026.43(e)(2)(vi)); the regulatory threshold was a back-end total-debt ratio and was superseded by the price-based General QM definition in 2021, so I use it as a behavioral anchor rather than a live regulatory constraint, and the model carries no total-debt data against which to test the threshold (Section~\ref{sec:limitations}); asymmetric loss aversion that scales perceived payment increases by 2.25$\times$ while leaving decreases unscaled (loss aversion: \citealp{kahneman1979}; the 2.25 coefficient: \citealp{tversky1992}; \citepos{andersen2022} housing-market estimate of roughly 2.5 is close, and because any coefficient in this range triggers the same turnover-floor recalibration described below, the artifact interpretation of Section~\ref{sec:abm-results} does not hinge on the choice); and a wait-and-see freeze that holds 20\% of otherwise-mobile households in place whenever the trailing six-month change in mortgage rates exceeds 150 bp, modeled as a fixed per-agent trait so the CPR surface remains deterministic; the 20\% share and 150 bp trigger are author calibrations with no external source, and the same is true of the sentiment-to-friction mapping and the base transaction-friction level of the preceding paragraph (the UMCSENT and ACTLISCOUUS inputs are data; the $+5$ bp-per-point mapping, its $+150$ bp cap, and the base friction level are mine). An externally parameterized variant of the mobility-desire scale and the freeze share, drawn from the same source as Path B's elasticity, is tested in Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign}.

Because loss aversion steepens the penalty function facing every household, and the model's mobility-desire parameter is independently calibrated to reproduce a real-world baseline turnover floor of 4--5\% CPR at 8\% market rates (the turnover speed observed on deeply out-of-the-money discount cohorts during 2023--2024, when refinancing incentives were absent and remaining prepayment reflected death, divorce, and forced relocation), I hold that empirical floor fixed and re-run the calibration under the steeper penalty function, which requires nearly tripling the mobility-desire scale parameter. This mechanical recalibration is the principal driver of the results in Section~\ref{sec:abm-results}.

Whenever a specification change alters the penalty or covariate structure facing every household, the mobility-desire scale, and only that parameter, is re-derived so that the simulation continues to reproduce the empirically observed 4--5\% baseline turnover floor. The same rule generates the recalibrated variant of the cross-design test (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign}), whose frozen variant is defined by suspending it, and Appendix~\ref{sec:robustness-extensions} reports how much work this single choice does. Because the 4--5\% floor is imposed by construction, the ABM's agreement with observed discount-cohort turnover at deep rate gaps is an input, not a finding. The model earns no evidential credit on that dimension; its evidential exposure lies entirely on the dimensions the anchor leaves free, the dollar recovery and the timing of the CPR path. The anchor is also insulated from the behavioral gates by construction: the calibration point is evaluated at zero rate velocity, where the wait-and-see freeze cannot bind, so no freeze parameterization can move the floor (Appendix~\ref{sec:robustness-extensions} verifies the calibrated scale and floor CPR are bit-identical at every freeze share swept). What the anchor purchases, and what dropping it costs, is measured directly in Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign}: the externally parameterized variant that removes the floor overshoots the benchmark by half while predicting essentially zero turnover at QT-window rate gaps.

An income-scaled curtailment channel was added as an extension specified ex ante: a diagnostic correlation between the ABM's monthly CPR over-prediction and year-over-year growth in real disposable income (FRED series DSPIC96) of $r = -0.487$, concentrated in the worst over-prediction months (June--September 2022), led me to predict ex ante that curtailment, a strictly additive channel, would worsen the aggregate match. Curtailment was implemented as an income-scaled addition to simulated roll-off, mapping real income growth linearly from zero curtailment at $-2$\% growth to a full-scale annualized rate of 1.2\% CPR at +4\% growth, consistent with GSE daily prepayment report estimates.

Finally, the single representative 3.0\% coupon assumed elsewhere was replaced, as a diagnostic, with a multi-vintage composition: SOMA holdings bucketed into seven coupon cohorts yield a weighted-average coupon of approximately 2.55\% on the 30-year book, with the 2.0\% and 2.5\% buckets accounting for roughly 69\% of 30-year face value. Because a lower coupon implies a larger gap to prevailing market rates, my ex ante hypothesis was that this composition would increase predicted trapped liquidity toward the empirical benchmark. Fifteen-year MBS (approximately 9\% of SOMA face value) are excluded from this pass, and cohort origination dates are approximated from parsed maturity strings under a 360-month term assumption. Section~\ref{sec:abm-results} describes the production replacement of this diagnostic with a live, CUSIP-level pull of SOMA holdings.

\section{ABM Decision Rule and Parameter Provenance}\label{app:abmspec}
% Sources: abm/abm_lockin_simulation.py ("S:"), abm/fed_mbs_extension_risk.py ("F:"),
% abm/cross_design_test.py, abm/abm_external_gates.py. Line pins refer to the committed code.

This appendix specifies the ABM's move decision and every behavioral parameter at the level needed to reimplement the model without consulting the code; each equation and value is pinned to its source line in the replication package.

\paragraph{The move decision.}
Let $A(P,r,n) = P\,(r/12)\big/\big(1-(1+r/12)^{-n}\big)$ be the monthly payment on principal $P$ at annual rate $r$ over $n$ months. % S:130-136
Household $i$ carries gross income $y_i$ (\$/year), home value $H_i$ (\$), original principal $P_i = 0.8\,H_i$ (a fixed 80\% origination LTV in the synthetic population), note rate $c_i$, original term $n_i$ months, and outstanding balance $K_i$ (closed form at the cohort's seasoning; the U.S. par payoff). % S:141-155, S:314
The replacement-loan payment is $M^{\mathrm{new}}_{i,t} = A(K_i, m_t, n_i)$: the par payoff financed at the market rate $m_t$ over a fresh \emph{full original term}, not the remaining term (term-preference persistence). % S:222-225, S:366-381
With $\Delta_{i,t} = M^{\mathrm{new}}_{i,t} - A(P_i, c_i, n_i)$, whose second term is the current payment (by the amortization identity, equal to the payment on $K_i$ at $c_i$ over the remaining term), the loss-aversion-scaled penalty, in \$/month, is term-dependent:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:abmpenalty}
\pi_{i,t} =
\begin{cases}
\lambda\,[\Delta_{i,t}]_{+} + [\Delta_{i,t}]_{-} & n_i > 240 \text{ (30-year book)},\\
\lambda\,\big[M^{\mathrm{new}}_{i,t} - A(K_i, c_i, n_i)\big]_{+} & n_i \le 240 \text{ (native short-term gate)},
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
% S:383-409; the 240-month switch is S:408
with $\lambda = 2.25$, $[x]_{+} = \max(x,0)$, $[x]_{-} = \min(x,0)$: payment increases are scaled by $\lambda$; on the 30-year book, decreases pass through unscaled as gains, while the short-term gate floors them at zero to isolate the pure rate-lock cost. The frozen production headline draws 15-year voluntary CPR from the same-coupon 30-year surface, so the short-term branch is operative only in the Berger-run calibration stage (Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}).
The effective transaction rate is $\tau_{i,t} = \mathrm{clip}\big(\phi_t + (\tau_i - 0.07),\,0.02,\,0.12\big)$: the agent's idiosyncratic draw enters as an offset around the 7\% base mean and is re-clipped after the macro friction $\phi_t$ shifts it. % S:241-243, S:459-461
Household $i$ moves in month $t$ iff
\begin{equation}\label{eq:abmmove}
\underbrace{\frac{M^{\mathrm{new}}_{i,t}}{y_i/12} \le 0.43}_{\text{DTI veto}}
\;\wedge\;
\underbrace{D_i > S\,\pi_{i,t} + \tau_{i,t} H_i}_{\text{cost--benefit}}
\;\wedge\;
\underbrace{\neg\big(v_t > 0.015 \,\wedge\, u_i < 0.20\big)}_{\text{wait-and-see freeze}},
\end{equation}
% Gates: DTI S:455-457, cost-benefit S:458-463, freeze S:464-467, conjunction S:468
where $D_i \sim \mathrm{Exponential}(\theta)$ is the mobility-desire draw in dollars, $S = 60$ months is the expected-stay horizon converting the monthly penalty into a dollar stock, $v_t$ is the trailing six-month mortgage-rate change (decimal), and $u_i \sim U[0,1]$ is a fixed per-agent trait (the code's ``patience'' draw, consumed only by the freeze gate; it is not a time-preference parameter). % S:48, S:192, S:302-309
All inequalities are as coded: the DTI gate passes at exactly 43\%, the cost--benefit gate requires strict exceedance, and the freeze binds only strictly above 150 bp. The share of a pool passing \eqref{eq:abmmove} at an evaluation point is read as that point's annualized CPR (the calibration anchor is an annual floor) and consumed by the macro accounting layer at $\mathrm{CPR}/12$ per month. % F:957

\paragraph{Parameter provenance.}
Table~\ref{tab:abmparams} collects every behavioral parameter. None is tuned to the \$764.7 billion benchmark; the mobility-desire scale $\theta$ is the one parameter re-derived under the floor rule, and only ever that one.

\centering

\label{tab:abmparams}

\paragraph{Order of operations.}
Within a simulated month the three gates of \eqref{eq:abmmove} are evaluated on the surviving population and combined conjunctively; they are side-effect-free, so their order cannot change the result, and the code evaluates them in vectorized form in the order DTI, cost--benefit, freeze. % S:449-468
In cohort-path (burnout) mode, movers are removed permanently before the next month's evaluation, so the turnover tail sets a natural floor on the depleted pool. % S:470-518
Two named components are not in-month gates. The 4--5\% floor enters once, before simulation, through the desire-scale search of Table~\ref{tab:abmparams}, re-run, for that parameter only, whenever a specification change alters the penalty or covariate structure facing every household; because the anchor is evaluated at zero velocity, no freeze parameterization can move it (Appendix~\ref{sec:method-frictions}). % S:649-685, S:520-524
Curtailment never touches the household decision: it is an additive term in the macro layer's simulated roll-off. In production the decision rule is also not re-solved monthly: the CPR surface is precomputed on the rate $\times$ friction $\times$ velocity grid with all gates applied at each node and interpolated month-by-month, with the freeze's step-versus-ramp semantics disclosed in Appendix~\ref{sec:method-abm}. % F:873-895, S:526-548

The correction of this paper's earlier ``log-normal'' description of the mobility-desire distribution---it is exponential, on a dollar scale---is recorded in the run ledger (Appendix~\ref{app:ledger}).

\item Case and subscript separate the two paths throughout, and no coefficient symbol is reused across them. Path A's burnout coefficient $\beta_b$ (on $b_{s,t}$, estimated on the panel) and Path B's $\beta_B$ (on the cohort state $B_{s(i),t}$, a calibrated prior) are distinct objects carrying distinct symbols, as are Path A's friction coefficient $\beta_\phi$ (on the friction index $\phi_t$) and Path B's FICO coefficient $\beta_F$. The rate-gap elasticities $\beta_g$ (Path A, log-rate, standardized) and $\beta_1$ (Path B, log-hazard, per 100 bp) are on different scales; the paper compares them on sign, not magnitude (Section~\ref{sec:hazard-interp}).

Table~\ref{tab:params} collects every Path B parameter. All values are fixed a priori in the configuration; none is estimated on, or tuned to, the \$764.7 billion benchmark. One parameter is nonetheless measured inside the evaluation window, and benchmark-independence should not be read as window-independence: the 4\% baseline turnover floor is anchored to turnover observed on discount cohorts during 2023--2024 (Appendix~\ref{sec:method-frictions} and Section~\ref{sec:pathb}), so the floor (which sets much of the aggregate level, binding in 36\% of loan-months) is in-sample with respect to the QT window even though it never touches the benchmark dollar figure. Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor} sweeps the floor jointly with the elasticity band: the aggregate level and the lock-in marginal both move materially with the floor, within the bounded interval that section reports; the marginal's sign does not move, but under this window's rate configuration it could not (Section~\ref{sec:identification}). The covariate and default-hazard coefficients are round calibration priors. The two prepayment covariates' signs run opposite the canonical credit-risk direction (higher FICO $\Rightarrow$ better refinancing access $\Rightarrow$ faster prepayment; higher LTV $\Rightarrow$ less equity $\Rightarrow$ slower prepayment): in the deeply out-of-the-money 2022--2025 window the refinancing channel is shut and prepayment is turnover-dominated, so credit covariates proxy mobility rather than refinancing access; high-LTV loans are disproportionately recent purchases that churn. A Path-A-style estimation on the same stratum-month panel (Poisson PML with vintage-by-coupon fixed effects, so the credit buckets are identified from cross-bucket variation; bucket-mean covariates z-scored to the microsim pool's own moments) yields point estimates consistent with the priors' direction (and, taken at face value, roughly double their magnitudes), but with intervals that reject neither zero nor the opposite signs: $\hat{\beta}_F = -0.39$ (stratum-cluster bootstrap 95\% CI $[-1.51, +0.65]$) and $\hat{\beta}_L = +0.21$ ($[-0.59, +1.00]$). The estimation does not settle the sign question; it fails to contradict the priors while quantifying their sensitivity. The sensitivity brackets are correspondingly modest: a $\beta_F = \beta_L = 0$ rerun moves the recovery from 107.0\% to 108.3\%, and the panel-estimated pair moves it to 101.3\% --- in-window figures; at the off-window floor the same endpoints span 8.2 points and `modest' does not carry over to the level, while the marginal's covariate sensitivity shrinks to $+0.1$ points (run \texttt{covariate\_ablation\_offwindow}); benchmark-independence is unaffected, since the estimation touches only the Freddie panel. The $\beta_B = 0$ rerun of Section~\ref{sec:pathb} quantifies the burnout channel the same way.


Delinquent loans transition monthly through the matrix of Table~\ref{tab:delinq}, estimated from consecutive stratum-month \emph{modal} servicer states in the Freddie cohort panel, exposure-UPB weighted (committed as an artifact in the replication package). The division of labor with the hazard draws: the default-hazard draw moves loans \emph{into} the pipeline (Current $\to$ D30) and terminates already-delinquent loans it hits, while the matrix governs month-to-month movement \emph{within} the pipeline; the matrix runs after the draw each month, so a loan entering D30 via the draw can also take a matrix transition in its entry month, an overlap bounded along with everything else by the \$0.39 billion extreme-matrix test below. Only the delinquency rows are used in simulation, terminal states are absorbing by construction, and the forbearance state is unpopulated in the estimation panel and never entered in production runs.
I publish the full crosstab. D30 row (21 transitions, \$58.3M row exposure): 13 cures carrying 95.6\% of the exposure weight, two D30 continuations (0.8\%), five D60 progressions (3.1\%), and one Defaulted transition (0.4\%); the printed 0.004 is the exposure share of that single event, whose count share would be $1/21 = 0.048$. D60 row (9 transitions, \$3.1M): six cures carrying 56.8\% and three D90+ progressions carrying 43.2\%; the count shares are $6/9$ and $3/9$; the divergence between count and exposure shares means that a few larger-balance stratum-months drive the matrix, which is what an exposure-weighted estimator is designed to do. D90+ row (38 transitions, \$76.2M): seven cures (30.3\%), one D30 (0.8\%), thirty self-continuations (68.9\%), and zero Defaulted events; the ``--'' cells are observed zeros in a thin panel, not structural zeros, which is why serious delinquency shows no direct default exit while D30 shows one. Probabilities are printed to three decimals from these exposure shares and rows may not sum to exactly one. The matrix is accordingly reported for completeness, not precision: with cells this thin it describes the estimation panel rather than estimating a transition law, and the paper's estimates rely on the materiality bound below, not on the matrix's point values.

The thinness is immaterial for the paper's estimates: replacing the entire matrix with either extreme (all delinquent loans cure next month; all default next month) moves trapped liquidity by \$0.39 billion (0.05\% of the estimate). The 482 loans delinquent at the window start are routed by the matrix from the first month (mostly cured, given the cure-heavy rows); the month-end delinquency stock thereafter never exceeds 154 of the 40,234 active loans, and delinquent UPB never exceeds 1.1\% of the pool. Both extreme-matrix runs include the initial 482, so the bound covers them.

Sampling design: the draw is not a probability sample of the SOMA book, and the allocation rule is why. A pool built at four times the target size takes an equal allocation from each quarterly Freddie origination file; the pool is then drawn down to 75,000 loans proportionally within stratum, and every retained loan carries weight one (\texttt{hazard/loan\_sample.py}). Nothing in that chain reweights toward the book. Each origination year from 2017 through 2021 therefore takes 20.0\% of the draw by count, so 2017--19 is 60.0\% of it against 6.0\% of book face. That count basis is not the weighting the aggregation applies, which is the surviving balance at the window open, and on that basis the picture is materially different: 2017--19 is 27.0\%, 2020 30.7\% and 2021 42.3\%, against book face shares of 6.0\%, 16.3\% and 43.9\%. So the 2021 cohort is close to matched, and the 2017--19 overweight is a factor of about four and a half rather than the factor of ten the count basis suggests; origination balance, which compares originations to current face and is the wrong basis for this comparison, gives 55.5\%, 22.2\% and 22.3\%. What no weighting repairs is out-of-support mass. The draw spans 2017 through 2021 only, so pre-2017 (10.6\% of book face) and the 2022 cohort (23.1\%) carry no support at all: 33.7\% of face, which must be netted against the Ginnie share on the book's own joint cells rather than added to it. The draw's 3.86\% unweighted mean note rate also sits above the book's 2.49\% face-weighted pass-through coupon. Both tilts move the rate-gap distribution the floor takes its maximum against under the production floor form, and this paper signs neither: Section~\ref{sec:robustness-hybrid}'s coupon reweight rescales the aggregate level and leaves the marginal unchanged, so it does not test a composition effect running through the floor's bind share.

Attrition accounting: of the 75,000 sampled loans, 34,734 prepaid and 32 defaulted before the QT window opens, so 40,234 are active at the June 2022 start (39,752 current, 482 delinquent). The production U.S. run therefore evaluates 1,683,124 loan-months over the 42-month window, a mean of 40,074 active loans per month. The baseline turnover floor $\underline{h}$ binds in 36\% of those loan-months (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}).

The $\delta$-to-$\beta_1$ conversion of \eqref{eq:beta1} is insensitive to the turnover proxy behind it: in the small-$P_q$ limit the transform reduces to $\beta_1 = -\ln(1-\delta) = 0.0672$ at the central $\delta$, and over $P_q \in (0, 0.12]$ (from arbitrarily low turnover to double the adopted proxy) the central $\beta_1$ ranges only from $0.0672$ to $0.0701$, so even a large error in $P_q$ moves the conversion by a few percent.

\section{Path A Estimation Detail and Timing Diagnostics}\label{app:patha-detail}

This appendix collects the estimation detail of Path A (Section~\ref{sec:patha}), the direct input-timing diagnosis, the Danish discount-rate diagnostic, and the monthly-timing exhibits of Section~\ref{sec:identification}.

The second hazard path aggregates Freddie Mac loan-level performance data to the stratum-month level: strata $s$ are the 296 observed four-way cells of vintage $\times$ coupon $\times$ FICO bucket $\times$ LTV bucket, and the unit of observation is the stratum-month pair $(s,t)$. The bucket edges are the estimation configuration's: FICO below 680, 680--740, and 740 and above; LTV at or below versus above 80; coupons in 0.5-percentage-point steps; vintages 2017--2021. Geography is not a stratum axis, and no geographic term enters \eqref{eq:pathA}. The model is a discrete-time hazard with a log-exposure offset,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:pathA}
\log \mathbb{E}\!\left[y_{s,t} \mid x_{s,t}\right] = \log n_{s,t} + \alpha_{s} + f\!\left(a_{s,t}\right) + \beta_g\, g_{s,t} + \beta_b\, b_{s,t} + \beta_\phi\, \phi_t ,
\end{equation}
where $y_{s,t}$ is stratum $s$'s prepaid unpaid principal balance (UPB) in month $t$, in dollars, $n_{s,t}$ its UPB exposure, $x_{s,t}$ the right-hand-side covariates, $\alpha_{s}$ the stratum fixed effect (295 enter, one absorbed as reference), $f(\cdot)$ a loan-age seasoning spline in mean loan age $a_{s,t}$ (truncated quadratic power basis: a linear term plus $(a - k)_+^2$ at knots $k \in \{12, 24, 36, 60, 84, 120\}$ months, seven basis columns), $g_{s,t}$ the rate gap in 100 bp units, $b_{s,t}$ the stratum burnout state, and $\phi_t$ the dynamic friction index. The outcome is dollars, not a count: with the log-exposure offset the modeled object is the prepayment rate $y_{s,t}/n_{s,t}$, a nonnegative fraction, so the model is estimated by Poisson pseudo-maximum likelihood, whose score equations remain consistent for the conditional mean without any distributional assumption on $y_{s,t}$; no dispersion statistic is meaningful for this pseudo-likelihood. The 295 stratum fixed effects raise no incidental-parameters problem for the coefficients of interest: because the model is Poisson, concentrating the fixed effects out of the log-likelihood returns exactly the conditional-likelihood score for the slope vector, so the unconditional dummy-variable fit computed here yields the same slope estimates as the conditional estimator and stays consistent as the number of strata grows with the 36 training months held fixed, unlike the fixed-effect logit or probit, whose slope estimates are inconsistent at fixed panel length \citep{wooldridge1999}. That consistency requires only the multiplicative conditional mean $\mathbb{E}[y_{s,t}\mid x_{s,t},\alpha_{s}] = n_{s,t}\exp(\alpha_{s} + x_{s,t}^{\top}\beta)$ with no restriction on the distribution, dispersion, or serial dependence of $y_{s,t}$. All uncertainty statements below come from the two bootstrap schemes of Table~\ref{tab:bootstrap}: a stratum-level cluster bootstrap, robust to arbitrary within-stratum dependence and overdispersion but structurally unable to measure uncertainty in the friction coefficient ($\phi_t$ varies only over time, so resampling strata reproduces the identical friction series in every replication), and a temporal moving-block bootstrap that resamples the 36 training months in blocks of six with each month's full cross-section intact, which carries that burden (and, partially, burnout's). Table~\ref{tab:panel} reports the estimation panel's accounting; both it and Table~\ref{tab:bootstrap} are collected in Appendix~\ref{app:ridge}, because Path A is excluded from every headline range by pre-committed specification and neither table enters a headline quantity. The displayed form \eqref{eq:pathA} is the no-seasonal spec v3 design; the production specification (spec v4) adds eleven calendar-month dummies and is the design under which the 121.5\% recovery reported below is computed, though that level is not calendar-attributable (Section~\ref{sec:patha}), while every Path A inferential object stays on spec v3. Table~\ref{tab:hazard-notation} collects the notation shared by the two hazard paths.

The 295 stratum fixed effects absorb each cohort's baseline prepayment speed so that the burnout term captures within-pool adverse selection over time rather than cross-sectional differences in pool quality. The burnout regressor is a within-stratum demeaned measure of the surviving loan stock, orthogonalized against the loan-age spline so that it is not mechanically collinear with seasoning. This design is the estimation counterpart of \citepos{lesniewski2026} selection identity: the exposure-offset outcome is itself a pool-level, survival-weighted (here UPB-exposure-weighted) hazard, the 295 strata cut the cross-sectional dispersion that drives the identity's negative selection term down to within-cell variation, and the demeaned surviving-stock regressor proxies the residual composition drift that the time-invariant fixed effects cannot absorb. An earlier specification using four vintage-year dummies in place of the 295 stratum fixed effects returned a burnout coefficient of the wrong sign (+0.76); replacing the coarse vintage fixed effects with the full 295-stratum specification and demeaning burnout within stratum removes that wrong-signed artifact. The corrected estimate ($-0.13$) carries the theoretically expected sign (a more heavily burned-out pool should prepay more slowly, holding age and rate gap fixed), but the sign statement is a point-estimate statement only: the temporal bootstrap scheme of Table~\ref{tab:bootstrap} centers this coefficient on the opposite sign (mean $+1.23$, median $+0.52$), and under both schemes it is imprecise and not statistically distinguishable from zero, so I treat it as a control rather than a finding. Because 295 fixed effects make the model ill-conditioned under plain iteratively reweighted least squares (20 strata record no training prepayment event at all, so the likelihood is monotone in their fixed effects and the maximum-likelihood estimates diverge to $-\infty$), the fit carries a nominal ridge penalty $\lambda_{\mathrm{ridge}}$ on the full coefficient vector, with $\lambda_{\mathrm{ridge}}$ chosen over the grid $\{10^{-5}, 10^{-4}\}$ on a temporally blocked holdout comprising all stratum-months from January 2024 onward, so that no within-stratum information crosses the train/holdout split. The penalty is not what stabilizes the fit: at these magnitudes it is a numerical no-op (the two grid points return coefficient vectors identical to machine precision from the IRLS warm start, so the production coefficients are the IRLS solution and the grid selection is inconsequential), the 20 zero-event strata are held at the finite value the warm-started optimizer leaves them at (the penalty shrinks them only negligibly); and because the split is temporal over a fixed set of strata, that same fixed effect scores each stratum's post-January-2024 holdout months, so no fixed effect is estimated on, or transferred to, a stratum absent from the training window. The penalty's operative role is as the fallback for resampled refits in the bootstrap machinery. Nor is the production point an artifact of the device: dropping the 20 zero-event strata and fitting unpenalized Poisson PML reproduces the spec v3 macro coefficients to within $0.002$ in standardized units (rate gap $+0.6727$ against the spec v3 $+0.6734$; under spec v4 the same check holds to within $0.0022$, Appendix~\ref{app:ridge}). The verification details, including a reference-stratum sensitivity of penalized cold-start refits that motivates the warm-started bootstrap below, are recorded in Appendix~\ref{app:ridge}, now under both specifications: the battery has been recomputed under spec v4 and the device is unchanged (run \texttt{pathA\_ridge\_v4}). Two caveats still attach: the same holdout later serves as the out-of-sample diagnostic, so the RMSE reported below is at least weakly favored by selection, and months never touched by any selection step do not exist in this design, one reason Path A is held to a robustness role.
Estimated on this panel, the production specification (spec v4, the calendar-month seasonal design adopted at the freeze) yields a rate-gap coefficient of $+0.65$ (standardized; the training standard deviation of the rate gap is 221 bp, so the equivalent per-100 bp coefficient is $+0.29$), a burnout coefficient of $-0.17$, and a friction coefficient of $-0.007$, each with the theoretically expected sign. The friction coefficient's shrinkage relative to the prior spec ($-0.037$) is expected under the seasonal design, whose eleven month dummies absorb most within-year variation in the time-only friction index. Point estimates alone overstate what is identified here, so Table~\ref{tab:bootstrap} reports cluster-bootstrap uncertainty for all three (estimated under the prior spec v3; see the table note), including the bias diagnostics: the rate-gap point estimate sits near the 3rd percentile of its own bootstrap distribution (bootstrap mean $+1.49$, median $+0.76$, estimated bias $+0.82$), a signature of resampling-induced upward skew under which percentile and basic intervals disagree (the percentile interval excludes zero while the bias-reflecting basic interval does not). I therefore rest no claim on interval-based significance for any Path A coefficient. The defensible statements are about direction and imprecision: the rate-gap coefficient's point estimate is positive, and positive in 197 of 198 converged replications (99.5\%), but that share is a property of a right-skewed resampling distribution rather than a test, and the pre-committed bias-respecting test of $H_0\!: \beta_g \le 0$ does not reject under any construction (BCa $p = 0.093$ stratum / $0.412$ temporal; permutation $p = 0.241$; run \texttt{patha\_sign\_test}), so the coefficient is directionally consistent with the imported elasticity without being statistically distinguishable from zero; its magnitude is identified only up to a wide, construction-dependent interval, and burnout and friction are not distinguishable from zero on any construction. All three enter the production simulation as fitted inputs, none as findings, and no result in this paper depends on their point values. Path A's aggregate recovery now carries the interval that pass produces: resimulating the QT window under each of the 198 converged replications (the three macro coefficients swapped in per draw; intercept, age spline, and fixed effects held at the production fit) yields a median of \$922.6 billion and an interquartile range of \$900.1--\$1{,}130.6 billion, but a 95\% percentile interval of $[-\$4{,}459.8, +\$1{,}190.1]$ billion. A joint rerun that swaps in each replication's full coefficient vector --- intercept, age spline, and at spec v4 the month effects, drawn together with the macros --- returns $[+\$689.0, +\$1{,}217.6]$ billion, far narrower, and it is not adopted: 106 of its 198 draws, among them all 79 rogue-mode draws (drawn rate-gap coefficient nearer the reference-swap attractor $+1.96$ than the production $+0.67$), sit at exactly the zero-voluntary-prepayment floor of \$1{,}217.6 billion --- the trapped figure a pool returns when voluntary prepayment is switched off and only scheduled amortization rolls off, measured by a probe through the same coefficient handoff rather than by formula (run \texttt{pathA\_resimulate\_joint}). The committed interval remains the quoted one, because its $-\$4{,}459.8$ billion tail and the joint rerun's floor pile are the same rogue-mode replicates rendered without and with their compensating intercepts: one optimizer pathology of penalized refits on resampled panels, not two sampling statements. Measured at spec v4, the same joint rerun fails 1 of 200 replications against spec v3's 2 and its draws avoid the floor entirely (0 of 199), so the seasonal design's instability manifests as rogue-mode attraction (73 of 199 draws), not as fit failure. Fifteen replications with extreme burnout or friction draws (the two coefficients the bootstrap classifies as statistically zero) destabilize the 42-month forward compounding entirely, and 83.8\% of draws land above the benchmark. Those two facts pull in opposite directions, and the unfavorable one belongs in the text as much as the favorable one: the same 198 draws have a \emph{mean} of \$598.9 billion, or 78.3\% of the benchmark---below the mechanical $\beta_1 = 0$ null's 85.7\% and below Path~B's headline 91.3\%---against a median of 120.6\%, and on the share basis the percentile interval is $[-583.2\%, +155.6\%]$ with a resampling standard error of 201.9 points. I report the mean alongside the median for the same reason the coefficient table does: these distributions are severely right-skewed, so no single summary is sufficient, and quoting only the median and the fraction above the benchmark would flatter the estimator. Propagated coefficient uncertainty therefore leaves the forward simulation with no useful precision, an additional reason the aggregate dollar figure is held to the aggregate-level role assigned below and is not carried in the abstract. Every Path A inferential object (Table~\ref{tab:bootstrap}, this interval, and the train/holdout decomposition below) remains estimated under the prior spec v3, because bootstrap replications under the spec v4 seasonal design are branch-unstable (the resampled penalized likelihood has a second optimum that attracts replications away from the production branch); on the production panel itself 1 of 50 dispersed starts lands on the production branch and no start reaches a better penalized objective --- the objective evaluated at the frozen coefficient vector is the best value any of 150 starts attains, and every non-production terminus sits at least seven orders of magnitude above it, an optimizer stall of a strictly convex objective rather than a rival optimum (run \texttt{pathA\_cold\_starts}) --- and until the resampled instability is stabilized, the production specification carries no valid uncertainty of its own, and Path A is excluded from the paper's headline figures for this reason, retained as an aggregate-context, non-corroborating exhibit. Simulating forward (draining each cohort's balance deterministically each month by balance times hazard, with fractional prepayment settling directly to simulated SOMA roll-off and no settlement-lag convolution), this model recovers \$928.9 billion, or 121.5\%, of the \$764.7 billion empirical benchmark under the production calendar-month specification (spec v4), adopted at the pre-submission freeze under pre-committed parity gates that reproduce the committed seasonal robustness artifact exactly (the prior specification is preserved as a spec-v3 exhibit).

Three diagnostics temper this aggregate result because they bear directly on how much weight the \$928.9 billion figure can carry. First, the holdout fit (evaluated under spec v3; the frozen spec v4 fit, re-scored on the same January-2024-onward holdout without refitting, gives 37.9 unweighted and 3.0 exposure-weighted percentage points against spec v3's 37.8 and 2.5, so the seasonal terms do not improve the out-of-sample fit) is weak in both weightings. The units matter: the unweighted RMSE of approximately 38 percentage points of annualized stratum-month CPR is dominated by small-exposure strata with unstable realized rates, while the exposure-weighted RMSE (the weighting the fit itself uses) is 2.5 percentage points against an empirical mean CPR of 5.14\%, half the mean. Neither weighting supports out-of-sample predictive power at the stratum-month level (and the ridge grid's two candidate fits are numerically identical on this holdout, so no selection hinges on the weighting). A flexible-learner ceiling test bounds how much of this weakness is functional form: a gradient-boosted Poisson learner given the same covariate information on the identical pre-committed holdout improves the exposure-weighted RMSE materially, to 0.9 points against the frozen spec v4's 3.0, while leaving the unweighted RMSE essentially unmoved (37.6 against 37.9) and out-of-sample $R^2$ at zero ($+0.003$); a penalized Poisson GLM on the exact v4 design does no better than the production fit (2.6 and 38.1 points). Read against the run's pre-committed thresholds, flexibility buys recalibration of the high-exposure cells but no cross-cell predictive power in the 2024-onward regime. The landmarking-with-recalibration strand receives the same test: an exposure-weighted isotonic map fit on the 2023 inner-validation year only, applied to the frozen spec v4 holdout predictions, improves the exposure-weighted RMSE to 2.53 points against the frozen 3.04 (short of the pre-committed 0.80$\times$ threshold) while leaving the unweighted RMSE at 37.8 and out-of-sample $R^2$ at zero ($-0.007$); the pre-committed verdict is no material change: a monotone level recalibration fit in the training regime does not transfer predictive power across the 2024 boundary. Second, the recovery is concentrated where the model is unsupervised (decomposition measured under spec v3; it was not recomputed at adoption): of the spec v3 fit's \$915.0 billion, \$331.0 billion accrues in the 19 QT months overlapping the training window (June 2022--December 2023), against \$337.5 billion empirical (98\%), while \$584.1 billion accrues in the 23 post-training simulation months from January 2024 onward (21 of which have performance data; the empirical comparison uses those), against \$427.3 billion empirical (137\%). Components are independently rounded, and the overshoot lives almost entirely out of sample. Third, the raw CPR correlation against the empirical path is $r = -0.444$ at zero lag (more negative even than the ABM's $-0.318$), and \eqref{eq:pathA} omits calendar-month seasonality, one of the four factors of \citepos{richard1989} canonical prepayment function and a natural first suspect for a monthly path fit this poor. Re-estimating with eleven calendar-month dummies confirms strong, correctly signed seasonality (spring and early-fall log-effects up to $+0.43$ against a January base) and improves the simulated path's contemporaneous correlation from $-0.444$ to $-0.378$, with the peak moving from lag $-3$ to $-2$. That timing improvement is calendar-attributable, and I test it rather than assert it. Permuting the assignment of the twelve estimated month log-effects to calendar months preserves their multiset exactly --- and therefore every level channel they drive --- while destroying only their alignment with calendar time; scored on the conjunctive criterion the claim actually states (a lag-0 correlation better than $-0.378$ \emph{and} a peak at lag $-2$), scrambled assignments win 1 of 64 draws, a one-sided $p$ of 0.031, with an independent sixteen-draw family at a different seed also returning a single win (\texttt{pathA\_seasonal\_permutation}). The two components scored separately, 11 and 8 of 64, are diagnostics only: each necessarily exceeds the joint count, so reading either alone understates a conjunctive claim. The recovery figure is a different matter, and I no longer credit it to the calendar terms. Across those same 64 scrambles the recovery spans 121.14--122.29\%, a range of 1.15 points that contains the adopted 121.5\%, so the level is calendar-invariant: permutation holds the multiset of month effects fixed, and it is the multiset, not its alignment, that sets the level. Nor is the spec-v4 total decomposable as production plus seasonality in the first place --- the seasonal refit with all twelve month multipliers zeroed moves the recovery by $+\$80.27$ billion on its own, and restoring the multipliers claws back $-\$66.44$ billion of that, against a net reported seasonal effect of $+\$13.83$ billion. The timing improvement is real but far from sufficient: the named first suspect accounts for only a modest share of the negative path fit. A model that recovers 121.5\% of the dollar benchmark while mismatching the monthly path this badly may be recovering the aggregate partly through offsetting errors: over-predicting prepayment in some cohort-months and under-predicting in others in a way that nets out near the benchmark without the underlying mechanism being correctly timed. I therefore treat Path A's recovery as evidence about the aggregate level only. No timing claim rests on Path B either: its apparent levels advantage does not survive detrending (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}), and every claim this paper makes for Path B is a level-accuracy claim. Table~\ref{tab:pathadiag} collects Path A's diagnostics in one place.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.9\textwidth]{fig2_ccf}
\caption{Cross-correlation functions between each estimator's simulated CPR path and the empirical SOMA-derived path, lags $-6$ to $+6$. Under the implemented convention (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}), a peak at negative lag means the empirical series leads and the simulated path trails. Path B's $-3$/$-4$ peak and Path A's and the ABM's negative contemporaneous correlations summarize the same fact from Table~\ref{tab:estimators}: no estimator shows positive contemporaneous alignment, and once the window's shared trend is removed no estimator's co-movement is distinguishable from zero under the moving-block bands.}
\label{fig:ccf}
\end{figure}

A moving-block bootstrap (block length 6, 4,000 replications) puts the 95\% interval on the peak-lag correlation at $[-0.21, +0.49]$ and on the lag-0 correlation at $[-0.13, +0.44]$; with seven lags searched, individual band-point differences in peak $r$ carry no evidential weight. These intervals are not sensitive to the block-length choice in any verdict-relevant way: at block lengths 4 and 8 the peak-lag endpoints move to $[-0.27, +0.39]$ and $[-0.22, +0.51]$, and the lag-0 endpoints to $[-0.11, +0.45]$ and $[-0.17, +0.42]$, every interval still containing zero.

A Theil decomposition formalizes where each estimator's monthly error lives; it is a fit diagnostic, not a timing credential. On levels, the ABM's mean-squared error is 68\% bias share (the level failure already visible in its aggregate), while Path B's is 11.3\% bias and 82.5\% variance share on the corrected amortization basis (its residual monthly error is under-dispersion against a seasonal empirical path, not level bias; run \texttt{coupon\_convention\_amortization}), with Path A intermediate (26.5\% bias, 63.0\% covariance share); after linear detrending the variance share still dominates for Path B (93.7\%). On first differences, Theil's $U_2$ is 1.23 for the ABM, 1.01 for Path A, and 1.00 for Path B: no estimator beats a naive no-change forecast of month-over-month CPR changes, which restates the timing concession above in forecast-accuracy units. The full decomposition by transform, with cumulative dollar-error profiles whose terminal values reproduce Table~\ref{tab:estimators}'s aggregates by construction, is collected in Table~\ref{tab:theil}.

A direct input-timing diagnosis rules out the dating of the rate input and narrows, but does not isolate, the mechanism. In monthly \emph{changes}, the simulated CPR responds to the contemporaneous rate change with the correct sign and near-mechanical strength ($\Delta$-correlation $-0.94$ at lag 0), while the empirical CPR's month-to-month changes show no detectable rate response ($+0.25$, inside the $\pm 0.31$ zero band at $n = 41$): at monthly frequency the empirical path's variation is dominated by non-rate factors the simulation does not model, calendar seasonality above all. One construction asymmetry also belongs in this reading: the empirical CPR back-out retains the income-scaled curtailment component (Section~\ref{sec:method-benchmark}), while the simulated CPR paths exclude it (curtailment enters only as a common dollar flow in the accounting layer), so the two series being correlated differ by a small, slowly-varying component as well as by seasonality.

\section{Path A Estimation Panel, Coefficient Uncertainty, and Ridge Device}\label{app:ridge}

Three facts about the ridge device of Section~\ref{sec:patha} are verified in the replication package, under both specifications: the spec v3 records stand and each fact has been recomputed under spec v4 (run \texttt{pathA\_ridge\_v4}); the device is unchanged. First, at production magnitudes the penalty is a numerical no-op on the full panel: the two grid points ($\lambda_{\mathrm{ridge}} \in \{10^{-5}, 10^{-4}\}$) return coefficient vectors identical to machine precision from the IRLS warm start (maximum parameter difference $4.5\times10^{-17}$ in the replication artifact; $8.4\times10^{-17}$ under spec v4), so the grid selection is inconsequential and the production coefficients are the IRLS solution to machine precision; ``mildly shrunk'' would overstate the penalty's effect, which is nil. Second, ridge on the full coefficient vector with one stratum absorbed as reference is not invariant to the reference choice: in cold-start refits on resampled panels, swapping the reference stratum can move the standardized rate-gap coefficient by more than its own magnitude (an optimizer-path artifact of penalized refits with the 20 inestimable fixed effects), which is why the bootstrap machinery warm-starts every replication and discards non-converged draws; the warm-started spec v4 swap refit is reference-invariant to $7.3\times10^{-14}$, consistent with the warm-start policy that concession motivated rather than evidence the cold-start sensitivity is gone. Third, the production point itself is not an artifact of these choices: a refit that drops the 20 zero-event strata (423 of 10,176 cells) and fits unpenalized Poisson PML converges, is exactly invariant to the reference choice, and reproduces the spec v3 macro coefficients to within $0.002$ in standardized units (rate gap $+0.6727$ against the spec v3 $+0.6734$ and friction $-0.0373$ against $-0.0371$, third-decimal agreement; burnout $-0.1317$ against $-0.1301$, the larger burnout gap reflecting that dropping the 423 cells changes the burnout regressor's within-stratum demeaning and age-orthogonalization sample). The same refit at spec v4 --- the identical 20 strata and 423 cells drop out, since the month dummies do not change the zero-event set --- is again exactly reference-invariant ($1.1\times10^{-14}$) and reproduces the spec v4 macro coefficients to within $0.0022$: rate gap $+0.6463$ against $+0.6469$, friction $-0.00715$ against $-0.00711$, burnout $-0.1734$ against $-0.1713$, against a tolerance pre-committed at $0.005$, loosened from v3's $0.002$ because the month dummies absorb time variation the friction coefficient formerly carried (run \texttt{pathA\_ridge\_v4}).

Table~\ref{tab:panel} reports the estimation panel this fit is built on, and Table~\ref{tab:bootstrap} the coefficient uncertainty the two bootstrap schemes of Section~\ref{sec:patha} return. Both sit here rather than in the body because Path A is an aggregate-context, non-corroborating exhibit excluded from every headline range by pre-committed specification, and no cell of either table enters a headline quantity.

This scheme is structurally silent about the friction coefficient: the friction index varies only over time, so every replication sees the identical series, and the stratum-scheme friction row understates that coefficient's uncertainty by construction. Temporal scheme: the 36 training months are resampled in moving blocks of six with each month's cross-section kept intact, same refit and rescaling; it is the informative scheme for friction and, partially, burnout; its friction SE (2.80) is roughly $5.5\times$ the stratum scheme's (0.51). The ridge penalty is held at the production $\lambda_{\mathrm{ridge}} = 10^{-4}$ rather than re-selected per replication (re-selection would bootstrap a different, coefficient-plus-model-selection estimator). Replications returning non-finite or implausible ($|\beta| > 20$) coefficients are counted as failures, not draws; the point refit reproduces the production coefficients to four decimals on the same 10,176 training cells. Coefficients are in production standardized units (training SD of the rate gap: 221 bp). The stratum-scheme burnout median coincides with the point estimate at reported precision only (unrounded: median $-0.1297$, point $-0.1301$). Mean, median, and the implied bias (mean $-$ point) are reported because both schemes' rate-gap distributions are heavily right-skewed; under that skew the percentile and basic 95\% intervals disagree about zero, so interval-based significance is construction-dependent and the text relies on sign stability only; but the bootstrap sign-share is itself percentile logic, and the pre-committed bias-respecting test of $H_0\!: \beta_g \le 0$ (run \texttt{patha\_sign\_test}; BCa with the 296-refit jackknife acceleration this note previously deferred, plus a month-permutation leg) does not reject under any construction ($p = 0.093$/$0.412$/$0.241$), so the 99.5\% positive share is a description of a right-skewed resampling distribution, not a sign test, and no sign claim for this coefficient survives.
\end{tablenotes}

Grouped calibration localizes the same verdict (run \texttt{grouped\_calibration}). With the frozen Path A cell predictions binned into five exposure-weighted prediction-CPR quintiles (edges fit on the training window only) crossed with calendar half-years, the training halves calibrate at a pooled predicted-to-realized ratio of 1.000 (exact by construction of the exposure-offset fit), with per-half ratios spanning 0.80--1.17, while the post-2024 holdout drifts into monotonically worsening under-prediction: 0.64, 0.57, 0.44, 0.43 across 2024H1--2025H2, pooled predicted 2.24 against realized 4.21 points. The departure is temporal drift rather than a reliability-slope failure: pooled across periods the four upper prediction bins calibrate at ratios 0.94--1.11, and the shortfall concentrates in the lowest-prediction bin (43.5\% of exposure), where the 2024--25 cells accumulate: the finer-resolution form of the $U_2 > 1$ diagnosis above. This is a cell-panel CPR object, distinct from the cumulative dollar-runoff split of Section~\ref{sec:patha}, and like every Path A diagnostic it moves no headline figure.

\section{Composition Shift: Estimation Sample versus SOMA Book}\label{app:composition}

Table~\ref{tab:composition} reports a formal covariate-shift analysis between the Freddie Mac data behind the hazard paths and the SOMA book the benchmark spans, on the dimensions both sides carry (run \texttt{composition\_shift}). The book side is the New York Fed's CUSIP-level SOMA holdings parsed by the same production code that builds the ABM cohorts, anchor-gated to the committed marginals (book WAC 2.49\%, Ginnie share 20.4\%, term split, vintage-group shares); the API also served the historical June 29, 2022 as-of, whose composition is consistent with those anchors and is reported in the artifact as a non-gated secondary block. The shifts on coupon, vintage, and agency are large (the expected result, disclosed in prose throughout the paper), and each carries a committed, signed bound as its consequence, listed in the table's final column. The joint agency$\times$vintage cells settle the one netting question the marginals leave open: of the 2022 vintage's 23.1\% of book face, 20.7\% is conventional and 2.4\% is the Ginnie intersection, so vintage and agency marginals cannot be added without double-counting that cell. Geography, loan size, FICO, and LTV do not exist at the CUSIP level of the Fed's published holdings (par value and security description only); panel (b) supplies the deliverable substitute (the Freddie and Fannie loan-level samples agree to negligible shift on exactly those dimensions), and the hazard specification's exposure to the loan-level covariates is bracketed directly by the printed covariate-block endpoints (99.2\%/92.2\% shared-basis, Section~\ref{sec:pathb}).

All three sides use rounded half-point coupon buckets, but the two Freddie sides bucket borrower note rates while the Book column buckets security pass-through coupons --- a wedge of $+0.80$ points --- so the buckets are aligned in width, not in object. Within the draw the $\geq 4.0\%$ share moves with both conventions and they must be read separately: on count weights, rounding to half points raises it from 47.0\% to 58.2\%; holding the rounding fixed, the weighting lowers it from 58.2\% by count to 53.7\% on origination balance and 27.2\% on surviving balance. The WAC pair is mixed-basis, as Section~\ref{sec:pathb} records: the draw's 3.86\% is the unweighted mean of borrower note rates, the book's 2.49\% the face-weighted mean of security pass-through coupons. Every shift is signed in the same conservative direction: the out-of-sample segments prepaid faster, so trapped liquidity is if anything overstated (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}). That signing covers observed speeds in the segments outside the draw's support; it does not cover what the draw's composition within 2017--2021 does to the floor's bind share, which no committed run scores (Appendix~\ref{app:params}). Panel (b) uses the committed Fannie replication sample under its aggregates-only license posture. Artifact \texttt{composition\_shift\_results.json}.\par}
