\section{Floor-Sweep and Contamination Mechanics}\label{app:floormech}

This appendix collects the mechanics behind the calibration box of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}: the floor-level sweep, the marginal as a curve in the imported elasticity, the separation of the two refinance contaminations behind the off-window floor anchors, and the out-of-sample anchor construction. The three exhibits themselves are read where the headline is set, in Section~\ref{sec:hazard} (Tables~\ref{tab:floorband}, \ref{tab:lowband}, and~\ref{tab:oosfloor}).

Table~\ref{tab:floorband} reports the lock-in marginal at every cell of the resulting calibration box. Three facts shape the reading. First, the pre-committed stability threshold ($\pm 2$ points across the empirically plausible 3--5\% floor range) fails: at the central elasticity the marginal spans $+10.8$ to $+5.5$ points across that range. The mechanism is structural rather than statistical: the hazard specification takes a maximum against the floor, so wherever the floor binds the elasticity is inert, and raising the floor mechanically crowds out the lock-in channel. Second, both pre-committed monotonicities hold, the marginal decreasing in the floor within every band row and increasing in the band at every floor, and the box maximum lands at the predicted corner: $+13.2$ points ($+\$100.7$ billion) at a 2\% floor on the 7.7\% band edge. Third, every cell is positive, with a minimum of $+2.1$ points at a 6\% floor on the 5.5\% band edge---a consistency check on the specification, not an identifying result, since the window's rate configuration fixes the sign before any floor or band is chosen (Section~\ref{sec:identification})---and every marginal in the box is basis-invariant because the shared-accounting netting is common to the central and null legs of each cell (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-hybrid}). The verification reading of Section~\ref{sec:pathb} accordingly weakens from a point to a bounded interval: the lock-in elasticity's identified contribution is $+9.2$ points at the production calibration, $+5.0$ to $+12.6$ points across the empirically plausible floor range, and $+2.1$ to $+13.2$ points across the full box, with the off-window re-measurement below placing the headline reading at $+4.3$ to $+6.8$ points, while the aggregate level remains floor-dominated throughout.

The box sweeps the elasticity only over its published band, and the band's lower edge is a literature boundary rather than an evidential one: the in-sample estimates cannot reject zero for the elasticity (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}), so a reader who weighs that non-rejection over the imported band needs the marginal at values the box never visits. A pre-committed extension (run \texttt{band\_low\_extension}; grid, parity gates against the committed production and off-window cells, and a monotonicity check fixed in the script header before any run) re-runs the paired legs at quarterly-decline values $\delta \in \{0, 1.0, 2.0, 3.0, 3.25, 4.0, 5.0\}\%$ below the band at both anchor floors, and Table~\ref{tab:lowband} reports the resulting curve beside the band's own cells. The curve is monotone at both floors, passes through zero at $\delta = 0$ by construction, and is concave at the off-window floor through the censoring mechanism of Section~\ref{sec:identification}: raising the response crowds the hazard into the floor (the bind share climbs from 35.8\% to 73.5\% along that curve), so each increment of elasticity buys less marginal. At half the adopted central, $\delta = 3.25\%$, the marginal is $+5.1$ points ($+\$39.1$ billion) at the in-sample floor and $+3.5$ points ($+\$26.5$ billion) at the headline off-window floor. This curve prices the dependence on the imported elasticity rather than identifying it: how small the marginal could be, if the moving response is weaker than the literature band, is read off the curve, and the identified content remains the bounded range stated in Section~\ref{sec:identification}.


Those two checks vary the floor's level and its functional form; a third pre-committed check interrogates the level's provenance---whether the 4\% figure is an artifact of being measured in-window on the locked-in cohorts the marginal later decomposes (runs \texttt{out\_of\_window\_floor} and \texttt{oos\_identification}; spec, anchor definitions, acceptance thresholds, and parity gate fixed in each script header before its runs, the ex-ante convention Appendix~\ref{app:ledger} records). Run in-window on the deeply out-of-the-money 2023--24 discount cohorts, the identical exposure-weighted-CPR procedure reproduces the production floor at 3.8--3.9\% annual CPR, flat across seasoning cuts and validating the measurement where it is used. Applied out-of-window to pooled 2017--2019 discount-cohort turnover, before both the QT window and the 2020--21 refinancing wave, it recovers a materially higher 6.1\%. That pooled reading, however, does not survive its own diagnostics, and I do not take it as the operative off-window anchor.

Two contaminations are separable, and separating them is what changes the reading. The first is seasoning. A floor meant to capture strictly involuntary turnover---death, divorce, relocation---should be approximately flat in loan age, but the pooled 2017--2019 out-of-the-money turnover \emph{rises} monotonically with it: 3.68\% below twelve months, 5.78\% at twelve to twenty-four, and 10.99\% at twenty-four to thirty-six. The second is rate regime. Splitting the pooled window at the 2018/2019 turn separates a rising-rate leg (the 30-year rate moved 4.03\% to 4.64\% over 2018, suppressing refinancing incentive) from a falling-rate leg (4.46\% to 3.72\% over 2019, a refinancing wave). The legs read very differently: the 2018 leg returns 4.70--5.33\% across the out-of-the-money gap grid at the age-$\geq$-12 cut, while the 2019 leg returns 6.91\% and carries the entire 10.99\% age-$\geq$-24 cell. The pooled 6.1\% is therefore a refinancing-inflated composite, not a baseline turnover read, and the 2019 leg is disqualified on both diagnostics at once. The rate-regime half of that disqualification, as stated, compares the two legs at different discount depths; the matched-depth reconciliation below repairs the comparison and reports what survives it, which is less than the sentence just written implies.

What remains is the 2018 rising-rate leg, and its status must be stated precisely because the paper's off-window anchor now rests on it. Its mature-age seasoning test is \emph{not computable}: the leg contains no out-of-the-money cohort-months at all past the twenty-four-month age cut, because a loan that is out of the money while rates are rising is necessarily a recent, low-coupon origination and therefore young. The 2018 leg is thus clean by rate-regime construction rather than clean by test, and its one computable age contrast (3.78\% to 5.33\% across the twelve-month cut) is confounded by the PSA ramp, on which young loans genuinely turn over less, so it is not evidence about the contrast the leg is missing. The out-of-agency read is: in every cell where a mature contrast exists at all, mature turnover is the higher one, and Section~\ref{sec:identification} reports by how much. A second limitation concerns depth rather than age. The gap-$\leq$-two-point cell at which the in-window anchor is measured does exist off-window: the 2018 leg's gap runs to $-2.87$ points, and it holds 35 cohort-months reading 3.440\% annual CPR. Grids that stop at half a point do so because their thresholds were fixed at zero, a quarter point, and half a point in the script header, not because the rate history forbade anything deeper. The true limitation is exposure collapse, and a matched-depth run (\texttt{matched\_depth\_reconciliation}) measures it: down the 2018 leg's depth ladder the share of its gap-$\leq$-0 exposure that survives runs 100\%, 83.7\%, 72.9\%, 35.9\%, 11.3\%, 3.9\%, and 0.02\%, so the two-point cell carries \$0.282 billion of \$1{,}264.5 billion. That cell is present and worthless, and an ex-ante support rule---a depth is admissible for a leg only if the leg retains at least 10\% of its gap-$\leq$-0 exposure there---excludes it before any reading is taken. These are the reasons the off-window floor resolves to a \emph{range} rather than a point, and I report it as one.



\section{Behavioral Extension Mechanics}\label{sec:robustness-extensions}

This appendix reports the mechanism behind each of the three behavioral extensions summarized in Section~\ref{sec:abm-results}. As Appendix~\ref{sec:method-frictions} describes, loss aversion steepens the penalty every household faces, which on its own would suppress simulated mobility below the historically observed 4--5\% baseline turnover floor; to preserve that floor, I re-calibrate the mobility-desire parameter upward, nearly tripling it. That recalibration mechanically inflates the model's overall responsiveness to rate gaps, so the jump to 54.9\% is an artifact of holding the turnover floor fixed, not a validation of loss aversion as a mechanism. Because that floor is anchored at maximum lock-in, the sign of the recalibration's effect on rate-sensitivity is not fixed: a specification change that eases turnover at the anchor lowers the recalibrated mobility scale (in the real-covariate cross-design of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign} it falls to 36,086 from the synthetic 43,883) and deflates rather than inflates rate-sensitivity, so the headline recovery, itself resting on a floor-anchored calibration, inherits that indeterminacy, which is why the ABM's negative verdict is read only alongside the cross-design hedge and never on its own. The second extension, income-scaled curtailment, pulls the estimate down to 45.1\%; because curtailment is a strictly additive drag on prepayment, this decline is what I predicted ex ante from a diagnostic correlation between the ABM's over-prediction and disposable-income growth (Appendix~\ref{sec:method-frictions}). The third extension, multi-vintage coupon composition, pulls the estimate down further, to 33.7\%. A lower weighted-average coupon widens the gap to prevailing market rates, and my hypothesis was that this should increase predicted trapped liquidity, not reduce it. The inversion suggests the ABM's CPR mechanism is not translating a wider coupon gap into slower prepayment the way the household-choice framework predicts it should. I have not diagnosed this inversion, and its implication cuts in both directions: an aggregate response to the rate gap running backward is equally consistent with ``household choice explains little'' and with ``this specific ABM's gap response is mis-specified,'' which is why the ABM's recovery share is never quoted in this paper without the cross-design qualifier of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign}, why the paradigm attribution inherits that section's hedge, and why the two-estimator contrast, not the ABM's point estimate, is what the paper asks the reader to carry. I cannot rule out that a differently specified household model would perform better, but that possibility does not bear on the results reported in Section~\ref{sec:abm}.

The wait-and-see freeze now carries a direct sensitivity run, scored on the frozen Danish-anchor production accounting (trapped \$84.5 billion at the production 150 bp trigger and 20\% share; parity gate $+\$0.02$ billion). Removing the freeze entirely drops trapped liquidity to \$44.6 billion (an isolated contribution of $+\$39.9$ billion, $+5.2$ points of benchmark), and moving the six-month trigger to 100 or 200 bp spans \$105.9 to \$70.5 billion, so the trigger's placement matters at the few-points scale and its author-calibrated status remains a disclosed limitation. Sweeping the freeze share with the floor anchor retained gives \$65.1 billion at a 10\% share, \$81.5 billion at the externally implied 18.3\%, and \$103.3 billion at 30\%: re-anchoring the share to the Liebersohn--Rothstein-implied value moves recovery by only $-0.4$ points of benchmark, and the calibrated mobility scale and floor CPR are bit-identical at every share, confirming that the freeze cannot compromise the baseline turnover floor. The front-end DTI gate receives the parallel treatment: moving the 43\% wall to 36\% or 50\% on the same frozen surface moves ABM trapped liquidity to \$153.3 and \$119.7 billion (from \$84.5 billion at the production 43\%), with the turnover floor re-anchored into the empirical 4--5\% band at every threshold, so the gate is material to the ABM's own estimate while never breaching the floor. That sweep is non-monotone in the wall, and the production 43\% sits at its minimum, which invites the reading that the paper's own parameter choice is the one minimizing its own estimate. It does not survive decomposition (run \texttt{dti\_nonmonotonicity\_decomposition}). The floor-retention rule re-calibrates the mobility-desire scale only where the fixed-scale floor would leave the 4--5\% band, and across these three thresholds that happens exactly once: at 50\%, where loosening the wall lifts the fixed-scale floor to 5.09\% and the search cuts the scale from 43{,}883 to 41{,}934. Holding the scale at its production value instead, the sweep is monotone---\$153.4, \$84.6 and \$46.4 billion at 36, 43 and 50\%---and the 50\% leg's entire rebound is the re-calibration, worth $-\$73.4$ billion. The frozen and re-calibrated legs are bit-identical at 36 and 43\%, where the rule is a no-op, which is what identifies the channel. The wall is therefore monotone in the direction the mechanism implies, and the published non-monotonicity is the free parameter absorbing the floor constraint rather than a property of the DTI gate. Nothing here moves a headline: the ABM is a stress-test device and the hazard marginal carries no DTI gate.

\section{Duration Quantification: Approximate Portfolio Weighted-Average Life}\label{sec:robustness-wal}

The mechanism the paper quantifies is duration extension, so I express it in maturity units. Table~\ref{tab:wal}, printed with the cap-design arithmetic in Section~\ref{sec:discussion-capdesign}, reports the SOMA book's approximate weighted-average life (WAL) under scheduled amortization only, under the empirical CPR path, and under each estimator's implied path, at the QT window's start and end; this appendix documents its construction and reads it against each estimator.

Read against the empirical column, each estimator's CPR error maps into effective-maturity error: the ABM's over-predicted prepayment implies a book roughly 3.4 years too short at window start, Path A's under-prediction a book 1.5 years too long, and Path B lands within 0.3 years. Relative to the no-shock world of the table's final row (prepayments continuing at the 22.81\% mean 2021 realized CPR, implying a 3.4-year WAL) the empirical 9.4-year WAL is a 6.0-year extension: that duration gap is what the paper's dollar benchmark prices month by month. The two Danish rows read the counterfactual in the same maturity units: feeding the Danish legs' mean CPRs through the identical calculator gives a rule-only WAL of 9.1 years at the window's start against the U.S. leg's 9.7 (a 0.6-year rule-only shortening, with the Danish-level bracketing case at 10.8), and the rule-only leg's extension is additionally rate-invariant on the rate-rise side by the identified moving-flatness (Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}). I deliberately price extension rather than convexity: option-adjusted duration and convexity require a term-structure and prepayment-option model outside this paper's scope, and ``convexity-neutral'' is used strictly in this rate-rise sense.

\section{Fifteen-Year MBS Fold-In and Path B Rate-Gap Units}\label{sec:robustness-pipeline}

Two features of the production pipeline set headline figures cited throughout this paper: the treatment of fifteen-year MBS, and the units in which Path B applies its rate-gap coefficient. Appendix~\ref{app:ledger} catalogues the figures each supersedes.

The first is the fifteen-year MBS fold-in. The SOMA cohort parser buckets 15-year holdings separately by coupon, never merging 15-year and 30-year buckets, and 15-year scheduled amortization enters both the empirical CPR back-out and the ABM's simulated roll-off. Restricting to 30-year MBS covers 90.6\% of face value, leaving the 15-year book (approximately 9.1\% of holdings) to contribute to total portfolio size but not to cohort-correct amortization or CPR weighting; folding it in brings coverage to 99.8\% of SOMA face value at a SOMA weighted-average coupon of 2.49\% (Table~\ref{tab:wal}'s note reconciles these coverage components with the audit parse's 90.7\%/9.1\% term split).

I tested, but did not adopt, a fully native treatment in which 15-year loans also draw voluntary CPR from a 15-year-specific surface. This produced out-of-bounds predicted values: the ABM's payment-delta gate (the change in \eqref{eq:annuity}'s monthly payment upon refinancing at the prevailing rate, which drives loss-aversion and mobility-desire responses elsewhere in this paper) breaks down for seasoned 15-year borrowers, whose same-term replacement payment is nearly flat relative to their current payment. Native 15-year surfaces predicted 32\%--61\% CPR against an empirical range of roughly 5\%--8\%, enough to flip the ABM's aggregate trapped-liquidity estimate to a large negative value, far outside the empirically observed range. Production therefore uses a structural-only fold-in: 15-year cohorts contribute real weights and real scheduled amortization, while voluntary CPR for those cohorts is drawn, in the frozen headline specification, from the same-coupon 30-year surface. This is a scope limitation for the production headline rather than for the framework: a 15-year-specific behavioral gate (a pure rate-lock penalty for short-amortization loans) is implemented and is the basis of the Berger run's calibration-stage freeze (Table~\ref{tab:danish}, note b); its adoption for the production headline is deferred to the pre-submission freeze.

The second is the units in which Path B applies its rate-gap coefficient $\beta_1$, calibrated from \citet{liebersohn2024} via the survival-function transform described in Section~\ref{sec:pathb} and expressed as a log-hazard-ratio per 100 bp increase in the rate gap. Applied to the gap in decimal rather than per-100 bp units the coefficient is numerically inert, yielding a multiplier of approximately $\times$1.0026 at a representative QT-era state (a 3.0\% coupon against a 6.8\% market rate) against the intended suppression of approximately $\times$0.77. Production applies it as $\exp(100\,\beta_1\,\tilde g_{i,t})$, with $\tilde g_{i,t}$ the decimal form of \eqref{eq:pathB}'s rate gap (equivalently $\exp(\beta_1 g_{i,t})$ in \eqref{eq:pathB}'s 100 bp units; the gap is negative when the borrower is locked in, so \eqref{eq:beta1}'s positive $\beta_1$ suppresses prepayment), consistent with Path A's convention of a positive coefficient applied to the gap in comparable units, and expresses the Danish counterfactual's rate-gap function in rate-equivalent units so that the buyback discount properly offsets the locked-in spread (live in the mechanism-substitution variant; the production Danish leg's prepay response, under either Berger-recalibrated anchor of Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}, does not consume that function's classification, which is nonetheless computed, as Section~\ref{sec:pathb} discloses). Section~\ref{sec:pathb} reports the results under this convention; the superseded \$747 billion (97.7\%) microsimulation is catalogued in Appendix~\ref{app:ledger}.

Both are structural or mechanical rather than changes in modeling philosophy, and both are additive: disabling either (30-year-only filtering; the decimal-unit $\beta_1$ convention) exactly reproduces the corresponding superseded figures catalogued in Appendix~\ref{app:ledger}, which I treat as evidence that each is isolated to its intended code paths.

\section{Seasonalizing the Turnover Floor: Floor Dispersion, and Why No Timing Claim Follows}\label{sec:robustness-seasonalfloor}

The three checks above vary the floor's level, its functional form, and its provenance, holding it constant within the window in each case. Two runs in this paper let the floor vary within the window instead: the cyclical variant of Section~\ref{sec:pathb}, which indexes it to the rate path, and the pre-committed seasonal check taken up here, which indexes it to the calendar. Both move the marginal, and this check is the one built to isolate why. It was designed to answer two questions at once: whether letting the turnover floor vary by calendar month moves the lock-in marginal, and whether the resulting monthly path tracks the empirical benchmark well enough to license a timing claim (run \texttt{seasonal\_floor\_timing}; spec, leg definitions, the placebo families, and a frozen timing rule fixed in the script header before any run executed). The answers are asymmetric, and the second is the more consequential: the marginal does move, but not for the reason the design was built to test, and the timing rule is met without being informative.

The calendar profile is a twelve-cell exposure-weighted annualized turnover CPR measured on deeply out-of-the-money cohort-months. It is estimated on the full cohort-month panel (7{,}756 cohort-months, \$74.2 trillion of exposure, pooled annual CPR 4.187\%), not on the QT window; the window-restricted variant differs in four calendar cells and drives nothing here, and I state the basis because an earlier module docstring described the profile as in-window and was wrong. The profile peaks in June with a peak-to-trough ratio of 2.13. Four legs are run, each as a paired central and $\beta_1 = 0$ null: the production constant 4.0\% floor, a constant 4.5\% floor, a \emph{shape-only} leg carrying the calendar profile with its exposure-weighted mean pinned at 4.0\%, and the pre-committed 4.5\%-mean seasonal leg. The shape-only leg is the one that isolates the calendar, because the pre-committed leg confounds shape with the level move the committed sweep already prices. Every leg re-runs the production microsimulation with only \texttt{INVOLUNTARY\_CPR\_ANNUAL} varied by calendar month; the constant-4.0\% control reproduces the committed production artifacts bitwise (central \$818.530 billion, null \$748.185 billion, marginal $+\$70.345$ billion, $+9.20$ points).

Giving the floor a calendar profile at a held-constant 4.0\% exposure-weighted mean moves the marginal from $+\$70.345$ billion to $+\$65.448$ billion, $+9.20$ to $+8.56$ points: a shape effect of $-\$4.897$ billion. A permutation family disposes of the natural reading of that number. Assigning the \emph{same} twelve floor values to scrambled calendar months, so that the multiset of values is preserved exactly and only the month labels change, retains on average 89.2\% of the shape effect across fourteen draws (69.9\% to 102.8\%), with permuted marginals of \$65.311 to \$66.924 billion against the true \$65.448 billion. Calendar order is very nearly irrelevant to the movement, so the movement is not a calendar effect.

What it is instead is a Jensen effect operating through the floor's combination rule. Production combines the floor and the voluntary hazard by a hard maximum (\texttt{FLOOR\_MODE = 'max'}, the semantics \eqref{eq:pathB} and the form test above discuss), and at the production floor the floor binds in 36.3\% of evaluated loan-months. Under a maximum, dispersion in the floor is one-sided: in months where the voluntary hazard already exceeds the floor, the low floor cells are truncated away and contribute nothing, while the high cells bind and lift the effective hazard. Any dispersion in the floor therefore raises book-wide prepayment regardless of which month carries which value, which is exactly what the permutation family measures. A convexity-matched control separates the small remaining piece: because the annual-CPR-to-monthly-hazard transform is itself convex, the twelve-cell profile is equivalent in level to a flat 4.0090\% floor rather than a flat 4.0000\% one, and running that flat leg gives $+\$70.156$ billion, a $-\$0.189$ billion move. Level leakage is thus 3.9\% of the shape effect and floor dispersion under the maximum rule is the remaining 96.1\%. I therefore report this as a floor-dispersion and functional-form result. It is not evidence that seasonality moves the marginal.

Level and shape do not separate cleanly, and I do not present an additive split as though they did. Measured against the constant-4.0\% control, the level move contributes $-\$12.704$ billion ($-1.66$ points), the shape move $-\$4.897$ billion ($-0.64$ points), and the two together $-\$16.040$ billion ($-2.10$ points), leaving an interaction residual of $+\$1.560$ billion ($+0.20$ points). On the simulated monthly paths the residual is larger relative to what it qualifies: mean absolute interaction residual 0.164 points of monthly CPR against mean absolute total movement 0.533 points, so the interaction is 30.9\% of the total movement. Any level-plus-shape decomposition of this floor must carry that residual; quoting either component alone misstates both.

What the experiment does buy is a narrow bound. Holding the turnover floor flat within the window is a modeling convenience, and this run bounds how much of the constant-floor marginal that convenience was responsible for: at most $\$4.897$ billion of the $+\$70.345$ billion in-sample marginal, 7.0\% of it, which is 11.5\% of the $+\$42.6$ billion headline marginal the same absolute bound is measured against, and 96.1\% of even that is the maximum rule's response to dispersion rather than anything about calendars. The identified lock-in marginal is not an artifact of a flat floor. That is the whole of what the seasonal design establishes, and it establishes it about levels only.

\paragraph{The timing concession.} The design's second purpose was to test whether a seasonalized floor earns a monthly-timing claim, under a rule frozen before the runs: Theil $U_2$ on first differences below one \emph{and} a positive detrended lag-0 correlation against the empirical benchmark. That rule is met as written by both seasonal legs (shape-only $U_2 = 0.995$, $r = +0.016$; pre-committed leg $U_2 = 0.990$, $r = +0.070$), and I report it as met, because it was frozen ex ante and I will not reopen a rule after seeing which way it fell. I nonetheless claim nothing from the pass, because five diagnostics computed afterwards show the rule cannot discriminate. First, the benchmark carries it. The SOMA back-out has four exact-zero months (June 2022, February 2023, April 2024, September 2025), three of them immediately followed by a spike of up to 14.01\% annualized. The zeros are now traced to their mechanism: each is clip-induced, a month whose reported rolloff falls below the scheduled-amortization floor so that the back-out's non-negativity clip pins it at exactly zero, with the displaced mass posting into the following month's reading (unclipped values $-1.59$/$-0.49$/$-0.84$/$-0.84$ points; run \texttt{h1\_zero\_months\_diagnosis}) --- a month-boundary allocation artifact of the reporting series, not a behavioral observation. The June 2022 zero opens the window, is followed by an ordinary month, and so contributes one first difference where the others contribute two. Those pairs touch 7 of 41 first differences in total but carry 0.813 of the empirical differenced signal and 0.796 to 0.811 of every leg's $U_2$ numerator. Repairing them by reallocating each spike evenly across its pair, conserving the pair total, flips both seasonal legs from pass to concede and turns the shape-only leg's detrended correlation from $+0.016$ to $-0.134$. Second, a calendar-rotation placebo moves the true profile to the wrong months while holding its amplitude and dispersion exactly; the rule is cleared by 1 of 11 wrong-month rotations for each seasonal leg, and the five-month rotation \emph{beats} the true profile on $U_2$ (0.9934 against 0.9947). Third, 3 of the 14 scrambled calendar orders above clear the rule, and the best of them attains $U_2 = 0.975$, better than either real seasonal leg. Fourth, and decisively, the $\beta_1 = 0$ null clears the rule on both constant-floor legs, the 4.0\% null doing so with $U_2 = 0.9997$ and a detrended correlation of $+0.115$, stronger than either seasonal central leg's. A criterion satisfied by a specification with the lock-in elasticity removed cannot be evidence for the lock-in mechanism. Fifth, the correlations are not distinguishable from zero on 42 months in any case ($p = 0.921$ and $p = 0.661$), and the leg that isolates the mechanism is the fragile one: the shape-only leg's verdict flips on 5 of 42 single-month deletions while the pre-committed leg's flips on none.

Two further diagnostics point the same way without being needed for the verdict. The seasonal legs concentrate 79.7\% (shape-only) and 89.4\% (pre-committed) of their simulated detrended variance in the twelve-month calendar harmonic, while the empirical benchmark carries 0.8\% of its own detrended variance there: the model acquires calendar structure the benchmark can neither confirm nor refute. And on the committed cross-correlation metric the seasonal legs peak at lag $+6$ with a negative correlation, on the boundary of a 42-month window, which I mark non-interpretable rather than read.

The right reading of this is a finding about the criterion, not a failure of the experiment. The frozen rule was a reasonable ex-ante test and it turns out to have no power against the alternatives that matter, because a lumpy benchmark, wrong-month profiles, scrambled calendars, and an elasticity-free null all satisfy it. I therefore restrict every claim in this paper to levels: no estimator here earns a monthly-timing claim, which extends to the seasonalized floor the levels-only concession the production microsimulation's monthly path already carries, where the raw lag-0 correlation does not survive detrending (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}). This is a restriction on what the design identifies, not a retraction of any level result, and no aggregate, marginal, or bound reported elsewhere in this paper depends on timing.

\paragraph{Carried caveats.} Four qualifications travel with the seasonal floor wherever it is quoted, and none of them is repaired by the runs above. First, the profile's identification is circular in the same way the in-window floor's is: the deep-out-of-the-money filter that defines it captures 98.2\% of QT exposure, so it is close to the identity map on the panel and the profile is not measured off a lock-in-free population. Second, the clean off-window leg that rescues the floor's \emph{level} cannot rescue its \emph{shape}: the 2017--2019 out-of-the-money cells number 335 cohort-months on \$86.2 billion of exposure and record zero prepaid balance in February and April, so no twelve-cell profile is estimable there at all. Third, the seasonal share is not stable across windows, the calendar terms explaining $R^2 = 0.011$ of the full-panel variation against 0.755 in the QT window, and the profile inherits that instability. Fourth, this floor profile peaks in June while the committed \emph{voluntary} seasonality of the estimated cohort-hazard form peaks in March and October. They are different objects and need not agree, but the tension is real and I state it rather than let the two profiles sit unreconciled in the same paper.

\section{Pre-Committed Rules and Their Post-Run Adjudications}\label{app:verdicts}

This paper's checks are pre-committed, but pre-commitment fixes what will be measured, not what a measurement means once it exists: adjudicating an outcome against its rule involves post-run judgment, and that judgment has run in both directions in this paper---failures have been diagnosed rather than merely reported, and passes have been weakened rather than banked. A green gate suite is therefore not self-certifying, and this table exists so the judgment itself can be audited. Its domain is narrower still, and worth stating plainly, because a pass count invites a confidence it cannot support. The suite checks three things: that every printed literal matches the committed artifact it is drawn from, that artifacts agree with one another where they overlap, and that named spans are present and, where order carries meaning, ordered. It checks no premise. Nothing in it establishes that a run's specification was the right one, that an imported elasticity transports to this population, or that any identification claim holds; those are argued in the body, and they are what a referee should read the body for. Nor did it see the page: a suite of 108 green gates once coexisted with roughly 919 text items set off the physical sheet across nine pages---among them a full page of this appendix's own ledger and a row of Table~\ref{tab:headline}---which is why a separate check now runs against the built PDFs rather than the source. I report that here because it is the sharpest evidence I have for the claim this appendix rests on: these checks constrain what the paper may say about its own runs, and nothing more. Its inclusion rule: every pre-committed interpretive rule or threshold whose post-run treatment was anything other than mechanical enforcement, in either direction, plus the enforced rules that bound a headline quantity. Each row states the rule as committed, the outcome, and the adjudication with its direction relative to the paper's headline claims.
